7.3. WORK ENVIRONMENT
The “Work Environment” is defined as any location where one or more Workers (i.e., Employees or
Reportable Contractors) are either (a) working or (b) present as a condition of employment. As such,
the Work Environment includes:
 Company Premises (Section 7.3.1)
 Off Company Premise locations at which work-related activity is conducted (Section 7.3.2)
 Locations where a Worker is present as a condition of employment (Section 7.3.3)
 Locations and transportations associated with work-related travel (Section 7.4).
If none of the Work Relationship Exceptions listed in Section 7.2.1 apply and the event or exposure in
question occurred in what might be considered to be the Work Environment, the event shall be Work-
Related. For injuries and illnesses that occur off Company Premises, certain exceptions may apply and
thus the work relationship must be established.
Incidents occurring in the “Work Environment” are considered work-related, and such incidents must
be recorded, unless demonstrated otherwise based on investigation and subject to the Work
Relatedness Exceptions in Section 7.2.1. Incident shall follow the full cycle of Notification, Recording,
Investigation and Reporting. Reporting in HSE Performance shall accordingly be evaluated based on
the investigation findings (establishing work-relatedness).
7.3.1. Company Premises
Incidents including Injuries and illnesses that result from events or exposures occurring on “Company
Premises” are considered work related unless demonstrated otherwise based on detailed investigation
and subject to the Work Relatedness Exceptions in Section 7.2.1. Refer to Appendix 6 for examples of
Incidents Occurring at Company Premises.
“Company Premises” for ADNOC reporting purposes are defined as locations and property owned (or
leased from others or accessed through rights secured by the Company), operated, and/or controlled
by the Company.
The determination of whether a particular location constitutes part of the employer’s premises depends
upon whether the location is considered part of the "employer’s domain", not whether he owns or leases
it. As a general rule, if the site is part of an establishment of the employer, it is considered part of his
premises. An establishment is a single physical location where business is conducted or where
services or industrial operations are performed.
Exceptions: Injuries or illnesses which occur to employees on company’s parking lots or company’s
controlled recreational facilities are not presumed to be work related, and are not recordable unless
the employee was engaged in some work related activity or present as a condition of employment
Examples of “Company Premises” include:
 ADNOC Group controlled offices, plants, equipment, process facilities, offshore platforms, well
sites, storage areas, parking lots, access roads, right-of-ways, terminals. This includes Offices
located in buildings with multiple occupancies (e.g. Offshore Offices in a Public Building) limited to
space occupied and controlled by ADNOC Group.
 ADNOC Group-owned and operated retail stores and Group owned dealer or agency-operated
retail sites
 Site or facility established by a Contractor to provide goods or services exclusively for the Company
and at which the Company maintains operational control and oversight (Contractual Mode 2).
Example: An off-site engineering work-space, laydown yard, or fabrication yard/shop exclusively
serving the Company and over which the Company maintains operational control (see Section
7.8.9 on Contractors Reportability).
Standard No.: HSE-GA-ST04 Version No.: 1 Effective date: Dec., 2019 Page 35 of 184

ADNOC Classification: Internal
HSE Management System
HSE Governance & Administration Standards
Incident Notification, Investigation & Reporting
 The front steps of company buildings and company sidewalks are considered company premises,
but the front steps of a public building in which the employer leases space are not considered to
be the company’s premises.
 Company operated marine vessels either owned or bareboat chartered.
 Vessels engaged in ADNOC Group’s offshore operations whether owned, chartered or contracted
whilst within the “Oil Field Limits”. Oil field limits are the limits defined by ADNOC and delineated on
necessary charts, Vessels require field entry permit before entering these limits. Petroleum Port
Limits are defined in Petroleum Port regulations available at PPA website (https://ppa.adnoc.ae/en).
Examples of situations that are not considered “Company Premises”:
 Contractor facility over which the Company does not maintain operational control, provide work
direction or project oversight (Contractual Mode 3). Typical examples include:
- Contractor facilities that provide services to multiple customers
- Contractor engineering office such as permanent EPC office sites serving multi-clients
- Contractor fabrication and laydown areas shared with multiple clients.
 Time chartered and spot/voyage chartered Marine Product Transportation Vessels
 Third party retail stores [Dealer-owned dealer-operated (DODO) retail stores].
7.3.2. Conducting Work off Company Premises
Incidents including Injuries and illnesses that result from an event or exposure off the employer's
premises are work-related if the worker was (a) engaged in work-related activities or (b) present as a
condition of employment.
Incidents including Injuries and illnesses that result from events or exposures occurring “Off Company
Premises” are considered work related unless demonstrated otherwise based on detailed investigation
and subject to the Work Relatedness Exceptions in Section 7.2.1. Refer to Appendix 6 for examples.
Work relationship off the employer’s premises is determined by the activity the worker is performing. If
the worker was injured while performing duties in the interest of the company, the injury would be
considered work-related. If the worker was injured while performing normal life activities (e.g., eating),
the injury would not be considered work-related (Section 7.2.1).
An illness or injury resulting from conduct of work-related activity while off company premises is
considered “work-related” (subject to the Exceptions in Section 7.2.1). Examples of instances in which
workers may be engaged in work-related activity at locations off Company Premises include:
 Visiting or inspecting third party facilities (e.g., vendor’s fabrication shop)
 Construction and other work on public or third party land
 Driving to and attending a business meeting (e.g., held at contract engineering firm office or in a
hotel conference room)
 Attending a required Company activity at a private facility (for the duration of the Company activity)
 Injuries to workers participating in social events off company’s premises are not work-related;
unless the workers are required to participate in the social events as a condition or expectation of
employment.
 When working at remote locations chosen by the Company (e.g., Contractor fabrication yard), an
incident would be Work-Related even if it were caused by factors solely related to the environment.
Also see Section 7.4.4 on work-related travel.
7.3.3. Present as A Condition of Employment
“Present as a condition of employment” means the Worker is in a location because of the duties and
responsibilities of their job. Incidents that occur while a Worker is at a location because there is a work-
Standard No.: HSE-GA-ST04 Version No.: 1 Effective date: Dec., 2019 Page 36 of 184

ADNOC Classification: Internal
HSE Management System
HSE Governance & Administration Standards
Incident Notification, Investigation & Reporting
related expectation of their being present at the location are Work Related (subject to the Work
Relatedness Exceptions in Section 7.2.1).
Examples include when Workers are:
 Hosting a service anniversary luncheon, retirement dinner, or a recreation event, off Company
Premises.
Note: Any injuries or illnesses of invited attendees that attend voluntarily are not Work Related.
 Attending a Company-paid and required business luncheon
 Hosting business clients at a sporting event
 Attending work-related training provided by a third party. Where a worker is injured at an off-
premises training session (such as fire-training), the case is work-related if the worker was present
at the session as a condition of employment.
 A truck on the road or loading and unloading away from its home base would be off the employer’s
premises. However, injury or illness exposures experienced during these activities would still be
work-related because the employees are engaged in work-related activities. The truck and its
surroundings are considered part of the work environment even though they are not part of the
employer’s premises.
 Living as required in Company-Controlled accommodations (See Section 7.3.4 & 7.3.5 below)
7.3.4. Company-Controlled Housing Accommodation
Incidents including injuries and illnesses occurring at housing accommodation sites owned, managed,
or controlled by ADNOC Group (employer) and provided to employees as a condition of employment
are considered work-related, unless demonstrated otherwise based on detailed investigation and
subject to the work relatedness Exceptions listed in Section 7.2.1 and below. Incident shall follow the
full cycle of Notification, Recording, Investigation and Reporting.
All incidents shall be investigated to establish work relatedness and Incidents resulting due to a failure
to manage the risks or a lack of risk controls put in place by the company (e.g. slippery floor, lack of
non-slip mat in the shower, no ramp, no maintenance of equipment, poor hygiene, inadequate
emergency protection, etc.) shall be reportable. Reporting in HSE Performance shall accordingly be
evaluated based on the investigation findings (establishing work-relatedness).
The housing accommodations provided by employers to employees is considered to be a "condition of
their employment" when (1) employees are required by the employer to use them or (2) are compelled
by the practical realities of the employment situation to use them. This means that employer-provided
living accommodations are considered a condition of employment when there is an employer policy or
contractual agreement that the employee reside at the employer-furnished housing, or a practical,
economical, geographical, or physical necessity leading to the same result.
Exceptions:
1. If housing made available by the employer is accepted by the employee voluntarily, for example,
on a normal landlord-tenant base, and in preference to other reasonably-available facilities, such
housing would not constitute a condition of employment.
2. Company-controlled housing where Worker family members are permitted to live such as Ruwais
Housing Complex is not considered as work environment. Incidents occurring in these
accommodations should however be investigated for finding the root cause and putting controls to
avoid recurrence.
3. Accommodation rented by employees or leased by the employer where the employer only pays
the accommodation rent / housing allowance is not considered work environment such as housing
in Abu Dhabi.

7.3.5. Reportable Contractors-Controlled Housing Accommodation
Incidents including injuries and illnesses occurring at housing accommodation sites (camps) owned,
managed, or controlled by reportable contractors (employer) constructed within or outside ADNOC
regulated area (CICPA Controlled) exclusive for ADNOC Group, NOT regulated by other UAE Govt.
Departments / Authorities and provided to workers as a condition of employment are considered work-
related, unless demonstrated otherwise based on investigation and subject to the work relatedness
Exceptions listed in Section 7.2.1 and below. Incident shall follow the full cycle of Notification,
Recording, Investigation and Reporting. Reporting in HSE Performance shall however be evaluated
based on the investigation findings (establishing work-relatedness) and considering the below
Exceptions. This is equally binding for housing accommodations being used by multiple contractors
deployed exclusively for different ADNOC Group Companies.
All incidents shall be investigated to establish work relatedness and incidents resulting due to a failure
to manage the risks or a lack of risk controls put in place by the contractor (e.g. slippery floor, lack of
non-slip mat in the shower, no ramp, no maintenance of equipment, poor hygiene, inadequate
emergency protection, etc.) shall be reportable. Reporting in HSE Performance shall accordingly be
evaluated based on the investigation findings (establishing work-relatedness).
The housing accommodation provided by employers to employees is considered to be a "condition of
their employment" when (1) employees are required by the employer to use them or (2) are compelled
by the practical realities of the employment situation to use them. This means that employer-provided
living accommodations are considered a condition of employment when there is an employer policy or
contractual agreement that the employee reside at the employer-furnished housing, or a practical,
economical, geographical, or physical necessity leading to the same result.
Exceptions:
1. Where the contractors’ housing accommodation is regulated by other UAE Government
Departments or Authorities including Municipality, Civil Defence, Center for Waste Management
(TADWEER) in Emirate of Abu Dhabi, Higher Corporation for Specialized Economic Zones
(ZonesCorp) in Emirate of Abu Dhabi. Incidents occurring at contractors’ accommodation shall be
recorded, notified, investigated and reported as per requirements of this Standard but excluded
from reporting in HSE Performance.
2. If housing made available by the employer is accepted by the employee voluntarily, for example,
on a normal landlord-tenant base, and in preference to other reasonably-available facilities, such
housing would not constitute a condition of employment.
3. Contractor-controlled housing accommodation where workers’ family members are permitted to
live are not considered work environment. Incidents occurring in these accommodations should
however be investigated for finding the root cause and putting controls to avoid recurrence
4. Accommodation rented by employees or leased by the employer where the employer only pays
the accommodation rent / housing allowance is not considered work environment such as housing
in Abu Dhabi.

7.4. WORK RELATEDNESS FOR TRAVEL INCIDENTS
7.4.1. Commuting Incidents
The daily trips between the residence and work site are considered as employees’ commute regardless
of whether they are made by personal vehicle or company-provided vehicle.
Commuting incidents are considered non-work related unless demonstrated otherwise based on
investigation and subject to the exceptions given in this Section and Work Relatedness Exceptions in
Section 7.2.1. Refer to Table 7.4.1 Scenarios for Commuting & Travel Work Relatedness
After initial notification of an incident has been completed, a preliminary analysis of the incident shall
be completed within 24 hours and a decision shall be taken on the incident whether it is work-related
or not by the respective Group Company / Incident Owner.
Group Company responsible departments (Human Capital & Administration etc.) may refer “Industrial
Hours” for processing the insurance and compensation claims for non-work related incidents (see
definition of “Industrial Hours”).
The following commuting activities in a personal or a company-provided vehicle (not provided as
condition of employment) are not considered to be the part of work and any incident occurring from
these activities are non-work related.
 Travel between the employees’ home and a regular place of work or assembly point during the first
and last trip of the day.
 Travel from home to first work site and travel from last work site to home.
 Travel between a workers’ identified work location and any location for personal business, including
a non-business lunch or meal break (or other non-business activity such as doctor appointment,
personal work, etc.).
 Travel between workers’ established ‘controlled housing’ site to the first worksite or to any location
for personal business, including a restaurant.
 Commuting to work for normal duty, even at irregular hours (late arrival / early departure) from
home / camp to the work location (HQ / Site) and vice versa.
 Travel between home and a non-company event e.g. local conference or other similar
 Travel using company provided vehicle for non-work related activities.
Exceptions:
Commuting shall be work related when it is done (a) in transportation operated or provided by the
Company (or the Reportable Contractor Company) as a condition of work / employment and/or
because alternatives are either not allowed or not available or (b) during emergency evacuation. The
following are examples of when commuting is considered to be work-related:
(a) Company/Reportable Contractor-Provided Transport
If the company requires the employee to use the company provided transport and the requirement
to use the company transport is a condition of employment, this makes the commute work related.
However if the company provides the transport free of charge to its employees / contractors and
the use of the service is encouraged but optional, it shall remain normal commute and non-work
related.
 Aircraft or Vessel to Offshore Company Premise: Personnel (including the crew) travelling
in Company/Reportable Contractor’s provided transportation (via owned/chartered aircraft or
vessels designed for personnel movement, i.e., crew boats/surfers) to or from an offshore
Company Premise as a condition of employment are Work-Related at all times. Travel from
home to the terminal / dock is considered normal commuting and hence non work related.
Standard No.: HSE-GA-ST04 Version No.: 1 Effective date: Dec., 2019 Page 39 of 184

ADNOC Classification: Internal
HSE Management System
HSE Governance & Administration Standards
Incident Notification, Investigation & Reporting
Note: Travel of crewmembers aboard an MNPV not designed for personnel transport during
its initial equipment mobilization to, and subsequent demobilization from, an offshore company
premise location is not considered Reportable business travel.
Incidents occurring while travelling to or from an offshore worksite using personal means of
transportation in violation to ADNOC direction or not approved by ADNOC are excluded for
reporting in HSE Performance. These incidents shall however follow the full cycle of
Notification, Recording, Investigation and Reporting to identify root cause and implement
controls to avoid recurrence
 Company/Reportable Contractor-Provided Vehicles: Personnel (including the driver) being
driven to work each day by a Company/Reportable Contractor-provided vehicle as a condition
of employment or in a location where no other means of transportation is available is
considered Work-Related.
(b) Emergency Evacuation: Travel associated with emergency evacuation of a worksite (e.g., off
platform due to threat of a hurricane) is work-related and is treated as a business trip.
Note: “Company/Reportable Contractor-provided transportation” is defined as personnel transportation
operated/directly contracted by the Company or operated/directly contracted by the Reportable
Contractor as required by, directed by, or as otherwise arranged directly on behalf of the Company. It
does not include transportation of personnel separately arranged by the Contractor for its own
convenience and outside of contract requirements.
7.4.2. Commuting / Parking Lots
 An employee's normal commute from home to office and return is not considered to be work
related. Therefore, any injury or illness occurring during this trip would not be recordable.
 Company parking facilities used for the worker's daily commuting are generally not considered part
of the employer's premises. Injuries or illnesses which occur to workers on these parking lots are
not presumed to be work-related, and are not reportable unless the worker was engaged in some
work-related activity or present as a condition of employment. if an employee is injured in a car
accident in the company parking lot while arriving at work or while leaving the company's property
at the end of the day, the case would not be work-related
 A parking lot ends with the perimeter of the parking pavement or surface. The boundary could be
the fence surrounding the parking lot in one case, or the curb encircling the parking spaces in
another. Parking ramps are normally considered part of parking facilities, not part of the employer's
premises. A parking lot is defined as the area on which cars can be parked; it does not include
surrounding sidewalks, which are considered part of the employer’s premises. Stairs and sidewalks
contained within the perimeter of a parking garage or facility are considered part of the parking lot.
 Examples when the injury would be considered Work-Related:
- Injuries incurred in a parking lot while repairing it or while going on a work-related trip are work-
related.
- Injuries incurred while passing through a parking lot to get from Building A to Building B on the
employer’s premises are considered work-related, since the worker had completed the
commute to work.
- Injuries incurred on a public sidewalk while engaged in work-related activity, such as walking
to a business meeting, are reportable.
- If an injury occurs in a company-maintained parking lot during normal commuting due to a
deficiency in that parking lot (such as potholes, slippery conditions, accumulated debris, etc.),
then the injury is work-related and reportable.
 Examples when the injury would not be considered Work-Related:
- Where a worker has to use a “swipe badge” to get through the company gate and is then on
the employer’s premises, but has an injury before reaching the parking lot, the injury is
considered to have occurred during a normal commute and is not presumed to be work-related.
Standard No.: HSE-GA-ST04 Version No.: 1 Effective date: Dec., 2019 Page 40 of 184

ADNOC Classification: Internal
HSE Management System
HSE Governance & Administration Standards
Incident Notification, Investigation & Reporting
- Injuries occurring on a public sidewalk while walking from a parking lot to an employer’s
premises are considered to be part of the normal commute and not reportable.
- Injuries incurred on a public sidewalk during a break are not presumed to be work-related.
- A Worker is injured when struck while walking in the Company parking lot to begin their
workday by a vehicle driven by another commuting Worker.
- A worker crashes their vehicle in the Company parking while attempting to leave for the day.
- Injuries from a fight between workers in a company parking lot are not work-related.
7.4.3. Commuting Outside of Work Environment
Commuting outside of Company Premises (or other Work Environment locations) is not Work-Related.
The following define some situations under which an individual is considered to be commuting.
 Rotator Commuting: Travel between the worker’s home country and destination in the assigned
work country is considered to be commuting as long as it is by commercial service (air, road, rail,
sea).
- Required travel by chartered flights and Company/Reportable Contractor-provided
transportation is viewed as work-related travel (Section 7.4.4).
 Delayed Relocation Status Commuting: An employee on "delayed relocation status" after a
transfer is considered to be commuting when traveling between home and the new regular work
location, regardless of the distance of travel and of whether or not the Company reimburses the
travel cost.
 Commuting to Sites other than Normal Work Location: Workers traveling to/from a location
(e.g., another office for meeting or hotel for seminar) that is different than their normal work location
may still be considered commuting if all of the following conditions are met:
- The travel must be the first trip of the day from home to work, the last trip of the day from work
back home, or other non-business travel from the work location for lunch or to handle other
personal affairs.
- The different work location is in the same municipal region (metropolitan area) as the Worker's
normal geographic area of operation.
- The travel does not require overnight accommodation (during the journey).
- The travel is not the result of the Worker being “on call” responding to an emergency situation,
or other unusual circumstance (e.g., being called to worksite when not scheduled to be on site)
7.4.4. Work-Related Travel
Employees traveling on Company business away from their normal geographic area of operation are
considered engaged in work-related activities for the duration of the trip between when they depart
from and return to the point of origin of the trip. Refer to Table 7.4.1 Scenarios for Commuting & Travel
Work Relatedness
A worker's "normal geographic area of operation" includes the town or city where the worker normally
works and directly adjoining municipalities.
Example: An Abu Dhabi Emirate based employees traveling on business to Dubai Emirate constitutes
work related travel regardless of the mode of transportation.
Travels between a worker's residence and a point outside of the worker's normal geographic area of
operations is considered work-related and not a normal commute.
Travel for work is a work-related activity even if the travel occurs on a day the employee is not
scheduled to work.
Standard No.: HSE-GA-ST04 Version No.: 1 Effective date: Dec., 2019 Page 41 of 184

ADNOC Classification: Internal
HSE Management System
HSE Governance & Administration Standards
Incident Notification, Investigation & Reporting
Work-related activities begin when the employee leaves home, assuming the employee did not intend
to report to his or her office prior to beginning the trip. If the employee first reports to the office, travel
status begins when the employee leaves the office to begin the trip. Travel status ends once the
employee returns to either their home or normal workplace.
However, when a traveling employee checks into a hotel or motel, company provided accommodation
or comparable accommodations, the employee establishes a "home away from home". Thereafter, the
Employee's activities are evaluated in the same manner as for non-traveling employees. Refer to
Section 7.4.5 for travel activities not considered work related.
Employees who travel on company business are considered to be engaged in work-related activities
all the time they spend in the interest of the company, including, but not limited to, travel to and from
customer contacts; and entertaining or being entertained for the purpose of transacting, discussing, or
promoting business, etc.
In this context, the following are considered to be the part of the work related travel and any incidents
occurring from these travel activities are work related.
 When travelling is indeed part of the work away from their normal geographic area of operation in
a company provided transport. In a situation where company provided transport is not available
and the employee is notified formally with an approval from Line Manager, then travel on personal
or rented transport is also considered as work related travel.
 Travels for business purpose such as meetings, audits, reviews etc. from worksite A to worksite B
in a company provided transport or personal or rented transport approved by Line Manager.
 When a worker on travel status is injured while participating in recreational activities with a
customer, whether the injury is work-related depends on whether the worker's participation in the
activities was work-related. Thus, an off-road incident over the weekend would not be work-related
if solely for recreational purposes, but would be work-related if the off-roading included transacting,
promoting, or discussing business, or was perceived by the worker to be an expectation of
employment.
 Where a worker is injured in the parking lot of an off-site training facility, whether the injury is
considered work-related or not depends on whether the worker was in travel status or engaged in
a normal commute. If the worker was in travel status, the injury is considered work-related.
Note: Employees on International Travel for Official Missions etc. shall follow the respective Human
Capital & Administration (HC&A) Procedures and requirements on “Travelling Abroad”.
7.4.5. Travel Activities – Not considered Work Related
There are conditions under which travel activities are not considered Work Related:
 Work relatedness shall not apply for injuries / illnesses occurring while on business travel / mission
outside the UAE. Therefore, the injuries or illnesses that may occur to a worker traveling outside
the UAE (starting from the port of departure) shall be recorded in company log however not
reported in HSE performance.
 “Home Away from Home”: When a traveling Worker physically checks into a hotel, company
provided accommodation or comparable accommodations, the Worker establishes a "home away
from home". Injuries or illnesses are not considered work-related if they occur while the employee is
commuting between the temporary residence "home away from home" and to a fixed worksite. These
cases are similar to those involving employees commuting to and from work when they are at their
home location, and as such, not work related (e.g., transit to initial worksite and last transit to hotel
at days end constitute commuting unless conveyed by required Company/Reportable Contractor-
provided transportation; injuries sustained inside the hotel are Work-Related only if related to
performance of work).
There are situations where workers in travel status report to, or rotate among, several different
worksites after they establish their "home away from home" (such as a salesperson travelling to
and from different customer contacts). In these situations, the injuries sustained when travelling to
and from the sales locations would be considered work-related.

HSE Governance & Administration Standards
Incident Notification, Investigation & Reporting
Injuries and illnesses occurring at accommodations owned, managed, or controlled by the
Company and furnished to employees as a condition of employment are considered work-related.
This means that Company provided living accommodations, such temporary residence on off-
shore platforms, would not be considered a "home away from home" for the purposes of work
relatedness.
 Non-Business Related Movements and Activities: Movements or activities not associated with
employment occurring while on travel status are not considered Work Related. Examples include:
 Travel for personal reasons, even if in a vehicle furnished by the Company, e.g.:
- Travel from hotel to eat non-business dinner
- Non-business related sightseeing or visits to markets
 Recreational activities undertaken for personal reasons (e.g., jogging or walking for exercise)
 Taking a Personal Detour from a reasonably direct route of travel (e.g., side trip for personal
reasons). An injury or illness would not be reportable if it occurred during normal life activities
(eating, sleeping, recreation); or if the worker deviates from a reasonably direct route (side trip for
vacation or other personal reasons). He or she would again be in the course of employment when
he or she returned to the normal route of travel. Refer to 7.2.1 for exceptions.
 If a person changed the normal route without any valid justification e.g. road closed by authorities
etc. while on an approved business mission/travel and injured as a result of the stops / activities /
travel while on a detour. Incidents are not considered work-related if they occur while the employee
is on a personal detour from a reasonably direct route of travel (e.g., has taken a side trip for
personal reasons).
 Commuting during Travel: Commuting while on travel status (e.g., between the hotel and the
worksite) or while on rotational status (between the home country and the assigned worksite) is
not considered work-related, except in cases defined in Section 7.4.1.

7.4.6. Contractors’ Travel
A Contractor’s travel must be directly on behalf of the Company to be considered work related.
For contractors working full time for the Company, work-related travel between sites during the day
is considered work-related subject to the condition described above. For example:
Example 1: A contractor, who is working full time on a Company construction project, commutes each
morning from his home to the job site. Once per week, he travels from the job site to a Company office
15 miles away for a mid-day status meeting with project management, and then returns to the job site.
The contractor travel to and from the job site and the Company office is Reportable since he was
working directly for the Company, and the Company required him to travel from one Company Premise
location to another.
Example 2: A contractor is asked to come to a meeting at a company’s facility at 2:00 PM. Since the
contractor was not working for the Company prior to or after the meeting, he is only reportable while
on Company premises.
For Contractors that do not work full time for the Company, the determination of work relatedness of
travel depends on the degree of direction provided. The presumption is that, without specific direction,
the Contractor is able to work for others when not at the Company-requested work site. As examples:
Example 1: Company hired a contract inspector and requested him go to the vendor shop immediately
to resolve the problem. The contract inspector would be reportable for the travel as well as the time
spent at the vendor’s shop, since travel is directly on behalf of the Company. If the Company requested
the inspector to "drop by the vendor’s shop sometime during the next week and give us a report", the
inspector would be reportable while at the vendor’s facility but not while traveling to or from the shop,
as he is not traveling directly for the Company.
Example 2: A contractor is hired to fly spotter planes along company’s pipeline routes, looking for oil
spills. The contract calls for observing the pipeline at least once per week, but there is no fixed time
and the contractor is free to work for others between trips. The contractor would only be reportable
while the plane was on Company Premises (includes airspace). If, for example, the pilot is injured while
attempting to land at nearby airport, the incident is not reportable, assuming the airport is not on
Company Premises.
Example 3: A contractor is hired to maintain ground water recovery well pumps at various Company
locations. The contract calls for visiting each site at least once per week, but there is no fixed time that
each site must be visited, and the contractor is free to work for others between visits. The contractor
would only be Reportable while on Company Premises.
7.4.7. Work Relatedness for Motor Vehicle Crash
The scope for reporting of Motor Vehicle Crashes (MVC) applies to employees and reportable
contractors and covers motor vehicles defined in this Standard (see definition).
Any crash involving a company, leased or personal vehicle while performing company business shall
be work related.
Standard No.: HSE-GA-ST04 Version No.: 1 Effective date: Dec., 2019 Page 45 of 184

ADNOC Classification: Internal
HSE Management System
HSE Governance & Administration Standards
Incident Notification, Investigation & Reporting
Work-relationship is presumed for crashes resulting from business being conducted on behalf of the
company while operating a company assigned vehicle. Examples of company business include driving
a client to the airport, driving to the airport for a business trip, taking a client or work colleague out for
a meal, deliveries, visiting clients or customers, or driving to a business related appointment.
Personal business which is not work related includes, but is not limited to, personal shopping, getting
a meal by yourself, commuting to and from home, or driving to a private medical appointment.
Contractor Motor Vehicle Crash includes any vehicle operated by a reportable contractor while
performing work on behalf of the company, where injuries, kilometers driven or hours worked should
be recorded (e.g. delivery/courier services are excluded).
Exclusions from reporting:
The following should not be reported as a motor vehicle crash when the vehicle is properly parked:
 injuries that occur when entering or exiting the vehicle
 any event involving loading or unloading from the vehicle
 another motor vehicle crashes into the parked vehicle.
In addition, the following should not be reported as a motor vehicle crash:
 damage to or total loss of a vehicle solely due to environmental conditions (such as a flood) or
vandalism
 damage related to the theft of a vehicle
 superficial damage, such as a stone/rock chip damaging a windscreen/or paintwork while the
vehicle is being driven
 an event where there has been no collision or any other damage than to the vehicle itself, this
includes but not limited to: engine fire, losing a wheel and brake failure while maintaining control
of the vehicle.
7.5. SITUATIONS WHERE INJURY OR ILLNESS RELATEDNESS TO WORK
ENVIRONMENT IS UNCLEAR
In cases where the injury or illness relatedness to the Work Environment is unclear because (a) there
is no identifiable event or exposure that occurred in the Work Environment or (b) non-work-related
factors (pre-existing conditions, activities outside of work) may have caused, contributed to, or
significantly aggravated a symptom that surfaces in the workplace:
• The case shall be reviewed and final classification issued by ADNOC Group HSE Function;
• GC Occupational Health (OH) Specialist/LHCP shall be consulted for their opinion on the work-
relatedness of the case in all potentially recordable cases, subject to available information for
Contractor cases.
• Assessment of such cases, should apply the “More Likely Than Not” Test:
Is it more likely than not (i.e., greater than 50% probability) that work-related factors contributed
either to cause or significantly aggravate the injury or illness?
An event or exposure in the Work Environment need not be the sole or predominant cause to make
the injury or illness case potentially Work-Related. The injury is considered Work-Related if a work-
related event or exposure represents any discernible contribution to the cause of an (or to the
significant aggravation of an existing) injury or illness.
Refer to Appendix 6 for examples.

7.6. INCIDENT SEVERITY ASSESSMENT
Determination of severity is critical to define the scale of exposure and consequences experienced during
an incident. The severity of the incident shall be assessed using the ADNOC Risk Matrix (Appendix 1).
The severity categories are based on ADNOC HSE Risk Classification Methodology and ADNOC Risk
Matrix (Appendix 1).
All incidents (whether resulting in an undesirable outcome and consequences or not) must be evaluated
to access the potential severity and classify them as per the severity of consequences described in the
ADNOC Risk Matrix (Appendix 1).
7.6.1. Actual Severity
The actual severity of an incident is determined by evaluating the resulting damage and assigning a
numeric value (1 to 6) using the consequence categories described in the ADNOC Risk Matrix. Where
an incident results in more than one consequence categories, the overall severity ranking will be
determined by the consequence category with the highest level of severity.
7.6.2. Potential Severity
The potential severity of the incident and the probability of a similar incident re-occurring determines the
degree of reporting required in the event of an incident. When determining potential severity, the assessor
must take consideration of active barriers in place during the incident, then develop the potential scenario
considering the barriers that are considered to have failed (worse-case scenario). The potential scenario
must be documented and recorded for further analysis and verification.
All Incidents including Near Misses and shall be analyzed to determine potential severity by evaluating
the probability and potential consequences by using the ADNOC Risk Matrix (APPENDIX 1). The
investigation process must verify and validate the initial potential severity assessment and provide all
required information for this process. Only a validated potential severity can be considered for incident
recording.
7.6.3. High Potential Events (HIPO)
High Potential (HIPO) events are the incidents with potential Severity 4 or above on the ADNOC Risk
Matrix. Refer to Section 7.12 for details on determining notification and reporting of HIPO event and
Appendix 4 for a non-exhaustive list of HIPO events.
The learning value of an incident does not affect the decision on HIPO classification. HIPO classification
retains an element of ‘judgment’. The judgment is based on the consequences that in different
circumstances ‘could reasonably result’ from the event and the robustness of barriers in place.
7.6.4. Hurt Level
In the Hurt Free Approach, the actual and potential consequence of a safety or health work related
event in the work environment is assessed according to six levels of injury severity, which includes
consideration of the duration of the effect. Section 7.13 provides details on process for Hurt Level
determination and reporting requirements.

7.7. DETERMINING INCIDENT’S RECORDABILITY & REPORTABILTY
7.7.1. Recordable Incidents
All Incidents are Recordable (Logged) in ADNOC Group’s incident recording system, if it is in any
way associated with the employees or activities of ADNOC Group or a Contractor.
Irrespective of the severity, ALL incidents including but not limited to the following confirmed
occurrences shall be recorded internally by the Group Company / Incident Owner.
 Fatality
 Work-related injuries (occupational safety)
 Work-related illnesses or diseases (occupational health)
 Environmental Incidents
 Financial Impacts / Asset Damages
 Process Safety Events
 Work-related travel / commuting incidents
 Near Misses
 Non-Accidental Death (NAD)
 Motor Vehicle Crashes (MVC)
 Reputation Impacts e.g. workers unrest, strikes, violence etc.
 Others e.g. welfare, etc.
Recordable Injuries / Illness
Recordable Injuries / Illness requiring reporting in Total Recordable Injury Rate [TRIR] includes any
work related injury or illness resulting in:
 Fatality or Permanent Total Disability or Permanent Partial Disability
 Lost Workday or Restricted Work
 Medical Treatment Case
The Injury Recordability and Reporting requirements are illustrated in Figure 7.7.1 & 7.7.2
Note: An injury may progress from a lower category to a higher or more severe category; however it
must be reported only in the higher category.
Incident Owner shall revise the Injury / Illness classification where required due to escalation (low to
high severity), communicate to ADNOC Group HSE (Severity 3 or above) following the requirements
of this Standard and update the HSE Performance.
7.7.2. Reportable Incidents to ADNOC
All work related incidents with Actual Severity 3 or above OR Potential Severity 4 or above involving
ADNOC Group’s or Reportable Contractors’ employees are Reportable to ADNOC within the
timeframe prescribed in this Standard.
Injuries and illnesses of secondee employee shall be reported and investigated by the host employer
who supervises the secondee employee’s work on a day-to-day basis even if these employees are not
carried on the host employer’s payroll. This shall follow the same requirements of the reportable
contractors.
Refer to Section 7.14 for details on notification requirements and Appendix 6 for examples of
Reportable Incidents. Where an incident results in more than one consequence categories, the overall
severity ranking will be determined by the consequence category with the highest level of severity.
Incidents Reportable to ADNOC include but not limited to the following.

7.7.3. Incidents Involving Radioactive Sources
Federal Authority for Nuclear Regulation (FANR) license holders within ADNOC Group shall follow the
FANR regulations for reporting incidents involving radioactive sources to FANR as per the timeline
stipulated in the relevant regulation [FANR-REG-24 (Article 19)]
7.7.4. Contagious Diseases
Contagious diseases (e.g. flu, chickenpox, measles etc.) arising within work environment are
considered work related unless demonstrated otherwise based on detailed investigation. Reporting in
HSE Performance (TRIR) shall accordingly be evaluated based on the investigation findings
(establishing work-relatedness). Group Companies shall establish internal procedures in consultation
with the Occupational Health Subject Matter Expert (OH SME) / Licensed Health Care Professional
(LHCP) to take necessary measures including quarantine, case management etc. to avoid spreading
disease to other employees. Refer to Appendix 6 Section 6 for examples of Occupational Illnesses.
ADNOC Occupational Health Framework (HSE-OH-ST01) shall be consulted for relevant guidance.
Reporting of contagious disease to relevant authorities, if required, shall follow the applicable
regulations established by the relevant authorities and ADNOC Group Medical Services Division /
Human Capital & Administration (HCA) Procedures.

7.7.5. On Board Aircraft & Sea Passengers
Investigation of incidents involving ADNOC Group employees while on board as aircraft passengers or
international sea passengers are normally conducted by the relevant authorities e.g. aviation, maritime
etc. In such cases, investigation reports, as applicable, shall be furnished by the concerned Group
Company / Directorate for insurance claims and compensation purposes in line with ADNOC Human
Capital & Administration (HCA) relevant procedures. Refer to Section 7.4.4 “Work-related Travel” and
Section 7.4.5 about work relatedness for incidents occurring during business travel and international
missions.

7.8. CLASSIFYING THE INJURY OR ILLNESS – TREATMENT BASED REPORTING
Whether a case is categorized as an injury or illness is determined by the nature of the original event
or exposure [i.e. interface with a hazardous agent] that caused the case, not by the resulting condition
of the affected employee. Injuries are caused by instantaneous events in the work environment or
events close in time. Cases resulting from anything other than instantaneous events are usually
considered illnesses.
Therefore, an occupational injury is any injury such as a cut, fracture, sprain, amputation, etc., which
results from a work incident or from an exposure involving a single incident in the work environment.
Conditions resulting from animal bites, such as insect or snakebites, or from one-time exposure to
chemicals are also considered to be injuries. (Refer to Section 5: Definitions and Section 7.2:
Establishing Work Relatedness.)
The method for reporting certain types of cases has its foundation in industrial safety practice. Safety
measures required to avoid instantaneous events are considered fundamentally different from those
required to prevent many occupational illnesses with a long latency period, or for which exposures over
a period of time are necessary for symptoms to appear. Hence, the classification of a case as an injury
or an illness is intended to reflect this distinction.
A work-related injury or illness of an Employee or Reportable Contractor is recordable if it:
 Is a “New Case” or a “Significant Aggravation of a Pre-existing Condition” (See Appendix 6)
AND
 Meets one or more of the following treatment-based reporting criteria:
- Fatality including Third Party Fatality (Section 7.8.1)
- Lost Workdays Case (Section 7.8.2)
- Disability (Section 7.8.3)
- Occupational Illness (Section 7.8.4 & Appendix 2)
- Restricted Work or transfer to another job (Section 7.8.5)
- Medical Treatment beyond First Aid (Section 7.8.6) including “Significant Diagnosed
Injury or Illness” such as cancer, chronic irreversible diseases, fractured or cracked
bones or teeth, and punctured eardrums, hearing loss, loss of consciousness, etc.)
 Section 7.10 establishes the process for managing conflicting contemporaneous medical
recommendations provided for the same injury or illness.
Standard No.: HSE-GA-ST04 Version No.: 1 Effective date: Dec., 2019 Page 52 of 184

ADNOC Classification: Internal
HSE Management System
HSE Governance & Administration Standards
Incident Notification, Investigation & Reporting
7.8.1. Fatality
“Fatality” is defined as a death of an individual that is determined to be Work-Related regardless of
the time intervening between the incident and death.
Cases that involve one or more people who died as a result of a work-related incident. ‘Delayed’ deaths
that occur after the incident are to be included if the deaths were a direct result of the incident
For example, if a fire killed one person outright, and a second died three weeks later from lung damage
caused by the fire, both shall be reported.
In some cases, a delayed fatality occurs in the next calendar year after the incident. For example, if
the above fire occurred on December 21, the second death from it might occur in January of the next
year. All fatalities from an incident should be included in the report for the year incident occurred
Employee or Reportable Contractor Fatalities are recorded as Lost Time Incidents.
Fatalities of Third Parties are also tracked if they are determined to be related to the business of the
Company (see below Section a).
Fatalities under the category of Non Accidental Death (NAD) shall be investigated and reported as per
Section 7.18.
(a) Third Party Fatality
A “Third Party Fatality” is defined as a fatal injury to an individual, other than a Company Employee
or Reportable Contractor, that includes any of the following (unless subject to the Exceptions below):
1. Involves Company Premises, regardless of the cause of the injury, e.g., including fatal injuries to:
 Customers at Company Owned & Operated retail stores
 Customers or dealer/agency employees at Company-owned dealer or agency-operated retail
sites.
 Visitors and Excluded Contractors at a Company office/worksite
2. Is the direct result of Company operations, e.g., including fatal injuries to third parties as a result
of incidents involving:
 Company/Reportable Contractor-operated motor vehicle
 Personal vehicle while in use on Company business
 Full-time or dedicated charter transport while operating for the Company
 Company-operated or bareboat chartered marine vessel
3. Is the direct result of the transport of Company property, e.g., including fatalities affecting:
 Spot charter truck driver and/or other third parties killed in an incident involving a spot charter
truck while transporting Company product not on Company Premises.
Note: A spot charter driver on Company Premises is considered a reportable Contractor.
Exceptions for Reporting of Third Party Fatalities:
Excluded from reporting requirements are:
1. Off Company Premises incidents related to:
 Joint ventures operated-by-others
 Spot charter trucks (off Company Premises) that are not transporting Company property
 Time or voyage/spot chartered marine transportation vessels
Standard No.: HSE-GA-ST04 Version No.: 1 Effective date: Dec., 2019 Page 53 of 184

ADNOC Classification: Internal
HSE Management System
HSE Governance & Administration Standards
Incident Notification, Investigation & Reporting
2. On Company Premises incidents involving:
 “Random” acts of violence unrelated to Company, dealer or agency operations. “Random” as
used here means each/every target (company, industry, expats, and nationals) has an equal
probability of being chosen. Examples of acts considered to be “random” include:
- Fights or other acts of violence between third parties for personal reasons
- Robbery of customer resulting in customer death.
- Note: Non-Random Acts – Third party fatalities arising from nonrandom acts are
recordable. Examples of non-random acts include:
 Terrorist activity, civil unrest, or other violence when targeted towards the
Company, the industry, or any expats/third-parties that may be considered
as collateral targets
 Robbery of a retail store resulting in customer death.
Note: Analysis may be needed to determine if the fatality is related to Company business (non-random)
or is random.
 Third-party incidents originating off Company Premises that incidentally or unintentionally carry
over to the premises (e.g., a vehicle driver or bicyclist loses control on the street and
unintentionally enters the premises).
- This exclusion applies to the occupants or riders of the vehicles, motorcycles,
bicycles, etc. The exclusion does not apply to Employees, Reportable Contractors, or
third parties present on the premises that are fatally injured as a result of this type
incident.
- The exclusion does not apply if the vehicle(s) was intending to enter the Company
Premises.
3. Third-party fatalities for which there is no known connection to the operation (e.g., random heart
attack, illegal drug overdose, suicide, etc.) or no cause can be determined.
7.8.2. Lost Workdays Case (LWDC)
The determination of Lost Workdays Case is based on whether or not the Worker was in condition to
return to work, an LHCP recommends days away from work for medical reasons, or the employer
recommends days away from work due to the injury or illness or because they cannot accommodate
recommended work limitations.
It is not based on whether or not the person actually worked or would have worked on that following
day. Therefore issues such as the following are not relevant in determining the classification:
 Whether the next calendar day is a scheduled workday or scheduled day off (e.g., a weekend,
a vacation day, a holiday, or a plant closing)
 Whether the person missed time at work while seeking appropriate medical attention
 If a Worker is placed on 'administrative leave' due to a drug test or other reason (Note: This
factor also should not be used or considered by medical personnel when considering whether
to recommend work limitations or days away from work.)
It is not based on whether or not the person could conceivably do some work.
 “Working at Hospital” and “Working at Home” are not acceptable alternatives to recording a
Lost Workdays Case. Providing a Worker with work-related tasks to complete while recovering
in the hospital or at home does not affect the requirement to record as Lost Time.
