Incident Notification, Investigation & Reporting
(a) Reporting Requirements
1. Time Lapse Before LHCP Evaluation:
When time elapses between the occurrence of a work-related injury or illness and obtaining a
medical recommendation with respect to the classification of the injury/illness (e.g., work-related
injury occurs on Thursday before scheduled time off on Friday and Saturday and an LHCP
evaluates the Worker on Sunday), the Worker’s management should request a recommendation
from the LHCP conducting the evaluation regarding the injured person's ability to have worked,
with or without restriction, during the interval before the medical evaluation was obtained. The
injury should be classified according to the LHCP evaluation (e.g., as “Lost Time (Lost Workdays)”
if determined that the Worker could not have worked on the following day, as “Restricted Work” if
the Worker could have worked with restrictions).
2. Counting Lost Workdays: Number of days away from work are to be recorded.
All calendar days (i.e., including weekend days, holidays, vacation days or other days off) after the
day on which incident occurred are included in the total number of days recorded if the Worker
would not have been able to work on those days because of a work-related injury or illness.
Note: The maximum Lost Workdays reportable for each Lost Workdays Case is 180.
7.8.3. Disability
(a) Permanent Total Disability:
 Any work related injury or illness resulting in termination of employment as employee
permanently incapacitates from doing any work.
 Permanent total disability shall be recorded and reported as LTI similar to fatality
recordkeeping and reporting requirements.
Examples: Disabilities as listed in Schedule No. 2 of UAE Federal Law No. 8 includes loss of
both arms from the shoulder, loss of any two or more limbs, total loss of sight or loss of both
eyes, total paralysis, Imbecility or total mental deficiency, Injuries or incidents to the head or skull
causing continuous headache.
(b) Permanent Partial Disability (PPD):
 Any work-related injury or illness which results in the complete loss or permanent loss of use
of any part(s) of the body or any permanent impairment of function or parts of body, regardless
of any pre-existing disability of the injured member of impaired body function.
 A PPD is not related to the ability of the injured person to do his normal work. Thus, a PPD is
classified if the worker has lost a finger, toe, arm, limb, hearing, etc. but upon recovery was
still able to perform normal work or any other work that permits for the partial disability.
Note: Reference is made to the Federal Law of 1980 on Regulating Labour Relations, which
provides a detailed description of such injuries. The examples above do not exclude all other
statutory requirements
 Any lost workdays associated with the PPD shall be reported in line with the requirements of
this Standard.
7.8.4. Occupational Illness
An occupational illness is any abnormal condition or disorder, other than one resulting from an
occupational injury, caused by exposure to environmental factors associated with employment. It
includes acute and chronic illnesses or diseases that may be caused by inhalation, absorption,
ingestion, or direct contact. Examples include food poisoning, chronic hearing loss, infectious diseases
and dermatitis. Refer to Appendix 2 for List of Serious Occupational Illness / Diseases and ADNOC
List of Occupational Diseases in ADNOC Health Screening & Surveillance Standard (HSE-OH-ST05).
Occupational illness includes acute and chronic illnesses or diseases that may be caused by inhalation,
absorption, ingestion, or direct contact, as well as repetitive strain cases (e.g. carpal tunnel syndrome)
Standard No.: HSE-GA-ST04 Version No.: 1 Effective date: Dec., 2019 Page 55 of 184

ADNOC Classification: Internal
HSE Management System
HSE Governance & Administration Standards
Incident Notification, Investigation & Reporting
and potentially other "ergonomically induced" conditions. Also, occupational illness includes mental
stress and psychiatric disorders that directly result from objectively verifiable psychologically traumatic
events. Illnesses are:
 deemed to have occurred on the date of diagnosis (for purposes of reporting)
 limited to active employees and/or contractors i.e. those on the job, not those on extensive
rest/leave periods. E.g. if an employee on a 60/20 days schedule proceeds perfectly healthy
to his leave/rest period (usually abroad) than illness contracted during his leave/rest period
should normally not be considered an occupational illness.
 those conditions where diagnosis is clear and diagnosed by a LHCP
 limited to those cases where there is substantial evidence that the condition was caused by a
work-related exposure
(a) Distinguishing between Injury and Illness
Whether a case is categorized as an injury or illness is determined by the nature of the original event
or exposure that caused the case, not by the resulting condition of the affected employee. Injuries are
caused by instantaneous events in the work environment or events close in time. Cases resulting from
anything other than instantaneous events are usually considered illnesses. This concept of illnesses
includes acute illnesses that result from exposures of relatively short duration.
Therefore, an occupational injury is any injury such as a cut, fracture, sprain, amputation, etc., which
results from a work incident or from an exposure involving a single incident in the work environment.
Conditions resulting from animal bites, such as insect or snakebites, or from one-time exposure to
chemicals are also considered to be injuries. An occupational illness is any abnormal condition or
disorder, other than one resulting from an occupational injury, caused by exposure to environmental
factors associated with employment. It includes acute and chronic illnesses or diseases that may be
caused by inhalation, absorption, ingestion, or direct contact. Examples include food poisoning, chronic
hearing loss, infectious diseases and dermatitis.
Some conditions may be classified as either an injury or an illness (but not both), depending upon the
nature of the event that produced the condition. For example, a loss of hearing resulting from an
explosion (an instantaneous event) is classified as an injury. The same condition arising from exposure
to industrial noise over a period of time would be classified as an occupational illness. Similarly,
irritation of the throat from exposure to chlorine gas fumes could be classified as either an injury or an
illness. If the exposure was instantaneous and occurred when a cylinder of gas ruptured, the case
would be considered an injury. The case would be an illness if the employee were exposed to the agent
over time, such as working in an area where chlorine fumes from a bleaching process were present
over a period of time. Muscle strain due to overexertion over a period of a few hours is considered to
be an injury. Refer to Appendix 6, Section 6 for explanation on distinguishing between Injury & Illness
and examples on reporting requirements.
When tracking illness, it is critical to determine that the occurrence is occupational i.e. work-related. In
some cases, such as contact dermatitis, the relationship between an illness and work-related exposure
is easy to recognize; but in other cases where the occupational cause is not direct and apparent, it may
be difficult to determine accurately whether an employee’s illness is occupational in nature.
In these situations, investigating the following aspects is important:
1. Has an illness been clearly established by LHCP?
2. Does it appear that the illness resulted from, or was aggravated by conditions in the work
environment?
3. Are there (or were there) hazardous agents present in the work environment?
4. Was the employee exposed to these agents in the work environment?
5. Can the nature and extent of exposure to these hazards be correlated with the illness?
Standard No.: HSE-GA-ST04 Version No.: 1 Effective date: Dec., 2019 Page 56 of 184

ADNOC Classification: Internal
HSE Management System
HSE Governance & Administration Standards
Incident Notification, Investigation & Reporting
Employers must check the work conditions, Safety Data Sheets, Risk Assessment Reports and any
other relevant documentation for the presence of agents that are suspected of causing illnesses and
to verify the relationship between exposure and the resulting symptoms. In case of cancers and chronic
irreversible illnesses where incubation periods between exposure and onset of illness can be
protracted, the employer should gather medical evidence of the disease, job exposure and history and
seek a work relationship opinion. LHCP together with the Occupational Health Specialist (or equivalent)
will provide an opinion on work-relatedness following careful review.
All confirmed cases of occupational (i.e. work-related) illnesses/ diseases listed in ADNOC list of
Serious Occupational illnesses / Diseases (Appendix 2) shall be notified / reported to ADNOC as per
requirements of this Standard.
To have an ADNOC Group common approach occupational illnesses are classified under one of the
following categories as referred in ADNOC Health Screening & Surveillance Standard (HSE-OH-ST05)
1 Back problems / Lower Limb Disorders
2 Neck/Upper Limb Disorders
3 Noise Induced Hearing Loss
4 Respiratory diseases
5 Skin diseases
6 Heat related illness
7 Cancers and malignant blood diseases
8 Infectious and preventable diseases
9 Mental ill-health
10 Poisoning
11 Other
Note: Any lost workdays resulting from Occupational Illness / Disease (e.g. food poisoning, heat stroke
etc.) shall be recorded as days lost due to Occupational Illness [see definitions of Lost Workdays Case
and Lost Workdays]. Work related food poisoning cases are classified as Occupational Illnesses
(Infections/Preventable Disease) [see Section 7.8.2 and Appendix 2].
7.8.5. Restricted Workday Case (RWDC)
A “Restricted Workday Case” (RWDC) is defined as a work-related injury or occupational illness that,
on any calendar day after the day of the illness or injury, keeps (either physically or by way of an LHCP
recommended work limitation) an individual from:
 Performing one or more of their routine job functions (i.e., activities regularly performed at least
once per week) or
 Working the full workday that they would otherwise have been scheduled to work.
Note: If an LHCP recommends work limitations which the employer then confirms that it
constitutes a restriction, the incident should be recorded as a RWDC, even if:
- The individual is not scheduled to work on the next calendar day
- The Worker does not follow the limitations/restrictions.
 Vague Work Limitation Recommendation: For cases involving Employees, if a LHCP provides
a vague limitation recommendation (e.g., non-task specific instructions such as “engage in light
duty” or “take it easy for a week”, a site LHCP or OH Specialist should review the case further
and if deemed necessary follow-up with the LHCP to determine if the limitation prevents the
Worker from either performing their routine job functions or working their normal assigned work
shift. Record the case accordingly.
Standard No.: HSE-GA-ST04 Version No.: 1 Effective date: Dec., 2019 Page 57 of 184

ADNOC Classification: Internal
HSE Management System
HSE Governance & Administration Standards
Incident Notification, Investigation & Reporting
 Worker Productivity: Worker productivity is not a determinant of Restricted Work. Do not
record cases where the injured or ill Worker produces fewer goods or services than would have
produced prior to the injury or illness but otherwise performs all of the routine functions of their
work.
Refer to Appendix 6 for examples of reportable incidents
(a) Other Scenarios
If the employer cannot or chooses not to accommodate the LHCP’s recommended work limitations and
the:
 Worker is temporary transferred to a different position, record as a Restricted Workdays Case
 Worker’s normal work duties are transferred to another Worker, record as a Restricted
Workdays Case
7.8.6. Medical Treatment Case
Any work related injury or illness that involves neither lost work days nor restricted workdays but which
requires medical treatment beyond the first aid (See 7.8.8) by physician or other Licensed Health Care
Professional
Medical treatment means the management and care of a patient to combat disease or disorder which
does not include first aid treatment even though provided by a physician or other professional medical
personnel.
The following are a few examples of Medical Treatment Cases (not an exhaustive list):
a) Treatment of second or third degree burns (Refer to Section 6.2 in Appendix 6)
b) Use of prescription medications whether one time or multiple times [except when used solely for
diagnostic purposes (e.g., eye drops to dilate pupils)]
c) Using adrenaline auto injectors, for example EpiPen, even if pre-prescribed for the patient.
d) Any procedures wherein medical treatment does not have to be administered by a LHCP (Licensed
Health Care Professional) in emergency situations to be recordable. For example, when an
employee or other individual performs a Heimlich manoeuvre, CPR (Cardio-Pulmonary
Resuscitation) or use of AED (Automated External Defibrillator) equipment are all recordable
medical treatment.
e) Occupational exposure from workplace environmental conditions, hazards or contaminated objects
or body fluids associated with work followed by acute signs and symptoms of disease or infection
(within 24-72 hours) as evidenced by laboratory examination or diagnosed by a physician or other
licensed healthcare professional (LHCP) and supported by an applicable and validated workplace
surveys or hazard assessment report.
The following are not considered as Medical Treatment Cases:
a) Visits to a physician or other registered Medical Personnel solely for counselling.
b) Diagnostic procedures, such as x-rays and blood tests, including the administration of prescription
medications used solely for diagnostic purposes (e.g. eye drops to dilate pupils).
c) Pure precautionary administration of oxygen (where no signs or symptoms of a work related
exposure exists)
The following may not involve any treatment but for purpose of injury/illness severity classification the
following will be reported as a minimum Medical Treatment Case [MTC]:
a) If a worker loses consciousness as the result of a work-related exposure or injury
b) Significant diagnosed injuries and illnesses that are work-related cases. Examples include
fractured or cracked bone/teeth, or a punctured eardrum, etc.
Standard No.: HSE-GA-ST04 Version No.: 1 Effective date: Dec., 2019 Page 58 of 184

ADNOC Classification: Internal
HSE Management System
HSE Governance & Administration Standards
Incident Notification, Investigation & Reporting
c) All work-related needle-stick injuries and cuts from sharp objects that are contaminated with
another person's blood or other potentially infectious material. These injuries should be
investigated as HIPO to confirm any transmission of infection. Refer to Section 7.8.7.
Prescription Medication
A “Prescription Medication” is a drug which requires by law a medical prescription from a LHCP, most
typically a physician, to be dispensed. While prescription medications are typically powerful
substances, some specific drugs may be available in both prescription and non-prescription dosages.
For recording and reporting purposes:
a) The following constitute Medical Treatment, even if the Worker does not fill the prescription or
take the medication:
- Issuance of a prescription for a Prescription Medication by an LHCP
- Provision, by an LHCP, of Prescription Medication samples in lieu of a prescription
recommendation or script.
- Administration of a Prescription Medication by or at the direction of an LHCP for example
administering Intra Venous Fluids for treatment or prophylactic purpose
- Recommendation by an LHCP to use a non-prescription medication at prescription strength
dosage
- Self-administration of a Prescription Medication presc.ribed/dispensed to the individual
specifically for use in the circumstance encountered that, except for the exposure at work,
would not have been needed (e.g., use of prescribed Epi-pen to counter allergic reaction
following bee sting at work, use of asthma inhaler after exposure to catalyst dust)
b) The following do not constitute Medical Treatment:
- Personal decision to use a Prescription Medication without a LHCP recommendation
- Self-administration of a Prescription Medication for which the prescription was written to treat
conditions unrelated to events or exposures at work.
c) Special Case: International Waters – For a drug prescribed while in international waters, the
laws and regulations of the country of the flag of the rig/vessel (subject to the Dosage Exception
above) shall apply for determining if the drug is a Prescription Medication.
7.8.7. Penetration (SHARPS) Injuries
A penetration (sharps) injury is an incident, which causes a needle, blade (such as scalpel) or other
medical instruments to penetrate the skin. The main risk from a sharps injury is the potential exposure
to infections such as blood-borne viruses (BBV) including Hepatitis B (HBV), Hepatitis C (HCV) and
Human immunodeficiency virus (HIV). This can occur where the injury involves a sharp that is
contaminated with blood or a bodily fluid from a patient (such as needle-stick injury).
All penetration (needle stick) injuries shall be recorded.
When a person is injured by a sharp KNOWN to be contaminated with a blood borne virus
(BBV) e.g. Hepatitis B or C or HIV, this shall be reported as a HIPO (refer to Section 7.12)
When a person receives a sharps injury and a BBV acquired by this route sero converts. This
shall be reported as Disease, infections and ill health following the Reportable Incident
Notification & Reporting Process (refer to Section 7.14)
if the injury itself is so severe (3 and above), it shall be reported following the Reportable
Incident Notification & Reporting Process (refer to Section 7.14).
7.8.8. First Aid Case (FAC)
First Aid means if the treatment of the resultant work related injury / illness is limited to one or more of
the following list of 14 specific treatments, and will therefore constitute First Aid Case (Exhaustive List).
These are:
Standard No.: HSE-GA-ST04 Version No.: 1 Effective date: Dec., 2019 Page 59 of 184

ADNOC Classification: Internal
HSE Management System
HSE Governance & Administration Standards
Incident Notification, Investigation & Reporting
1. Using a non-prescription Medication at non-prescription strength (for medications available in both
prescription* and non-prescription form, a recommendation by a physician or other licensed health
care professional to use a non-prescription medication at prescription strength is considered
medical treatment);
2. Administering tetanus immunizations. (other immunizations such as Hepatitis B vaccine, anti-
rabies vaccines, are considered medical treatment);
3. Cleaning, flushing or soaking wounds on the surface of the skin;
4. Using wound coverings such as bandages, Band-Aids™, gauze pads, etc.; or using butterfly
bandages or Steri-Strips™ (other wound closing devices such as sutures, staples, etc. are
considered medical treatment);
5. Using hot or cold therapy;
6. Using any non-rigid means of support, such as elastic bandages, wraps, non-rigid back belts, etc.
(devices with rigid stays or other systems designed to immobilize parts of the body are considered
medical treatment);
7. Using temporary immobilization devices while transporting an incident victim (e.g., splints, slings,
neck collars, back boards, etc.);
8. Drilling of a fingernail or toenail to relieve pressure, or draining fluid from a blister;
9. Using eye patches;
10. Removing foreign bodies from the eye using only irrigation or a cotton swab;
11. Removing splinters or foreign material from areas other than the eye by irrigation, tweezers, cotton
swabs or other simple means;
12. Using finger guards;
13. Using massages (physical therapy or chiropractic treatment is considered medical treatment); or
14. Drinking fluids for relief of heat stress
*Refer to local health regulations for list of prescription medications
Note: Items listed as first aid treatment are always considered as first aid case even when these
treatments are provided by a physician or other licensed health care professional.
7.8.9. Contractors Incidents
(a) Incidents involving Contractors operating under “Contractual Mode 1 and 2” shall be notified,
investigated and reported to ADNOC Group HSE by the ADNOC Group as per requirements of
this Standard. Refer to Section 5 for definitions of Contractual Modes and Excluded Contractors.
(b) Contractual Mode 1: ADNOC HSEMS Applies:
(i) ADNOC Leads & Directs Work. HSE Performance Monitored & Reported to ADNOC.
(ii) The contractor provides people, processes and tools for the execution of the contract
under the supervision, instructions and HSEMS of ADNOC.
(iii) The contractor has a management system to provide assurance that the personnel for
whom it is responsible are qualified and fit for the work and that the processes, tools,
materials and equipment they provide are properly maintained and suitable.
(iv) Contractual Mode 1 typically applies to scopes of minor modification or maintenance/
turnaround work on ADNOC owned or operated site.
(c) Contractual Mode 2: Contractor HSEMS Applies:
(i) Contractor Leads & Directs Work. HSE Performance Monitored & Reported to ADNOC.
(ii) ADNOC reviews and approves the contractor’s HSEMS, ensuring that it is aligned with
ADNOC HSEMS requirements. The contractor executes all aspects of the contract under
its own HSEMS providing the necessary supervision and verifying the proper functioning
of its HSEMS.
Standard No.: HSE-GA-ST04 Version No.: 1 Effective date: Dec., 2019 Page 60 of 184

ADNOC Classification: Internal
HSE Management System
HSE Governance & Administration Standards
Incident Notification, Investigation & Reporting
(iii) ADNOC verifies and assures as per agreed contract terms and conditions the overall
effectiveness of the HSE management controls put in place by the contractor, including its
interface with subcontractors.
(iv) Contractual Mode 2 typically applies to scopes of work on sites owned/operated either by
ADNOC, contractor, or by other persons having no relationship with ADNOC or contractors.
The work can be fenced off on a separate site, like a drilling location or separate project
site. This also applies to ADNOC Turnkey Projects.
(v) Requirements for ADNOC Supervision and Operational Control on Contractors’ Facilities
/ Laydown Area Outside Company’s Fence on land provided by ADNOC etc. operating
under Contractual Mode 2 shall follow the Group Company’s monitoring and assurance
process per agreed Contract Terms & Conditions. Group Company shall update / establish
relevant procedures / practices and administration controls helpful in implementing
ADNOC requirements.
(d) Contractors providing services to multiple ADNOC Group Companies (but not to any external
clients) such as site dedicated to manufacture concrete structures for ADNOC Group shall fall
under “Contractual Mode 2”. Incident notification and reporting shall however be with the ADNOC
Function or Group Company which is the “contract holder”.
(e) Excluded Contractors: Incidents involving “Excluded Contractors” (as defined in Section 5) are
exempted for reporting in HSE Performance. Incidents with Severity 4 or above shall however
be recorded, notified, and investigated as per requirements of this Standard.
Exception: A normally-reportable Contractor is not reportable when at a contractor-controlled and
operated site, separate from the ADNOC worksite (Ring-Fenced), where ADNOC or its Project
Management Consultant (PMC) is not maintaining ANY supervision on a day-to-day basis. Incidents
including Injuries / Illness occurring to Contractors employees in established ring-fenced sites are
excluded for reporting in HSE Performance. Incidents shall however be recorded, notified, investigated
and reported as per requirements of this Standard.
This exception is binding only where Contract Owner can demonstrate validity through a documented
evidence for example defined Contract’s Terms & Conditions or other pertinent documentation. This
exemption however is not valid for Green Field and Brown Field Project where ADNOC is maintaining
supervision and control.
7.8.10. Occupational Injury / Illness Classification and Reclassification
(a) ADNOC Group Company / Function / Affiliate’s VP Corporate HSE or equivalent is responsible
and accountable for “Incident Classification” as per requirements of this Standard. This includes
establishing work relatedness, assigning severity and any change in classification, if required.
The VP Corporate HSE or equivalent shall maintain duly approved, auditable record of Incident
Classification establishing justification with all supporting documents. This includes incidents
involving Reportable Contractors.
(b) VP Corporate HSE or equivalent shall ensure conducting proper awareness for respective Line
Management including relevant employees, reportable Contractors and Licensed Health Care
Professionals (where possible) on the Incident Classification requirements for effective
implementation across ADNOC Group.
(c) If required, VP Corporate HSE or equivalent may initiate the “Conflicting Contemporaneous
Medical Reports Management Process” [Section 7.10] for Occupational Injuries / Illnesses as
follows:
(i) If the treating Licensed Health Care Practitioner (LHCP) recommends medical treatment
(for a work‐related injury or illness) and Lost Workdays or Restricted Workdays Case has
occurred, the case is recordable. VP Corporate HSE or equivalent shall therefore not
consider a second recommendation once medical treatment, days away from work or
restricted workdays case have taken place, even if the subsequent recommendation is
more authoritative.

Incident Notification, Investigation & Reporting
(ii) If second recommendation is sought within 24 hours from another Licensed Health Care
Practitioner, i.e. contemporaneous recommendations regarding medical treatment
obtained provided that no Lost Workdays or Restricted Workdays Case have occurred,
the VP Corporate HSE or equivalent may decide to implement the more authoritative (best
documented, best reasoned or most persuasive) and update the classification accordingly.
(d) All associated resources and expenses required for obtaining the second opinion as deemed
necessary for Occupational injury /illnesses shall be the responsibility of respective Group
Company / Affiliate.
(e) ADNOC Group HSE Function shall implement an assurance process for verifying Incident
Classification in compliance with this Standard. This may include reviewing the GCs/Affiliates
incident management process including establishing work relatedness, assigned incident
severity and classification, with justification and all supporting documents. Verification (adhoc
and / or periodic) of Incident Classification shall be as per the direction of SVP Group HSE
Function.
(f) Any conflict in the incident classification shall be reported to the respective Group Company
/Affiliate’s Corporate VP HSE [or equivalent] and SVP ADNOC Group HSE Function.
(g) SVP ADNOC Group HSE Function may assign an independent team to review such cases and
provide recommendations for reclassification along with justification, reports, supporting
documents, etc.
(h) SVP ADNOC Group HSE Function is the final authority for reclassification of Incidents in ADNOC.
The decision made by SVP Group HSE Function on incidents’ reclassification shall be final and
not subject to any further review, with the exception of cases in which injury / illness classification
(severity) may change due to escalation such as LWDC to PPD / Fatality.
(i) Reclassification shall be communicated to all relevant stakeholders including ADNOC
Performance and Planning Team and respective VP Corporate HSE for necessary actions
including updating HSE Performance.
7.9. MEDICAL REMOVAL CRITERIA
“Medical Removal” occurs in situations where the worker must be removed from work environment
exposure to certain substances (health hazards) because an exposure criterion has been met or
exceeded as established by LHCP / OH SME. Medical Removal shall be recorded as either a case
involving days away from work or a case involving restricted work activity, depending on how to comply
with the medical removal requirement. (Note: Cases involving voluntary removal before meeting these
criteria do not need to be recorded.)
Examples of criteria for work-related medical removal includes but not limited to:
1. Lead: 50 ug/dL Blood Lead Level
2. Methylene Chloride – LHCP recommends removal (from work exposure) because the Worker’s
exposure to Methylene Chloride may contribute to or aggravate the Worker’s existing cardiac,
hepatic, neurological (including stroke), or skin disease.
3. Formaldehyde – LHCP finds that significant irritation of the mucosa of the eyes or of the upper
airways, respiratory sensitization, dermal irritation, or dermal sensitization result from workplace
formaldehyde exposure and recommends limitations or removal from occupational exposure to
formaldehyde.
4. Benzene – Decision by LHCP and hematologist/internist to remove Worker whose:
- Hemoglobin level or hematocrit falls below normal limit OR
- Thrombocyte (platelet) count varies more than 20% below Worker’s most recent values
OR
- Leukocyte count is below 4000 per mm3 or there is an abnormal differential count.

7.10. MANAGING THE CONFLICTING CONTEMPORANEOUS MEDICAL
RECOMMENDATIONS (CCMR)
When two or more “conflicting medical recommendations” (i.e., recommendations that would result in
different recordkeeping classifications) are received, the case is to be recorded, either:
 At the highest level of recordability among the treatment recommendations – OR –
 If applicable, according to “most authoritative (best documented, best reasoned or most
persuasive)” medical treatment recommendation as determined following the Conflicting
Contemporaneous Medical Recommendations (CCMR) Review, which in some cases can
result in a lower treatment classification.
 The following conditions must be met before the CCMR process can be applied:
- The treatment recommendations are "contemporaneous" (i.e., made close in time and at
the same stage of clinical development and same severity of the condition)
- Medical Treatment, Restricted Work or Lost Time has not occurred before both treatment
recommendations are given.
Exception: There is one situation, applicable only to cases with a recommendation for Medical
Treatment, where the CCMR Process can still be considered even though Medical Treatment has been
given. Specifically, if the “more authoritative” LHCP determines that no medical treatment is needed,
then the case is not recordable, even if a second (less authoritative) LHCP subsequently sees the
patient and prescribes Medical Treatment, providing that the two recommendations being considered
are contemporaneous.
Refer to Section 7.8.10 for Occupational Injuries / Illness Classification and Reclassification
7.10.1. CCMR Process
The key elements of the CCMR Process are as follows. Refer to Appendix 8 for examples on CCMR
Process implementation.
 To initiate the review process, the OH Specialist (or equivalent) within respective GC should
fill out the CCMR Report for the Incident Owner, gather appropriate documentation, and
forward to his / her respective HSE Management (VP or above) for review.
 The determination of which medical recommendation is “most authoritative” is made with key
input from employees visited hospital / clinic.
- Determination typically involves review of the nature and background of the injury or
illness, the nature of the medical examinations involved, as well as reviews of the LHCPs
making the recommendations (e.g., medical credentials, medical specialties, the area of
practice, expertise).
- Determination of “more authoritative” must be independent and so cannot be made by
LHCP that made one of the recommendations.
- A critique of another LHCP’s medical recommendation does not represent a valid medical
treatment recommendation (i.e., will not be considered “most authoritative”).
 The outcome and supporting rationale should be reviewed and validated by the respective
ADNOC GC / Affiliate OH Specialist before finalizing the classification recommendation.
- In case of a conflict in agreement on the recommendation, the Corporate VP HSE or
equivalent shall be consulted.
7.11. INJURED PERSON CASE MANAGEMENT
The respective Executive Management across ADNOC Group must ensure establishing a process to
ensure that injured / ill person will be medically managed and the outcome of such treatment to be
classified in timely manner. The respective senior management (VPs and above) must appoint
responsible parties to supervise and oversee this process. Refer to ADNOC Case Management &
Rehabilitation Standard (HSE-OH-ST04).

7.12. REPORTING THE HIGH POTENTIAL EVENTS (HIPO)
HSE event, sudden and undesirable with a potential severity 4 or above is considered to be a High
Potential Event (HIPO). Refer to Appendix 4 for examples on HIPO events.
In the High Potential Events’ reporting, the actual and potential consequence of a work-related event
(incident) in the Work Environment is assessed according to ADNOC Risk Matrix.
Assessment of both the Actual Severity Level (ASL) and the Potential Severity Level (PSL) of events,
helps in understanding and preventing future occurrences of any event that did or had the potential to
have a higher consequence outcome.
A Safety event with a Potential Severity Level of “4” (PSL 4) or higher is considered to be a Reportable
High Potential Event (HIPO).
The process steps for determining Potential Severity Level are as follows (see figure 7.12.1).
 Determine Actual Severity Level (ASL) – Assess the Severity for actual consequences using
ADNOC Risk Matrix (Appendix 1);
- The final Severity level is the highest result obtained in all types of applicable consequences
(e.g. HSE & Reputation).
- If the individual sustained multiple injuries, assess based on the most severe injury.
 Determine Potential Severity, Worst Case (PSLwc) – Use the Actual Severity Level Scenarios
to evaluate the expected consequence from a reasonable scenario similar to the actual event but
in which no barriers/safeguards or pre-event mitigations were in place.
 Analyze Barriers/Safeguards associated with the event:
- ‘Effective Barriers’ describes any controls / barriers / safeguards / pre-event mitigations that
were in place prior to the event occurring AND were effective in reducing the Potential Severity
Level or eliminated the potential for an event to occur.
- ‘Barriers Less than Adequate’ describes any barriers / safeguards / pre-event mitigations that
could have, but did not, mitigate the Potential Severity Level, Worst Case. This should include:
o Barriers not in place prior to the event and/or
o Barriers that were in place but were ineffective in reducing the Potential Severity Level
Note: The ‘Barriers Less Than Adequate’ should be addressed in the Corrective Actions to prevent
reoccurrence and / or mitigate future events.
 Develop a Potential Severity Level Rationale – Develop and document the basis for
determination of the Potential Severity Level, Final, including the reasonable scenario used to
select the PSLwc, why the assessed PSLwc was selected, and how the Effective Barriers reduced
the potential event consequence Severity.
 Determine the Potential Severity Level, Final (PSLf) – Considering the PSLwc, the Effective
Barriers in place along with the selected reasonable scenario, use the Actual Severity Level
Scenarios to determine the level of the highest Severity event that could have reasonably occurred
during the event.
- PSLf is a measure of the risk exposure of the event. PSLf is determined only once for an event,
even if multiple Workers were harmed in the event.
- Note: The focus in the PSL determination process is the risk identification and mitigation related
to specific events. Judgment can be applied but GCs to retain a level of accuracy on how best
to apply the approach in efforts to assess and appropriately record.
Standard No.: HSE-GA-ST04 Version No.: 1 Effective date: Dec., 2019 Page 64 of 184

ADNOC Classification: Internal
HSE Management System
HSE Governance & Administration Standards
Incident Notification, Investigation & Reporting
Example:
The following example is given to demonstrate the general thought processes in determining Actual and
Potential Severity Levels. Appendix 4 also provides a non-exhaustive list of High Potential Events.
Overturning of Lifting Equipment – During the lifting activity the crane overturned due to inadequate
implementation of lifting plan. There was no injury to Crane operator and nearby workers narrowly
escaped getting hit by the falling crane and the load. Incident didn’t result in any injury, illness or incur
significant cost for crane repair.
- Actual Severity Level (ASL): 1. No High Severity Consequence (HSE, Financial or
Reputation).
- Potential Severity Level, worst case (PSLwc): 4. Single worker fatality / permanent total
disability could have occurred potentially.
- Barriers:
o Effective Barriers: No barriers, pre-event barriers were in place that mitigated or
eliminated the PSL. Only random chance (workers’ quick action) prevented the worst
case scenario from occurring.
o Barriers Less than Adequate: Barriers that could have, but didn’t, mitigate the PSL
include adequate implementation of lifting plan, barricading lifting area, and/or pre-job
hazard analysis.
- PSL Rationale: Had the trajectory of the crane fall been a little different, it could have resulted
in the Crane Operator getting injured or the nearby worker getting struck by the crane or even
the asset damage could have been significant. Thus, having a higher Potential Severity as there
were no effective barriers to reduce Severity for the worst cases. Thus, PSLwc = 4 (Single
Fatality). There were no effective barriers to reduce potential for Severity versus the worst case.
- Potential Severity Level, Final (PSLf): 4

7.13. HURT FREE APPROACH REPORTING
The “Hurt Free Approach to Personnel Safety and Health” is intended to enable the persistent
pursuit of an injury and illness free workplace and aligns with ADNOC core values to help promote the
desired culture of 100% safety. The Hurt Free Approach looks at both the actual injury and illness
consequence that resulted from an event as well as the potential consequence that might have
occurred if circumstances had been slightly different. A Safety event with an Actual or Potential Hurt
Level of “3” (i.e., an injury from which the individual would never fully recover) or higher is considered
to be a “Reportable High Potential Event”.
The Hurt Free Reporting is not mandatory however incident owners are encouraged to implement /
practice the requirements for injuries / illnesses involving Employees and Reportable Contractors (as
applicable) consistent with the HIPO reporting.
An overview of the Hurt Free Approach is provided in Sections 7.13.1, and Section 7.13.2 covers the
process for Hurt Level determination.
Figure 7.13.2 provides process for determining Hurt Level. Table 7.13.2 provides Matrix for determining
Potential & Actual Hurt Level for Injuries and Illnesses. Section 7.13.3 details the reporting
requirements for the Hurt Free Approach, while Section 7.13.4 provides examples of the application of
the approach.
7.13.1. OVERVIEW
In the Hurt Free Approach, the actual and potential consequence of a safety or health work related
event in the Work Environment is assessed according to six levels of injury severity, which includes
consideration of the duration of the effect. As indicated in Figure 7.13.1, these levels include Level 0
(No Hurt, e.g., a near miss), Levels 1 and 2 (where a full recovery might be expected or only modest
impairment occurs), Level 3 (involving life altering injuries with little potential for full recovery), and
Levels 4 and 5 (involving one or more fatalities).

By looking at both the Actual Hurt Level (AHL) and the Potential Hurt Level (PHL) of events, the Hurt
Free Approach facilitates analyses in understanding and preventing future occurrences of any event
that did or had the potential to have a higher consequence outcome.
A Safety event with an Actual or Potential Hurt Level of “3” (AHL 3 or PHL 3, i.e., an injury from which
the individual would never fully recover) or higher is considered to be a Reportable High Potential Event.
Refer to Table 7.13.2 for Hurt Level Determination Matrix
7.13.2. HURT Level Determination
(a) Key Principles
To ensure integrity of approach and positive messaging to the workforce in determining Hurt Levels:
 Work the AHL and PHL assessments downward from the top of the scale until a reasonable/credible
severity level is selected.
 When there is a range of reasonable severity levels, select the higher level. This approach is used
because underreporting the level of the event may:
- Deprive the Company of important learnings – It is the recognition of those learnings and our
resulting actions that will eventually eliminate higher consequence events.
- Send mixed messages to the workforce, undermining our effort to foster the culture of caring
that will drive safety success.
(b) Process for determining HURT Levels
The process steps for determining Actual Hurt Level and Potential Hurt Level are as follows (see figure
7.13.2):
 Determine Actual Hurt Level (AHL) – Assess the actual physical body damage against the scale
found in the Actual Hurt Level Examples Table (7.13.1) and Table (7.13.2) Matrix for Determining
the Hurt Level.
- If the individual sustained multiple injuries, assess based on the most severe injury.
 Determine Potential Hurt, Worst Case (PHLwc) – Use the Actual Hurt Level Scenarios to evaluate
the expected consequence from a reasonable scenario similar to the actual event but in which no
barriers/safeguards or pre-event mitigations were in place.
Standard No.: HSE-GA-ST04 Version No.: 1 Effective date: Dec., 2019 Page 68 of 184

ADNOC Classification: Internal
HSE Management System
HSE Governance & Administration Standards
Incident Notification, Investigation & Reporting
 Analyze Barriers/Safeguards associated with the event:
- ‘Effective Barriers’ describes any controls / barriers / safeguards / pre-event mitigations that
were in place prior to the event occurring AND were effective in reducing the Potential Hurt
Level or eliminated the potential for an event to occur.
- ‘Barriers Less than Adequate’ describes any barriers / safeguards / pre-event mitigations that
could have, but did not, mitigate the Potential Hurt Level, Worst Case. This should include:
o Barriers not in place prior to the event and/or
o Barriers that were in place but were ineffective in reducing the Potential Hurt Level
Note: The ‘Barriers Less Than Adequate’ should be addressed in the Corrective Actions to prevent
reoccurrence and / or mitigate future events.
 Develop a Potential Hurt Level Rationale – Develop and document the basis for determination of
the Potential Hurt Level, Final, including the reasonable scenario used to select the PHLwc, why
the assessed PHLwc was selected, and how the Effective Barriers reduced the potential event
consequence Hurt.
 Determine the Potential Hurt Level, Final (PHLf) – Considering the PHLwc, the Effective Barriers
in place along with the selected reasonable scenario, use the Actual Hurt Level Scenarios to
determine the level of the highest Hurt event that could have reasonably occurred during the event.
- PHLf is a measure of the risk exposure of the event. PHLf is determined only once for an event,
even if multiple Workers were harmed in the event.
- Note: The focus in the PHL determination process is the risk identification and mitigation related
to specific events. Judgment can be applied but GCs to retain a level of accuracy on how best
to apply the approach in efforts to assess and appropriately record.
Examples:
The following examples are offered to demonstrate the general thought processes in determining Actual
and Potential Hurt Levels.
 Example 1: Fall from Height – Operations required running hoses through an open hatch. A worker
fell through open hatch 20 feet to the deck below, but the fall was slowed when the Worker’s clothing
hung on adjacent wellhead. Worker sustained minor bruising and scratches and a cut on shin that
required one stitch. Worker was released after Medical Treatment without recommended limitations.
- Actual Hurt Level (AHL): 1. A minor cut and bruises were sustained.
- Potential Hurt Level, worst case (PHLwc): 4. A Single Fatality could feasibly and reasonably
have occurred.
- Barriers:
o Effective Barriers: No barriers, pre-event barriers were in place that mitigated or
eliminated the PHL. Only random chance (clothing catching) prevented the worst case
scenario from occurring.
o Barriers Less than Adequate: Barriers that could have, but didn’t, mitigate the PHL
include dedicated observer, barricade around opening, and/or pre-job hazard analysis.
- PHL Rationale: Had the trajectory of the fall been shifted a few inches, the clothing would not
have broken the fall. Falls from heights above 10 feet are often fatal, but the likelihood of more
than one person falling is remote. Thus, PHLwc = 4 (Single Fatality). There were no effective
barriers to reduce potential for Hurt versus the worst case.
- Potential Hurt Level, Final (PHLf): 4
 Example 2: Repetitive Strain – A Worker started feeling discomfort in their wrists following
significant laptop usage. The Worker reported the issue to the supervisor, who had the Ergonomics
Standard No.: HSE-GA-ST04 Version No.: 1 Effective date: Dec., 2019 Page 69 of 184

ADNOC Classification: Internal
HSE Management System
HSE Governance & Administration Standards
Incident Notification, Investigation & Reporting
Contact perform an assessment. The Worker consulted LHCP, who recommended that the Worker
monitor symptoms closely.
- Actual Hurt Level (AHL): 0 (No Hurt). There is no evidence of physical body damage based on
lack of LHCP recommendation for treatment or work limitations.
- Potential Hurt Level, worst case (PHLwc): 3. A chronic illness such as a Musculoskeletal
disorder (MSD) or Repetitive Strain Injury (RSI, often referred to as “ergonomics incident”) can
escalate to become life-altering if not properly managed.
- Barriers:
o Effective Barriers: Timely reporting, early detection ergonomics management program,
post-event medical response
o Barriers Less than Adequate: None – all barriers and pre-event mitigations appear to be
effective.
- PHL Rationale: If not managed, an MSD can progress to become a prolonged, debilitating chronic
illness. Hence, PHLwc = 3. Ergonomic management program and post-event medical response
represent Effective Barriers that limit the potential for Hurt versus the worst case.
- Potential Hurt Level, Final (PHLf): 1 Considering Effective Barriers, it is reasonable to expect
any escalation to be maintained within one level.

7.14. REPORTABLE INCIDENT NOTIFICATION
All reportable incidents (actual Severity 3 or above OR Potential Severity 4 or above) must be notified to
ADNOC as soon as it is practical [verbal within two (2) hours], dependent on the situation but nominally
immediately. This shall include but not limited to:
 Fatality or NAD (Section 7.8.1, 7.14.4, & 7.18)
 Lost Workdays Cases (more than 7 Days – Section 7.8.2 & 7.14.4)
 Permanent Total or Permanent Partial Disability (Section 7.8.3 & 7.14.4)
 Inpatient Hospitalization of 24 hours or more for care or treatment (Section 7.14.4)
 Confirmed Cases of Serious Occupational Disease or Illness (Appendix 2) (Section 7.14.5)
 HIPO with Potential Severity 4 or Higher (Section 7.12)
 Assets / Property Damage
 Environmental Incidents (Section 7.14.6)
 Loss of Well Control Incidents (Section 7.14.7)
 Tier 1 & Tier 2 Process Safety Event (LOPC) irrespective of Severity (Section 7.14.8)
 Fire incidents within Process Facilities / Area including Flame, Smoldering and Smoke
irrespective of Severity (Section 7.14.9)
 Motor Vehicle Crash (MVC) Incidents (7.14.10)
 Incidents involving Reputational Impacts
The notification timeline vary based on the magnitude of the consequence / severity of the incident as
per ADNOC Risk Matrix (Appendix 1). Where an incident results in more than one consequence
categories, the overall severity ranking will be determined by the consequence category with the highest
level of severity. Refer to Section 7.6 for Severity Determination and Section 7.8 for Classification of
Injuries and Illnesses.
Actual severity requires evaluation of the actual consequences (observed and/or measured). Potential
severity considers and evaluates the likely additional consequences in the general context of the event.
The potential severity level is always greater than or equal to the actual severity level.
All consequences of an incident shall be reported. If an incident with Occupational Safety & Health
consequences also results in other consequences with respect to Process Safety, and/or the
Environment, those other consequences shall also be reported.
7.14.1. Reportable Incident Notification Responsibility
The reportable incidents notification(s) shall be reviewed by the line management, approved by the
incident owner and reviewed by Corporate HSE or relevant ADNOC Functions before submitting to
ADNOC however, this shall not delay the notification and reporting process as stipulated in this Standard.
ADNOC Group Company / Function shall assign the Signatories / Responsible Personnel for sending out
Initial Incident Notification and Preliminary Incident Notification to ADNOC.
In case of flaring incident involving two or more Group Companies, the Incident Owner will be the Group
Company that caused the incident. Incident owner Group Company may request the impacted Group
Company (flaring site) to notify the incident on their behalf to ADNOC Group HSE
7.14.2. Initial Incident Notification (Within 2 hours)
When an ADNOC Group Company / Function experiences an incident with actual Severity 3 or above
(where Severity 3 can be established within 2 hours), they must notify ADNOC Group HSE immediately
or within Two (2) hours of identification of such occurrence through verbal communication (phone call,
text, email) with a brief summary of available information.
Standard No.: HSE-GA-ST04 Version No.: 1 Effective date: Dec., 2019 Page 74 of 184

ADNOC Classification: Internal
HSE Management System
HSE Governance & Administration Standards
Incident Notification, Investigation & Reporting
If Severity 3 cannot be established within 2 hours, the incident must be notified within 2 hours with
assumed Severity which can be validated during 24 hours notification process (7.14.3.)
Two (2) hours verbal notification shall include but not limited to the following incidents:
 Fatality or NAD
 Permanent Total Disability or Permanent Partial Disability
 Tier 1 & Tier 2 Process Safety Event (LOPC) (irrespective of Severity)
 Assets / Property Damage with Actual Severity 3 or Higher
 Environmental Incidents as per Section 7.14.6 (irrespective of Severity)
 Loss of Well Control Incidents as per Section 7.14.7
 Fire incidents within Process Facilities / Area including Open Flame, Smoldering and Smoke
(irrespective of severity level)
 Motor Vehicle Crash Incident as per Section 7.14.10.
 Incidents involving Reputational Impact
7.14.3. Preliminary Incident Notification (Within 24 hours)
ADNOC Group Company / Function shall notify ADNOC within 24 hours of identification / confirmation of
the following using the “Preliminary Incident Notification (24 hrs.)” Template FM-GAST04-01
Preliminary Notification shall include but not limited to the following incidents:
 Fatality or NAD
 Permanent Total or Permanent Partial Disability
 Lost Workdays Cases (more than 7 days)
 Inpatient Hospitalization of 24 hours or more for care or treatment
 Confirmed Cases of Serious Occupational Disease or Illness (Appendix 2)
 High Potential Events (HIPO) with Potential Severity 4 or Higher
 Tier 1 & Tier 2 Process Safety Event (LOPC) irrespective of Severity
 Assets / Property Damage with Actual Severity 3 or Higher
 Environmental Incidents as per Section 7.14.6 (irrespective of Severity)
 Loss of Well Control Incidents as per Section 7.14.7
 Fire incidents within Process Facilities / Area including open flame, smoldering and smoke
(irrespective of severity level)
 Motor Vehicle Crash Incident as per Section 7.14.10.
 Incidents involving Reputational Impacts
When doing a preliminary analysis of an incident, the incident owner can update the incident with accurate
details, consequence details etc. available at that time. The notification shall be sent to ADNOC Control
Room and ADNOC Group HSE.

7.14.4. Health & Safety Incidents (Occupational Injuries / Illnesses) Notification
Occupational Injuries / Illnesses shall be notified and reported based on the below Severity Matrix

7.14.5. Occupational Illness or Disease Notification & Reporting
A case of occupational or work related injury shall be clearly distinguished from a case of occupational
illness or disease. This is determined by the nature of the original event or exposure that caused the
case, not by the resulting condition of the affected employee.
The identification of illness should be made by a Licensed Health Care Professional. Cases that are
difficult to determine may require evaluation by medical specialists or consultants. Nevertheless, it is not
always possible to establish beyond all doubt whether an illness is occupational or not.
Initial notification of suspected / identified / confirmed Serious Occupational Illnesses / Diseases
(Appendix 2) classified as a 'reportable incident' shall be submitted to ADNOC immediately after the
illness / disease incurred as per requirements of this standard i.e. verbally in two (2) hours and using the
Preliminary Incident Notification Form (FM-GAST04-01) within twenty four (24) hrs.
An update on the confirmation of Occupational illnesses/diseases shall be reported to ADNOC within 7
days as per the requirements of this Standard by using the Preliminary Investigation Report Form (FM-
GAST04-03) along with Initial Injury Illness Report (FM-GAST04-05) and Occupational Injury & Illness
Classification Report (FM-GAST04-06)

Incident Notification, Investigation & Reporting
(a) Identification of Suspected Occupational Illness / Disease Case
 Suspected occupational disease case shall be identified by Group Company’s medical team by
reviewing the employee medical records along with supporting data from employees’ exposure
monitoring.
 Suspected cases of occupational disease can also be identified based on health complaints from
employees.
 The Medical team, Industrial Hygienist / OH Engineer / OH Specialist shall jointly review the case
and decide on additional data requirement.
(b) Assessment of Suspected Occupational Disease / Illness Case
Group Company investigating a suspected Occupational Illness / Disease case shall compile and submit
supporting exposure monitoring data to assist the investigation of attributability of the suspected case
and evaluate cause and effect relationship.
(c) Supporting Data Related to Work Conditions and Exposure Monitoring
The supporting data may include but not be limited to the following:
 Occupational Health Risk Assessment for the employee activities.
 Area monitoring data on OH hazard responsible for the suspected Occupational illness.
 Personal Dose Monitoring data for the employee or his SEG on the concerned OH hazard.
 Related observation from other reports like on incident investigation, near miss, inspection or
area audits providing details on employee exposure pattern to the OH hazard.
 Available or established control measures in the workplace and their effectiveness in controlling
OH hazard.
 Data on job responsibilities, work schedules, attendance records.
 Occupational Exposure Limits (OEL) established for the concerned OH hazard and SOPs
established for the employee activities involving the hazard.
 Other regulatory compliance records.
Based upon the above submitted data the Group Company medical team and OH/IH team shall assess
the suspected case for conducting a formal investigation to determine work-relatedness.
Availability of an updated OH Risk Assessment along with supporting exposure monitoring data is
required to investigate a suspected Occupational disease case. Refer to ADNOC Occupational Health
Framework (HSE-OH-ST01).
In the absence of such data the confirmation or otherwise of the occupational disease case will be based
upon equivalent data (e.g. SEG) and evidence-based best judgement by the investigation team/OH
committee or equivalent.
(d) Employee Medical Records
The Group Company / Incident Owner shall maintain reliable and updated employee OH & Medical
records and develop a process to define secured access to all or limited data for regular review,
Occupational disease identification and subsequent assessment or investigations. The employees’
medical records along with OH risk assessment data are critical in establishing and confirming an
Occupational disease case.
The OH & Medical records that will be required will include but not be limited to:
 Medical history of employee prior to joining the company
 Pre-employment medical check-up data
 Periodic medical Check-up data
 Health surveillance (Risk Based) records
 Sickness absence and medical records during the working tenure with the GC

(e) Notification and Reporting the Confirmed Occupational Disease Case
 Based on the review of the employees’ health data or health reports, a team of OH / IH from the
Group Company / Incident Owner will take up specific cases, as suspected Occupational
Disease Cases for further investigation, confirmation and subsequent reporting to ADNOC.
 The Initial Injury Illness Report (FM-GAST04-05) and Occupational Injury & Illness Classification
Report (FM-GAST04-06) shall be submitted to ADNOC Group HSE along with Preliminary
Investigation Report (FM-GAST04-03) within 7 days.
 Group Company’s OH/IH expert team shall investigate occupational disease/illness cases for
confirming work relatedness, considering but not limited to supporting IH monitoring data as
against the OEL, exposure frequency and duration, work related data and medical record for the
SEG or for that particular employee.
 Occupational Illness Investigation Report (FM-GAST04-08) along with the “Final Investigation
Report” (FM-GASTO4-07) shall be submitted to ADNOC within forty [40] days of initial
notification.
ADNOC Group HSE will codify the reported confirmed Occupational Diseases as per ADNOC List of
Occupational Disease in accordance with the requirements of ADNOC Health Screening & Surveillance
Standard (HSE-OH-ST05).
7.14.6. Environmental Incidents Notification
Environmental protection is a particularly sensitive issue in the UAE (and everywhere in the world) and
the media may report on an incident without contacting ADNOC or the Group Company in which an
environmental incident has occurred. It is therefore crucial that an accurate, timely and well-
implemented reporting system is in place with which ADNOC management can prepare for such events
with an appropriate corporate response to third parties, if required. The environmental incidents listed
in Table 7.14.2 shall be notified to ADNOC within Two (2) Hours (verbal) / Twenty Four (24) hours
from their identification.

Table 7.14.3 Flaring Severity Matrix
Flared Gas Volume Mass in Tons of
Severity Severity Category
(MMCF*/ Event) Flared Gas per Event
> 100 MMSCF > 30000 T Disastrous 6
50 – 100 MSCF 1500 T – 30000 T Catastrophic 5
10 – 50 MMSCF 300 T – 1500 T Major 4
5 -10 MMSCF 150 T – 300 T Serious 3
1 -5 MMSCF 30 T – 150 T Minor 2
Less than 1 MMSCF < 30 T Notable 1
*MMSCF: Million Standard Cubic Feet
Notes:
1. Non-routine flaring involves operations which results in high emissions, large scale emission of smoke,
high noise levels or other unusual features; any incident where complaints could arise from regulatory
bodies or others.
2. All non-routine flaring with Severity 3 or above (as per Flaring Severity Matrix Table 7.14.3) shall be
notified to ADNOC within 2 hours (verbal) / 24-hours (FM-GAST04-01) with the associated Severity
and estimation of flared volume (MMSCF).
3. The flaring volume with severity category “Notable” or below does not require notification to ADNOC
however shall be recorded and investigated by the Group Companies.
4. The threshold volumetric and mass limit for reporting has been defined for each flaring event.
However, it can be noted that one event can have more than one source of release.
5. Density of 1 kg/m3 is assumed for flared gas to calculate the gas weight in Mass basis.
6. Flaring Incident Investigation and Root Cause Analysis shall be completed within 7 days of Incident
and Reported to ADNOC using the “Flaring Incidents Investigation & Root Cause Analysis Report
(FM-GAST04-10)”
7. ADNOC at their discretion may ask the Group Company for detailed investigation of any flaring incident
causing environmental pollution and raising public concern as per the requirements of this Standard.
8. In case of flaring incident involving two or more Group Companies, the Incident Owner will be the
Group Company that caused the incident. Incident owner Group Company may request the
impacted Group Company (flaring site) to notify the incident on their behalf to ADNOC Group HSE.
9. Any planned / routine flaring causing Environmental Pollution and raising public concern etc. shall
also be notified to ADNOC using the Preliminary Incident Notification Form [FM-GAST04-01].
10. Group Companies shall establish and implement the communication protocol for managing flaring
events that remain active beyond 24 hours. This shall include communication of such scenarios to
concerned management in the respective ADNOC Directorates and obtaining approval for required
actions for example continuing flaring beyond certain hours, gradually reducing plant load, plant
shut down, etc. ADNOC Group HSE shall be notified as per the requirements of this Standard within
2 hours / 24 hours and once the flaring stopped along with all relevant details including, total flared
volume (MMSCF) per event, reasons and justification.
11. If the flaring continues for more than 2 hours, the final (updated) notification shall be submitted to
ADNOC at the end of the event (within 24-hours or full duration of the event, whichever is applicable)
with the total flared volume (MMSCF) per event.
12. All flaring events shall be reported to ADNOC in accordance with ADNOC HSE Performance
Monitoring & Reporting Standard (HSE-GA-ST08).

7.14.8. Process Safety Event Notification
Tier 1 and Tier 2 Process Safety Events (LOPC) shall be notified to ADNOC within 2 hours (verbally)
followed by the preliminary notification (24 hours [FM-GAST04-01) as per the consequences for
establishing the Tier level in accordance with API RP 754. Where applicable, all consequences as per
ADNOC Risk Matrix (Appendix 1) associated with Process Safety Event for example Injury, Fatality, Asset
Damage, etc. shall be notified along with Process Safety Event. Refer to Appendix 6 for examples of
Process Safety Events.
7.14.9. Fire Incident Notification
All fire incidents (open flame, smoldering, smoke, etc.) occurring within the Process Facilities/Areas
including Electrical Sub-Stations and Project Sites involving equipment, system, LOPC, etc. shall be
notified to ADNOC verbally within 2 hours (irrespective of Severity).
All other fire incidents shall follow the notification and reporting requirements as per the severity level.
Refer to ADNOC Fire & Explosion Risk Assessment Standard (HSE-RM-ST09).

7.15. INCIDENT INVESTIGATION
Incidents investigation shall be initiated as soon as the incident is identified and notified to ADNOC. The
investigation process must contain as minimum the following steps:
 Determine incident ownership and issue Terms of Reference – ToR (FM-GAST04-02) within 48
hours of incident
 Assign Investigation team with clear objectives within 48 hours of incident
 Evaluation of initial site response, incident assessment, incident notification and necessary
evidences
 Perform Investigation by identifying sequence of events and the timeline
 Issue Preliminary Investigation Report (7 days)
 Issue Final Investigation Report (40 days)
 Identify Corrective Actions
 Approve assignment of actions
 Conduct validation sessions on implementation of actions arising from the investigation
 Tracking, follow-up and close out
 Share Lessons Learned from Incidents along with Final Investigation Report
Note: While investigating the Injuries and Illnesses the Actual and Potential Hurt Level should also be
evaluated as per Section 7.13
Refer to Section 7.18 for Non Accidental Death (NAD) Investigation
7.15.1. Determining Incident Ownership
The level of incident ownership shall be determined based on the severity ranking of the incident as
defined below and explained in subsequent sections.
Severity Level Incident Owner
Actual 5 or 6 / Potential 6 ADNOC GC Executive Management
Actual 4 / Potential 5 ADNOC GC Executive Management
Actual 3 / Potential 4 ADNOC GC Site Management
Actual 1,2 / Potential 1,2,3 ADNOC GC Site Management
Severity Level Incident Owner (ADNOC HQ or Group Companies HQ)
Actual 5 or 6 / Potential 6 Director / GC Executive Management
Actual 4 / Potential 5 Function Manager / GC Executive Management
Actual 3 / Potential 4 Division Manager or Equivalent
Actual 1,2 / Potential 1,2,3 Division Manager or Equivalent

ADNOC Classification: Internal
HSE Management System
HSE Governance & Administration Standards
Incident Notification, Investigation & Reporting
7.15.2. Determining Incident Ownership between Multiple Group Companies
Incident Ownership:
The Group Company who is the site owner accountable for managing a specific work location,
equipment, activity / operation or person(s) involved in an incident. The site owner where the incident
occurred is required to conduct the incident management, notification, investigation and reporting.
Where an incident in one Group Company impacts on another Group Company, the Group Company
having the ownership and overall control of the site or operation where the incident occurred shall be
the incident owner and must report the incident to ADNOC.
Where one Group Company is providing a service to another Group Company, the Group Company in
overall control must report the incident to ADNOC. For example, if an incident occurs on an ADNOC
Drilling rig while it is working for ADNOC Onshore, ADNOC Onshore must report the incident to ADNOC.
Other cases where ADNOC Onshore is not in control of the operation activity i.e. work has been
suspended by ADNOC Onshore and ADNOC Drilling is working under its own HSEMS, ADNOC Drilling
must report the incident to ADNOC.
When a Group Company is providing services to another Group Company as part of resource
optimization, the GC where the activity is being performed shall be the incident owner. For example, if
an incident occurs at a GC involving Ruwais Site Integration (RSI), the Group Company where the
incident occurred must report the incident to ADNOC.
Incident Owner in all such cases shall be the site owner accountable for managing a specific work
location, equipment, activity / operation or person(s) involved in an incident.
In case of flaring incident involving two or more Group Companies, the Incident Owner will be the Group
Company that caused the incident.
HSE Performance Reporting:
Incidents (during execution of services) shall be reported in HSE Performance of Group Companies
receiving the services (Client GC) at their premises and NOT to GC providing Services (Contractor GC)
as it will be regarded as a Contractor under this arrangement and hence, the Client GC will be
maintaining the man-hours and recording the incident under its HSE performance / KPIs. Once the
work is suspended by the Client GC, the HSE Performance shall be recorded with the Contractor GC
Refer to Appendix 7 for examples on determining incident ownership and HSE performance reporting
requirements for incidents involving multiple Group Companies. Refer to ADNOC HSE Performance
Monitoring & Reporting Standard (HSE-GA-ST08)
7.15.3. Investigation Levels
The investigation level concept is set to optimize the resources and ensure involvement of the skills based
on the severity of the incident. The investigation levels are shown in Table 7.15.1 below.
Table 7.15.1 Investigation Levels
Incident Severity Level Investigation Level
Actual 5 or 6 / Potential 6 ADNOC Investigation
Actual 4 / Potential 5 Senior Management Investigation
Actual 3 / Potential 4 Team Investigation
Actual 2 / Potential 3 Team Investigation
Actual 1 / Potential 2 Site Investigation
Note:
a. Investigation shall follow the higher level of Severity (Actual vs Potential)
b. Tier 1 & Tier 2 Process Safety Events (LOPC) shall be investigated based on the actual
consequences of the incident as per API RP 754 and ADNOC Risk Matrix.

c. Tier 1 and Tier 2 Process Safety Event (LOPC) with no other associated consequences (e.g.
injury, illness, environment, assets, etc.) shall be investigated as below:
Investigation level
Process Tiers
Actual Tier Potential Tier
1 Senior Management Investigation Team Investigation
2 Team Investigation Site Investigation
d. For non-work related incidents investigation, the Severity shall be assumed considering the
actual consequences of incident
e. Refer to Section 7.18 for Non Accidental Death (NAD) Investigation
7.15.4. ADNOC Investigation
ADNOC investigation requires the highest level of independency when the incident with actual severity
of level 5 or above and HIPO with potential severity of level 6. ADNOC and/or GCs Shareholders may
take the lead of investigation or appoint an investigation Team Leader from other ADNOC GCs to
represent them. (Refer to Section 7.15.9). The Investigation Team Leader shall be independent of the
ADNOC Directorate / Function / Group Company where the Incident occurred.
7.15.5. Senior Management Investigation
Senior Management (VPs and above) investigation requires a higher level of team independency. The
Terms of Reference must be issued by GCs CEO/SVP. The investigation team must be selected from
the particular GC and Contractors management level (if required), with member (s) from ADNOC.
7.15.6. Team Investigation
Team investigation requires a high level of team independency. The Terms of Reference must be issued
by GC Management (e.g. Site VP / Manager Level).
7.15.7. Site Investigation
Site investigation requires local independency of investigation team as applicable. The Terms of
Reference must be issued by GC site management (e.g.: Manager / Department Head). For Incidents
with potential Severity 1, a formal investigation process may not be required (no TOR etc.) and site
investigation report can be issued directly.
7.15.8. Terms of Reference
Following the incident severity assessment as described in Section 7.6, the Terms of Reference (ToR)
must be approved and issued by the authority (incident owner) within 48 hours of the incident using the
template FM-GAST04-02. ToR for investigations must be specific and relevant to the incident.
Severity Level Investigation Level TOR Signatory
Relevant ADNOC Directorate
Actual 5 or 6 / Potential 6 ADNOC (Executive Director) / Function (Senior
VP) / Group Company (CEO)
Actual 4 / Potential 5 Senior Management Investigation ADNOC GC Executive Management
Actual 3 / Potential 4 Team Investigation ADNOC GC Site Management
Actual 2 / Potential 3 Team Investigation ADNOC GC Site Management
Actual 1 / Potential 2 Site Investigation ToR not required
The approval of the ToR formally initiates the investigation process. The ToR approver must ensure that
investigation objectives and deliverables are fully understood by the investigation team.
Approved Terms of Reference (ToR) shall facilitate investigation team on key investigation requirements
including access to facility, place of incident, healthcare facilities (as applicable), interview of witnesses
Standard No.: HSE-GA-ST04 Version No.: 1 Effective date: Dec., 2019 Page 85 of 184

ADNOC Classification: Internal
HSE Management System
HSE Governance & Administration Standards
Incident Notification, Investigation & Reporting
and other relevant personnel, review of records and reports pertinent to the incident including information
on any regulatory action if exists in relation to the incident.
The ToR must contain as minimum the following (Refer to FM-GAST04-02):
 Incident Details (Date / Time / Location / Severity / Title)
 Incident Description
 Investigation team details
 Investigation objectives shall consider the following:
- Investigate the circumstances leading up to the incident and establish sequence of
events.
- Investigate the response to the incident, including emergency actions taken.
- Analyze the information gathered and identify the immediate/root causes including
human factors, IOGP Casual Factors & Barrier Failures (as applicable), verify
effectiveness of existing Controls and identify any Missing Controls. Final Investigation
Report Form (FM-GAST04-07) facilitates Identifying IOGP Reporting Information
- Make recommendations to avoid recurrence of similar incidents and to improve the
incident response as appropriate.
- All the gathered information and identified causes shall be kept confidential.
 Timeline and Deliverables:
- A presentation shall be delivered by the Group Company CEO to ADNOC ELT on
DD/MM/YYYY along with the Preliminary Investigation Report.
- The Final Incident Investigation Report shall be submitted to Incident Owner and copy
to ADNOC Group HSE by DD/MM/YYYY
Investigation team shall adhere to the agreed ToR. If however, when an Investigation is in progress,
and additional facts come to light it may be justifiable and necessary to go beyond the ToR. In these
cases, the Authority who initially issued the ToR should be approached, to get an approval for required
changes including any changes to the established investigation timeline, for example more than 40
days.
7.15.9. Investigation Team
TOR Signatory shall nominate the Investigation Team within 48 hours of the Incident.
Investigation team is a group of persons nominated by the Incident Owner to perform a formal
investigation. HSE professional shall participate as member and not lead incident investigations.
There shall be a member from ADNOC Group HSE for investigation incidents with Severity Level 4 and
above.
Incident Owner may request ADNOC Group Medical Services Division (GMSD) to nominate a member
for investigating Occupational Health / Illness incidents (Severity 3 or above). GMSD Participation is
however mandatory for Non Accidental Death (NAD) Investigation.
The investigation team shall be responsible for conducting a formal investigation to:
 Identify significant findings and sequence of events leading to the incident
 Identify the immediate causes, root causes including human factors, IOGP Casual Factor and
Barrier Failures
 Link root causes to failed management systems
 Develop recommendations for each identified root causes
 Submit the investigation report to Incident Owner & a copy to ADNOC Group HSE
Standard No.: HSE-GA-ST04 Version No.: 1 Effective date: Dec., 2019 Page 86 of 184

Incident Notification, Investigation & Reporting
As a minimum, the Investigation Team Leader must be:
 A Department Head, Manager or SME level individual (for high-medium potential risk) outside or
within the site but not directly involved with the activities/operations associated with the incident.
 A Vice President / Site Manager (for high potential) outside the site and not directly involved with
the activities/operations associated with the incident.
 Knowledgeable about work process or activity associated with the incident
 Formally trained on Incident Investigation process, methodologies or tools
Note: For Incidents with Actual Severity 5 or above the Investigation Team Leader shall be
independent of the ADNOC Directorate / Function / Group Company where the Incident occurred
The Investigation Team Leader is responsible for:
 Ensuring that a comprehensive and timely investigation is performed.
 Coordinating with Incident Owner to distribute the investigation ToR to the Investigation Team
 Selecting and applying the most appropriate investigation technique considering the
consequences (actual / potential)
 Recording the outcome of an incident investigation in a formal written report and communicating
outcome to appropriate levels of Management
The Secretary of the incident Investigation is responsible for:
 Providing assistance to the Team Leader in complying with the investigation requirement
 Compiling investigation report
 Providing technical advice on the utilisation of right incident investigation software
 Secretary must formally be trained on Incident Investigation process, methodologies or tools
The Investigation Team Members must also be:
 Trained in the investigation process and competent in the skills necessary to support the
investigation.
 Knowledgeable about the process/activity associated with the incident or have been involved in
a similar incident investigation.
The team size may vary from two to eight persons depending on the severity, complexity and need of
expertise (e.g.: Subject Matter Expert, vendor representative etc.). Refer to Section 7.15.3 for
investigation levels based on actual and potential severity of the incidents.
Note: The concerned Incident Owner / GC must ensure that the investigation team including team leader,
secretory and members are released from their normal duty to dedicate their time and efforts exclusively
on the investigation respecting the defined timeline.
