7.15.10. Investigation Techniques
Incidents that fall in Severity 4 and above (Actual & Potential) must be investigated by using well known
Root Cause Analysis (RCA) techniques (Tripod- Beta, Bow-Tie, Incident trees (Cause Tree /Fault Tree),
Taproot etc.). At least one of the team members preferably the HSE member should be proficient in the
use of selected tool for the investigation.
The most commonly used investigation tools are represented in the below table.
Types of Root Cause Analysis Best Suited For
Methods (Including but not limited to)
Why – Why  Quick method for finding immediate & root causes
 Best for small events
 Good for initial investigation
Fault Tree Analysis  Identifies probability of failure for a complex system
like a control logic
 Suitable for failure events
Cause Tree Analysis (CTA)  Systematic, consistent, repeatable process to
identify a wide range of potential causes
 Identify Causal Factors from the Incident Timeline
List of Causes  For minor incidents, findings are directly related
with a relevant cause from the list of causes
Failure Mode & Effect Analysis (FMEA)  Method for getting an overview of all potential
failure causes and effects at an initial stage of an
investigation
 Requires detailed knowledge of the problem in
question
 Easy to use for both events and for potential losses
where risk is included
Sequential Time Event Plot Method  Investigation of larger incidents/accidents where
(STEP) method the time sequence is important
 Handles complex events with: several factors,
several events in parallel, a longer time horizon
 Include both equipment, control & human actions
Tripod Beta  Systematic method for finding basic and underlying
causes
 Suitable for complex incident investigations
TapRoot®  Structured root cause analysis
 Suitable for process safety incident investigation
 Human factor
 Barrier Analysis

Incident Notification, Investigation & Reporting
7.16. INCIDENT INVESTIGATION PROCESS
The key steps in the investigation process are outlined below and explained in below sections.
Figure 7.16.1: Investigation Process
7.16.1. Collect Facts
Facts surrounding the incident can be obtained by collecting information from four primary sources;
people, positions, parts, and papers. To form a clear picture of the incident in order to determine
immediate and root causes, all available information sources should be used.
It is also advisable taking photographs of the incident site as supporting evidence in the investigation
process. The incident site should be kept undisturbed until the required information from the site has been
gathered.
7.16.2. Witness Interviews / Statement
Interviews of incident witnesses must be conducted as soon as practicable. If there are multiple witnesses
to an incident, interviews should be conducted individually. Interviews may be conducted by two members
of the Incident Investigation Team, with one member responsible for the interview (asking questions),
and the other responsible for recording / documenting the interview (taking notes).
To ensure all relevant facts are revealed and captured during witness interviews, interviewers should
begin by asking broad, open-ended questions such as “who, what, where, when, why, and how”. More
specific, detailed questioning may continue based on responses to the broad questions. Interview notes
or witness statements for incidents involving an employee or contractor injury or fatality shall be
maintained.
Standard No.: HSE-GA-ST04 Version No.: 1 Effective date: Dec., 2019 Page 89 of 184

ADNOC Classification: Internal
HSE Management System
HSE Governance & Administration Standards
Incident Notification, Investigation & Reporting
7.16.3. Survey Incident Location
Incident scene surveys should focus on establishing positions of individuals, equipment, materials, etc.,
before, during, and after the incident or near miss. Diagrams and/or photographs shall be used, as
appropriate, to document the incident scene, and shall be appropriately labeled with a description of the
image. Where applicable, incident scene surveys may include, but are not limited to:
 Resting positions of equipment and other items.
 Positions of individuals before, during, and after the incident.
 Evidence of movement, such as skid marks, paint damage, etc.
 Location of Personal Protective Equipment (PPE) and/or alarms
 Operating conditions
7.16.4. Collect Physical Evidence
No physical evidence should be moved prior to surveying and documenting the scene of an incident.
Once positions of critical materials, equipment, etc., have been determined and documented, physical
evidence necessary for the investigation may be cataloged and removed for further investigation.
7.16.5. Review Relevant Documents
The Investigation team shall review documents relevant to the circumstances surrounding the incident.
Examples of documents that may be relevant to an incident include, but are not limited to:
 Work Permits
 Operating Procedures / Operations Daily Reports
 Risk Assessment
 Process Diagrams
 Project Plans
 HSE Plans
 Work Control Procedures
 Awareness & Training Records
 Maintenance Schedule & Records
 Inspection & Audit Schedules & Records
 Previous Incident & Near- miss Report
 Vendor documents / manuals
 Medical Certificates / Records (as applicable)
7.16.6. Evaluate Investigation Evidence
Team Leader of investigation team is responsible for coordination of all evidence collected by the
investigation committee. Once collected, the investigation team will determine the sequence of events
leading up to the incident, the immediate causes of the incident, the root causes of the incident, and make
recommendations for preventing similar incidents in the future.
7.16.7. Determine the Sequence of Events
The investigation team shall establish a chronology of events by Date, Time, and Place, indicating the
likely movement and progression of events as well as conditions leading up to the incident. Less
complicated sequence of events may be written as a narrative. Complex sequence of events involving
multiple individuals, equipment, or other materials may require the construction of an event timeline
(diagram).
Standard No.: HSE-GA-ST04 Version No.: 1 Effective date: Dec., 2019 Page 90 of 184

ADNOC Classification: Internal
HSE Management System
HSE Governance & Administration Standards
Incident Notification, Investigation & Reporting
7.16.8. Determine Incident Causes – Immediate and Root Causes
The investigation team shall determine and document immediate and root causes of the incident.
Every incident is triggered by one or a cumulative set of immediate causes that were supported by the
composition of underlying root causes. The nature of the immediate causes represents the mixture of
sub-standard acts by individuals and presence of sub-standard conditions at the time of the incident.
These will not evolve unless the root causes were existent (Human Factor and Job Factor) which possess
the highest weight in contribution, to provoke the presence of unsafe practices / conditions leading the
incidents to occur. Therefore, incident investigation primary goal must be the identification of the root
causes rather than immediate causes.
The incident causation model is represented in the below Figure 7.16.2
Lack of Control Root Causes Immediate Causes Incident Loss
Inadequate: •Lack of Mangement •Substandard Acts/ •Event
•Uninteded Harm /
System Control Practices Damage
Standard •Personal Factors •Substandard
Compliance •Job Factors Conditions
Figure 7.16.2: Incident Causation Model
(a) Immediate Causes
Immediate causes are defined as the “symptoms” of the root causes, or the cursory, direct cause of the
incident.
The immediate causes of an incident are the circumstances that immediately preceded the contact and
can usually be seen or sensed. It is defined as substandard acts or conditions that lead directly to the
incident. For example, if the incident is an oil spill, the immediate cause could be a broken sealing.
(b) Root Causes
Root causes are defined as the management system breakdown that permitted the substandard action
or condition (immediate cause) to occur. Root Causes may also be referred to as System Causes or
Underlying Causes.
Root causes are inadequacies in the management system that allow the immediate causes to arise
unchecked, leading to the incidents. Root Causes are the real causes behind the immediate causes: the
reasons why the substandard acts and conditions occurred, the factors that, when identified, permit
meaningful management control.
In case of an oil spill caused by a broken sealing, the root causes could be that the sealing used was of
wrong type, it had a design failure or it might be installed wrong due to a lack of procedure.
Investigation teams shall align and map incident causes with the List of ADNOC Causes (Appendix 3)
(c) Human Factors
Human Factors’ are simply those things that can influence what people do. They may include factors
relating to the job people do (e.g., time available or control panel design) personnel factors (e.g., fatigue,
capability) and organisational factors (roles, manning levels). ADNOC List of Causes includes Human
Factor under the Root Causes. This list of factors is often referred to as “Performance Shaping Factors”.
If a behaviour was causal or contributory to the incident, Human Factors analysis helps the investigator
in understanding why human performance suffered, and help develop meaningful recommendations to
reduce that risk in the future.
People are involved in maintaining and instigating barriers and safeguards. People design, operate, and
maintain engineered barriers. They also perform tasks, checks and monitoring that we rely on to prevent,
detect, and respond to risk events. Humans are involved in every aspect of organization’s business:
Standard No.: HSE-GA-ST04 Version No.: 1 Effective date: Dec., 2019 Page 91 of 184

ADNOC Classification: Internal
HSE Management System
HSE Governance & Administration Standards
Incident Notification, Investigation & Reporting
 Engineers and planners in project design
 Crafts in construction
 Operators in the control of processes
 Maintenance technicians in maintaining the kit
 Inspectors in checking the kit
 Managers and leaders who oversee and influence the conditions for all of the above actions
If people do not perform as anticipated, it can have a negative effect on the outcome, including causing
or contributing to an incident. Human performance can directly affect the effectiveness of a barrier that
relies on a human to sense, decide, and/or act to make it work.
The goal of human factors (HF) in investigation is to understand what influenced the behaviours that were
causal or contributory to the incident. Plants, tools, and activities can be designed to reduce mistakes
and manage risk better.
Refer to ADNOC List of Causes (Appendix 3), International Standards and Best Practices including IOGP
Report [621] of 2018 “Demystifying Human Factors: Building confidence in human factors investigation”
and “HSE UK Guidance on Human factors in Accident Investigations” for understating expectations on
Human Factors’ Analysis in each stage of Incident Investigation
7.16.9. Investigation Recommendations
All recommendations shall be risk classified (High, High/Medium, Medium, Low) in accordance with the
ADNOC Risk Matrix (Appendix 1). Accordingly the investigation process shall identify corrective actions
to prevent the recurrence of similar incidents. Corrective actions shall address root causes and conditions
by identifying and correcting failures in systems and procedures which led to the incident and could pose
a similar hazard in the future.
The Responsible Parties assigned to each corrective action shall be notified by the Incident Owner, and
timeline for completion of action items will be recorded. Refer to Section 7.19 “Corrective Actions”

7.17. INCIDENT INVESTIGATION REPORTING
7.17.1. Preliminary Investigation Report
A Preliminary Investigation Report (FM-GAST04-03) for incidents with Severity 3 or above must be
completed and submitted to ADNOC Group HSE within seven (07) days following an incident occurrence.
This report must contain the initial incident findings, immediate causes, the initial actions, initial potential
severity assessment validation, any required investigation support, etc.
Preliminary Investigation Report shall be presented to the ToR signatories and any other party identified
in the ToR (FM-GAST02-03) that must be informed based on the incident severity level. Refer to Section
7.21 about requirements for issuing the HSE Flash. Refer to Section 7.17.4 for presentation of preliminary
investigation findings to ADNOC ELT and Management.
7.17.2. Final Investigation Report
The investigation team is responsible of developing recommendations as part of final investigation report.
The recommendation must be clearly written and its closure easily measurable. The investigation team
must ensure that the recommendations address all identified root causes.
The final Investigation Report (FM-GAST04-07) must be completed, approved by ToR signatory and
issued within forty [40] days of the incident occurring. Occupational Illness Investigation Report (FM-
GAST04-08) shall be completed for confirmed cases of Occupational Illness and attached as supplement
to FM-GAST04-07.
Detailed Investigation Report shall be prepared using the ADNOC Investigation Report Templates FM-
GAST04-12 (for internal users) and FM-GAST04-13 (for external users).
As a minimum, the detailed investigation report should include the following sections and contents:
1. Title Page
2. Table of Contents
3. Investigation Team
4. Executive Summary
a. Incident Description
b. Key findings
c. Key causes and contributors
d. Key Recommendations
5. Introduction
Investigation Methodology
6. Background (e.g. descriptions of equipment, organizations and management systems (policies/
standards/ procedures).
7. Sequence of Events
a. Status of Operation at Time of Incident
b. Status of Events Leading to and following the Incident
8. Description of Emergency Response (Include any gaps and deviations from established
procedures/practices)
9. Significant Findings and Causes
a. Immediate Causes
b. Root Causes including Human Factors
c. ADNOC Life Safety Rules Violation

10. Recommendations with Risk Classification
11. Recommendations segregated as a confidential report in relation with the Consequence
Management Process
12. Cause Tree Analysis or Fault Tree Analysis
13. References
14. Appendices
a. Investigation Terms of Reference (FM-GAST04-02)
b. Root Cause Analysis

d. Photographs, Diagrams, Maps
e. Relevant documents such as Permit, Lab Analysis, witness statements etc.

Note: Relevant documents should include procedures, copies of Work Permits or other documents of

was damaged, the details may be attached in the relevant document section.
For Occupational Disease / Illness Investigation attach the Occupational Illness Investigation Report (FM-
GAST04-08)
Upon completion of the final investigation, the Investigation Team Leader shall elaborate and provide any
clarifications on the investigation’s recommendations to the Incident Owner as and when required.
HSE Lessons Learned (HSELL) Report (FM-GAST04-09) shall be developed as per requirements of
Section 7.21.
7.17.3. Review by ADNOC Group HSE (GHSE)
Detailed Investigation Report [FM-GAST04-12] for Reportable Incidents shall be submitted to ADNOC
Group HSE for necessary review and verifying compliance with this Standard [Refer to Figure 7.1.1].
Incident Owner shall provide clarification and additional information on the investigation if deemed
necessary by ADNOC Group HSE.
SVP ADNOC Group HSE Function reserves the right to direct additional requirements for the
investigation, additional actions on submitted investigation report or undertake further investigation, as
deemed necessary.
7.17.4. Presentation to ADNOC ELT & Management
CEO of the affected Group Company / ADNOC Function Manager of concerned Directorate shall make
presentation to ADNOC Executive Leadership Team (ELT) the findings of Preliminary Investigation (7
days) for ALL Incidents (Severity 4 and above)
Upon completion of Investigation, A high-level summary of the Final Incident Investigation findings is
required for presentation to the GC Management and to ADNOC Group HSE.
As a minimum, such power point presentation shall include:
 Incident summary (date, time, injured person(s) details, activities that led to incident)
 Significant findings and root causes
 Recommendations and lessons learned
Similar communication practice to be followed in the respective GC Board & technical committees / sub
committees’ meetings.
CEO of the affected Group Company / ADNOC Function Manager of concerned Directorate shall ensure
that upon completion of investigation for all incident with Severity Level 3 or above, investigation reports
are submitted to ADNOC Group HSE and respective Directorate for review followed by a presentation by
the investigation team leader along with respective incident owner.

7.17.5. Conducting Roadshows
CEO of the affected Group Company / ADNOC Function Manager of concerned Directorate shall ensure
that the roadshows are conducted by the Incident Owner across the ADNOC Group for all incidents with
actual Severity Level 5 or above focusing on sharing the lessons learned and creating awareness to
avoid recurrence. Detailed schedule for organizing roadshows shall be completed within 30 days of
approval / issuance of Final Investigation Report.
7.17.6. Investigation Report for External Users
A summary of the incident investigation findings may be required for sharing with external users including
Group Companies shareholder, media, etc. using ADNOC Investigation Report Template for External
Users (FM-GAST04-13). Investigation Report for External Users shall be approved by CEO of the Group
Company
As a minimum, such report shall include:
 Incident summary (brief description, date, time, location);
 Key Findings (What Went Wrong?)
 Key Recommendations
 Key Lessons Learnt

7.18. INVESTIGATING NON ACCIDENTAL DEATH (NAD)
NAD investigation shall be initiated as soon as possible. The investigation shall be completed within fourty
[40] days from the occurrence of the NAD. When delays are expected, ADNOC Group HSE shall be
notified about the possible delays in completing the investigation in time.
Non-accidental death is the death of a company or contractor employee due to suicide or a non-work
related illness occurring in the work environment or at employers’ controlled accommodations (Refer
to Section 7.3.4 & 7.3.5). Examples of NAD include:
 Suicide
 Death due to heart attack or stroke occurred at office, site, employer’s provided camp or
accommodation
 A death as a result of non-work related illness occurring while an employee (company /
contractor) is off duty and resting in his/her onsite accommodation / camp (employers’
controlled accommodation).
 A non-work related illness, that started in the work environment and resulted in death
outside of the work environment, e.g. on the way to hospital, at the hospital or at home
All NADs shall be notified and investigated to establish work relatedness by identifying and analyzing
contributors and causes.
The key objectives of the NAD investigation include
 Rule out work relatedness of death cases at a workplace, so to ensure the case is not work
related when it is not apparent that occupational illness or injury is not a contributory factor
to death.
 Helps in establishing any potential trend of NAD within the Company / Area / Operation,
 Evaluating adequacy of first aid and medical emergency provisions and
 Evaluate implementation of 'fitness to work' and health risk assessment within the
workplace.
Common causes and contributors like fatigue, work stress, hypertension, work conditions, excessive
shift work and other such conditions triggering health impacts including cardiac complications, stroke
leading to serious illness or fatality.
Hospital medical reports, employees’ medical records / history, supervisors / colleagues feedback,
occupational health risk assessment (OHRA), work conditions, etc. are helpful in establishing work
relatedness.
Note: Investigating fatality / injury at workers accommodation (camp) resulting due to violence,
criminal, personal activity (drug) etc. is not under the scope of this Standard.
7.18.1. Investigation Objectives
 Investigate the situation leading to the event.
 To rule out work relatedness of death cases at a workplace, so to ensure the case is not work related
when it is not apparent that occupational illness or injury is not a contributory factor to death.
 To identify possible causes of the death. Analyze the information gathered and identify the factors
leading to the non-accidental death e.g. review pre-employment/periodic medical checks, fitness to
work, medical and police
 Make recommendations to reduce the possibility of similar events occurring again, together any
recommendation to improve the response as appropriate.
 To establish any potential trend of NAD within the area, operation or Group Company, where the
NAD occurred.
 To enhance health management system, including welfare, in order to prevent / minimize cases of
NAD.
Standard No.: HSE-GA-ST04 Version No.: 1 Effective date: Dec., 2019 Page 96 of 184

ADNOC Classification: Internal
HSE Management System
HSE Governance & Administration Standards
Incident Notification, Investigation & Reporting
 Evaluate adequacy of Emergency Preparedness & Response (ERP) including resources, first-aid
and medical facilities
 Evaluate effectiveness of response to the event including any first aid, AED and medical assistance
delivered.
 Evaluate implementation of 'fitness to work' and health risk assessment within the workplace.
 Determine facts by reviewing relevant documents and records including as a minimum site NAD
report, Hospital Report, Health Surveillance Records, Training Records, Site Medical Team
Certification / Licenses, Police Report, Job Description, Timesheet, Medical Emergency Drill, SOPs,
OHRA, etc.
 To identify areas of improvement in HSE performance.
7.18.2. Investigation Requirements
 NAD Investigation shall be instigated as soon as reasonably practicable.
 NAD Owner i.e. the relevant ADNOC Directorate / Function / Group Company’s Management shall
ensure that they have pool of competent Incident Investigation Leaders.
 NAD Owner shall select investigation team leader.
 If deemed necessary, the NAD owner may outsource expert services to support / conduct the
investigation, e.g. toxicologist.
 Terms of Reference (ToR) for investigation shall be approved and issued by NAD owner prior to the
investigation (Refer to Section 7.15.8).
 Terms of Reference (ToR), shall facilitate investigation team on investigation requirements including
access to facility, place of incident, healthcare facilities, interview of witnesses and other relevant
personnel, review of records and reports pertinent to the incident including information on any
regulatory action or litigation if exists in relation to the incident.
 The expected composition of the NAD investigation team should be multi-level and cross-functional.
If a contractor's employee is involved in NAD, a contractor representative shall be a member of the
investigation team.
 HSE Occupational Health SME and Medical representative from respective ADNOC Group
Companies / Affiliates / Function shall be appointed as member of the team to provide the required
technical support.
 A member from ADNOC Group Medical Division (GMD) shall be included in the Investigation Team,
as required.
 ADNOC Group Medical Division shall support the investigation team on any medical matters (e.g.
access to the medical facility & information, interview of the medical personnel, obtaining necessary
medical records, etc.) during the course of the investigation.
 Records pertaining to health and medical information including those related to NAD (e.g. forensic
report, death certificate etc.) shall be accessed in line with local and federal regulatory requirements
governing the release of confidential medical information.
 Investigation team members shall be given adequate relief from their normal duties to complete the
investigation. (already addressed)
 The investigation team shall conduct the investigation independently, without interference from other
parties.
 While investigating NAD, investigation team shall review effectiveness of first aid & emergency
response, pre-employment medical records, fitness to work, OHRA, site medical team certification /
licenses, medical and police reports etc.

HSE Management System
HSE Governance & Administration Standards
Incident Notification, Investigation & Reporting
7.18.3. NAD Investigation Report
 NAD investigation report shall follow the applicable requirements stipulated in Section 7.16 of this
Standard.
 The initial notification report shall be reviewed by the NAD investigation team to ensure that no
immediate action is required.
 The investigation team shall conduct the sole NAD investigation into the facts leading to the NAD,
and shall pursue any reasonable line of inquiry to establish evidence addressing what happened,
how it happened and why it happened.
 The investigation team should maintain the confidentiality of the investigation materials, controlling
the flow of information regarding the investigation and releasing information only when they find it
prudent or necessary to do so.
 Where possible the investigation team shall visit the scene of the death. Interview shall be conducted
when/where necessary and statements shall be documented.
 Once the information is gathered, it shall be analysed to identify immediate and underlying causes.
 Some deficiencies identified during the investigation may not contribute directly or indirectly to the
NAD. However, if such deficiencies are identified, they shall be presented in the report and marked
as "opportunities for improvement".
 NAD Investigation Checklist (FM-GAST04-14) shall be completed and enclosed with the Final and
Detailed Investigation Reports (FM-GAST04-07 & 12)
 An investigation report shall be made of the investigation team's findings and conclusions and should
represent a consensus among investigation team members.
 The report shall be treated as a highly confidential and distributed to limited personnel as agreed in
ToR.
 The NAD investigation report shall include proposed remedial action and corrective actions plan.
 The report shall be addressed to the relevant NAD Owner’s Management [ADNOC Directorate
(Executive Director) / Function (Senior VP) / Group Company (CEO)] with accountability for the area
or operation where the NAD occurred.
 NAD Owner upon receiving the report shall establish a schedule and assign personnel to complete
the actions.
 NAD Owner shall obtain progress reports from assigned personnel and ensure completion of the
tasks required to meet the proposed corrective actions in a timely manner.
7.18.4. Post Investigation Reporting
 All the gathered information and identified causes shall be kept strictly in confidential manner.
 Investigation report distribution shall be limited and agreed in Terms of Reference prior to the
investigation.
 No information shall be disclosed without formal consent of the relevant NAD Owner [ADNOC
Directorate (Executive Director) / Function (Senior VP) / Group Company (CEO)]
 After the completion of the investigation, final classification (work or non-work related) shall be
established and reporting (NAD or work related Fatality) shall be updated accordingly.
 All NAD investigation reports shall be submitted to ADNOC Group HSE.
Standard No.: HSE-GA-ST04 Version No.: 1 Effective date: Dec., 2019 Page 98 of 184

Incident Notification, Investigation & Reporting
7.19. CORRECTIVE ACTIONS
Corrective actions ensure that appropriate measures are in place to prevent recurrence of similar
incidents. Upon submittal of an investigation report and recommendations to the Incident Owner,
corrective action plan (CAP) shall be developed by the Incident Owner identifying corrective actions
responsible parties, target dates and execution priorities for implementation considering risk
classification. Corrective Action Plan shall be developed using Form FM-GAST04-11.
An Action Party must be selected from within or outside the Incident Owner’s organization depending
on the nature of action to be implemented.
Action Parities are responsible for:
 Effective and timely implementation of assigned actions
 Requesting due dates changes (extension) if assigned due date is not feasible
The action items and their target dates shall be entered in ADNOC Action Tracking System after
agreement between Action party and the Incident owner. The status of these actions will be monitored
by the Incident Owner to ensure timely completion. Where the due dates for the assigned actions are
not feasible, the Action Party may request a change.
For all incidents with Severity 3 & above (actual), HIPO Severity 4 & above (potential), update on action
plan shall be provided to ADNOC Group HSE on quarterly basis.
7.20. RECORDKEEPING AND ARCHIVING
Incident Owners across ADNOC Group shall archive all details and maintain relevant records related to
incident investigation for minimum period of ten (10) Years. The details shall include as minimum:
 Incident notification report(s);
 Final / Detailed Investigation Report along with associated records / documents; and
 Action closeouts

7.21. HSE FLASH & HSE LESSONS LEARNED
7.21.1. HSE Flash
HSE Flash (FM-GAST04-04) shall be issued as soon as possible (no later than 7 Days) following an HSE
Occurrence / Incident (examples provided below) summarizing information known at that time. It is an
effective tool to share the initial findings and recommendations within ADNOC Group to take robust action
to prevent recurrence for similar activities and operations.
HSE Flash shall later be updated (as deemed necessary) as and further detailed information and issued
along with the Preliminary Investigation Report (FM-GAST04-03), if any specific HSE issues which
require immediate action being taken to prevent serious or fatal injury and recurrence of incident.
When dangerous equipment, failure of processes, inadequate or missing procedures, serious gaps in
HSE Management System (system failure), technological changes or harmful substances are identified
during the investigation, the GC must issue an HSE Flash within the company and inform ADNOC.
ADNOC will then decide if it may need to notify other GCs or stakeholders of the issue.
While preparing the HSE Flash and HSE Lessons Learned (Section 7.21.2), the information should be
sanitized by the reporting ADNOC Group Function, Division and Group Company to omit any sensitive
information such as injured person(s)’ personal information.
ADNOC Group Function, Division and Group Company to ensure effective communication of HSE Flash
and Lessons Learned by cascading information to all levels including employees, contractors, sub-
contractors, displaying at bulletin boards, discussing in toolbox talks, meeting, trainings, etc.
Examples of scenarios for issuing HSE Flash include but not limited to:
 Incidents (Severity 3 and above)
 High potential incidents (HIPO)
 Significant HSE Observations that provide learning across the organization, such as:
 Serious HSE non-compliance
 Failure of Procedures / Processes (e.g. MoC, Risk Management etc.)
ADNOC HSE Flash minimum shall contain as a:
 Unique issue number and issue date;
 HSE occurrence / observation, location, date and description;
 Relevant photos or illustrative diagrams;
 Summary of initial findings;
 Summary of immediate actions and recommendations
Refer to Section 7.21.3 & Figure 7.21.1 about the Requirements and Process for Issuing HSE Flash &
Lessons Learned
Standard No.: HSE-GA-ST04 Version No.: 1 Effective date: Dec., 2019 Page 100 of 184

ADNOC Classification: Internal
HSE Management System
HSE Governance & Administration Standards
Incident Notification, Investigation & Reporting
7.21.2. HSE LESSON LEARNED
HSE Lessons Learned [HSELL] are integral part of Incident Management Process, ensuring continual
improvement through communication of significant learning from investigation of HSE Occurrences
including but not limited to Incidents, High Potential Incidents, Significant HSE Observations, etc.
HSELL are used for effective sharing of lessons learned within the ADNOC Group to prevent similar
reoccurrence and shall be issued no later than 5 Days of completing the investigation.
HSE Lessons Learned helps in gaining more insight into the HSE occurrences, contributors, root
causes and taking corresponding actions to address the identified gaps and adopting best practices
for continual improvement.
HSE Lessons Learned shall be based on the:
 Findings of HSE Occurrences’ Investigations
 Recommendations by ADNOC Consequence Action Committee
 Vulnerabilities identified during monitoring, audit and review processes
HSE Lesson Learned Report (FM-GAST04-09) must be developed by the investigation team and
submitted within five (5) Days of completing the investigation. As a minimum, the HSELL shall contain:
 Title, unique issue number
 Location and Date of Occurrence
 Brief description with photos or illustrative diagrams (see below for info sanitization)
 Summary of Immediate and Root causes
 Summary of key actions and recommendations
The target audience for sharing the HSE Lesson Learned (HSELL) are the internal ADNOC Group, i.e.
all ADNOC employees (direct and contracted), Shareholders and ADNOC Contractors with whom
Confidentiality Contract in place.
The benefit of HSELL is seen in terms of mitigating potential HSE and business risks (e.g. what can
go wrong and how), taking proactive corrective actions and adopting current best HSE and Technical
practices for continual improvement.
The following are potential options for sharing / communicating the HSE Lessons Learned
 Web Portal(s)
 Email Notifications
 Bulletin Boards
 HSE Meetings / Toolbox Talks
 Trainings
 Newsletters
Refer to Figure 7.21.1 about the Process for Issuing HSE Flash & Lesson Learned as described below.
7.21.3. ADNOC Group Functions, Divisions & Group Companies
a) Relevant ADNOC Group Function, Division or Group Company shall prepare the HSE Flash within
seven (7) days following an HSE Occurrence and HSE Lessons Learned (HSELL) within five (5)
days of completing the Incident Investigation.
b) Relevant ADNOC Group Function, Division or Group Company HSE to communicate the “HSE
Flash / HSELL” to ADNOC Group HSE Function (hseppd@adnoc.ae) for sharing across ADNOC
Group.
c) ADNOC Group HSE Function upon receiving the HSE Flash / HSELL may raise comments / ask
clarification as applicable and return the HSE Flash / HSELL to concerned ADNOC Group
Standard No.: HSE-GA-ST04 Version No.: 1 Effective date: Dec., 2019 Page 101 of 184

ADNOC Classification: Internal
HSE Management System
HSE Governance & Administration Standards
Incident Notification, Investigation & Reporting
Function, Division or Group Company within two (2) working days of receiving the HSE Flash /
HSELL. The relevant ADNOC Group Function, Division or Group Company shall address the
comments (as applicable) and submit the final HSE Flash / HSELL within two (2) working days of
receiving the comments.
d) ADNOC Group HSE shall share the final HSE Flash / HSELL across ADNOC Group within two (2)
working days of receiving from ADNOC Group Function, Division and Group Company.
e) ADNOC Group Function, Division and Group Company upon receiving HSE Flash / HSELL from
ADNOC Group HSE shall ensure cascading information to relevant audience including contractors
and sub-contractors.
f) ADNOC Group Function, Division and Group Company shall ensure effective communication of
HSE Flash / HSELL through electronic distribution, displaying on notice boards, reviewing in
meetings, toolbox talks, trainings, etc.
g) ADNOC Group Function, Division and Group Company to ensure that all recommendations
provided in the HSE Flash / HSELL shall be addressed and actions implemented accordingly.
h) ADNOC Group Function, Division and Group Company shall keep ADNOC Group HSE updated
on progress / status of actions and maintain records.
i) The information including photos contained in the HSELL should be sanitized by the reporting
ADNOC Group Function, Division or Group Company to omit any sensitive information such as
injured persons’ personal details, specific location, propriety information, identification of specific
ADNOC asset, etc.
j) ADNOC HSELL template includes a Disclaimer to control dissemination of information.
7.21.4. ADNOC GROUP HSE FUNCTION
a) Engage relevant Subject Matter Experts (SMEs) at ADNOC Group HSE Function to review the
HSE Flash / HSELL received from the relevant ADNOC Group Function, Division or Group
Company to verify contents integrity and elimination of discrepancies / conflict (if any) with ADNOC
Rules / Standards. Any improvement areas / comments identified during the review shall be
communicated to relevant ADNOC Group Function, Division or Group Company within two (2)
working days of receiving the HSE Flash / HSELL
b) Receive final HSE Flash / HSELL from the relevant ADNOC Group Function, Division and Group
Company.
c) Circulate HSE Flash / HSELL to relevant audiences across ADNOC Group within two (2) working
days of receiving from ADNOC Group Function, Division and Group Company.

8. COMPLIANCE ASSURANCE
ADNOC will conduct a compliance audit of this Standard at an interval defined in ADNOC HSE Audit &
Assurance Standard (HSE-GA-ST09). These Audits will be in addition to GC’s internal audits and where
required, ADNOC will take steps to minimise overlap and duplication between ADNOC and Group
Companies internal audits.
The main audit deliverable is a formal and structured report for the attention of GC Management and the
respective ADNOC Directorate.
8.1. INCIDENT ANALYSIS
Every GC must develop, document, implement and maintain a systematic process to review and analyze
the reported incidents and completed incident investigations annually.
As a minimum this review shall identify:
 Repeating incidents;
 Incident causation;
 Reoccurring trends;
 Root Cause(s); and
 Statistical data as required
8.2. PERFORMANCE KPIS
Key Performance Indicators for Incident Investigation and Reporting shall include as minimum the
following:
No. KPI Targets
1. Notification of Reportable Incidents (incidents with 100% within timeframe defined in
actual severity 3 or above and HIPO potential this Standard
severity 4 or above).
2. Appropriateness of ToR and issuance within 100% compliance with
timeframe defined in this Standard. requirements of this Standard
3. Nominating Investigation Team. 100% compliance with
requirements of this Standard
4. Identification of immediate causes and root causes 100% compliance with ADNOC List
(using ADNOC List Of Causes Appendix 3). of Causes (Appendix 3)
5. Submission of Investigation Report. 100% within timeframe defined in
this Standard
6. Completeness of Investigation Report 100% compliance with
requirements of this Standard
7. Issuance of Learning From Incident 100% within timeframe defined in
this Standard
8. Quarterly Update on Action Plan for Incidents with 100% compliance with
Severity 4 and above requirements of this Standard
9. Timely closure of investigation findings. 100% closed as per target dates set
in the action plan (entered in
ADNOC tracking system)

Appendix 4: Non-Exhaustive List of High Potential (HIPO) Events
APPENDIX 4: NON-EXHAUSTIVE LIST OF HIGH POTENTIAL (HIPO) EVENTS
1. General
The potential scenario to be considered is a combination of the actual event with an unfavorable, likely,
and independent factor. To support the decision, the following questions and aspects should be considered:
FOR THE CIRCUMSTANCES:
a) Was the presence of people in the impacted area (usually or intermittently) possible?
b) Was a routine activity (e.g. hot work), or the normal operation of equipment (e.g. furnace in operation)
taking place near the incident area possible?
c) Were less favorable weather conditions (e.g. speed and direction of wind, ambient temperature) or
different sea conditions possible?
d) Was the presence of an entirely different fact in the context of the actual incident plausible? (e.g. media
presence nearby).
FOR EMERGENCY EQUIPMENT:
a) Is the failure of the ultimate recovery or mitigation equipment (e.g. water curtain, sprinklers, choking
steams, retention bund) that clearly contributed to limiting the severity of the event reasonably
conceivable?
b) Breathing apparatus
The malfunction of breathing apparatus:
(i) where the malfunction causes a significant risk of personal injury to the user; or
(ii) during testing immediately prior to use, where the malfunction would have caused a
significant risk to the health and safety of the user had it occurred during use.
FOR UNUSUAL RECOVERY ACTIONS:
a) Could the unexpected recovery or mitigation actions that has contributed to limiting or preventing the
severity of the actual event (e.g. human recovery action, excluding routine or planned actions, recovery
action, excluding design – specifications, activation of emergency stop button) not have happened?
2. Specific Cases
The following cases are systematically evaluated and reported as HIPO
2.1 Workplace Hazards
PENETRATION (SHARPS) INJURIES
All work-related penetration injures such as needle-stick injuries and cuts from sharp objects that are
contaminated with another person's blood or other potentially infectious material. These injuries shall be
reported and investigated as HIPO to confirm any transmission of infection. Refer to Section 7.8.7 for
Penetration (SHARPS) Injuries’ reporting requirements
WORK AND OPERATIONS AT HEIGHT
 Person falling from more than 3 m.
 Rupture of the fastenings of a handrail or a solicited grating, even without the person falling, with a
potential fall of more than 3 m.
 The complete or partial collapse (including falling, buckling or overturning) of:
(a) a substantial part of any scaffold more than 5 meters in height;
(b) any supporting part of any slung or suspended scaffold which causes a working platform to fall
(whether or not in use); or
(c) any part of any scaffold in circumstances such that there would be a significant risk of drowning
to a person falling from the scaffold.

Appendix 4: Non-Exhaustive List of High Potential (HIPO) Events
LIFTING OPERATION
 Overturning of lifting equipment, including forklifts, even with a closed cabin.
 Breaking of the structure of lifting equipment (e.g. stem, bridge crane, any crane).
 Falling of a load during lifting on an unmarked or un-barricaded area.
 Contact of a gear element with a medium or high voltage line (> 500 V).
EXCAVATION WORKS
 Contact of earthmoving or excavation equipment with a buried medium or high voltage electrical line
(> 500 V).
 Contact of earthmoving or excavation equipment with a pipe or pipeline buried under gas pressure,
or flammable liquid (flash point ≤ 37.8 °C) or toxic liquid.
 Undeclared excavation works performed by third parties near or over a gas-transporting pipeline,
flammable (flash point ≤ 37.8 °C) or toxic product.
DIVING OPERATIONS
The failure, damaging or endangering of—
 any life support equipment, including control panels, hoses and breathing apparatus;
 the dive platform, or any failure of the dive platform to remain on station,
Which causes a significant risk of personal injury to a diver.
- The failure or endangering of any lifting equipment associated with a diving operation.
- The trapping of a diver.
- Any explosion in the vicinity of a diver.
- Any uncontrolled ascent or any omitted decompression which causes a significant risk of
personal injury to a diver.
VARIOUS WORKS
 Fall of an object from a height that could have caused death (mass x height of fall > 50 Joules or 5
kg.m) with the presence of personnel near the point of fall or on frequent passage area.
 Projection of an object that could have caused death because of the mass and speed of the object
being projected as well as its trajectory (presence of personnel or frequent passage area).
 Electrification with low or medium voltage (> 50 V and < 500 V not protected by a residual current
circuit breaker, or > 500 V even if protected by a circuit breaker).
HOT WORK OPERATIONS
 Uncontrollable fire following hot work operations (i.e. intervention of site emergency response teams
or external firefighters - utilization of means exceeding those anticipated in the permit to work).
 Hot work on non-isolated piping or a non-degassed vessel containing a flammable product (even if
there has been no fire or explosion).
CONFINED SPACES
 Sickness, loss of consciousness in a confined space caused by lack of oxygen or presence of toxic
product.
ON-SITE TRAFFIC
 Transport on site (see section “Transport risks” below).
 Pedestrian or cyclist hit or Severity by a moving motorized vehicle on site.
 Mobile, floating, or rolling device trapping a person against a structure.

Appendix 4: Non-Exhaustive List of High Potential (HIPO) Events
2.2 Technological Risks
PROCESS INCIDENTS
 Unstable product decomposition, reaction runaway potentially leading to an event with a severity level
≥ 4.
 Emergency Shutdown of Plant
FLARE
 Spill of ignited / unignited liquids from the flare.
 Unplanned Extinguishing of a flare (Pilot).
LOSSES OF PRIMARY CONTAINMENT
 Leak of flammable liquid or gas, leading to a confirmed detection of gas on several detectors and
resulting in an emergency shutdown of the unit.
 Leak of toxic product generating an immediate danger to human life (Irreversible Exposure Threshold
or Immediately Dangerous to Life or Health [IDLH] reached) due to concentration levels obtained
around the leak.
 Massive and severe flow of product in a retention bund (mechanical effects).
 Leak of flammable product having a flash point ≤ 37.8 °C in a retention bund depending on the quantity
and sensitivity of the environment (presence of ignition sources).
 Leak of product having a flash point > 37.8 °C under special conditions (temperature, pressure) or
near facilities allowing ignition.
 Overfilling of a vessel or LPG tank (not immediately detected).
 Uncontrollable leak of LPG liquid.
 Projection of bitumen during loading (especially following the presence of water).
WELLS
In relation to a well (other than a well sunk for the purpose of the abstraction of water)—
 a blow-out (which includes any uncontrolled flow of well-fluids from a well);
 the coming into operation of a blow-out prevention or diversion system to control flow of well-fluids
where normal control procedures fail;
 the detection of hydrogen sulphide at a well or in samples of well-fluids where the responsible person
did not anticipate its presence in the reservoir drawn on by the well;
 the taking of precautionary measures additional to any contained in the original drilling programme
where a planned minimum separation distance between adjacent wells was not maintained; or
 the mechanical failure of any part of a well whose purpose is to prevent or limit the effect of the
unintentional release of fluids from a well or a reservoir being drawn on by a well, or whose failure
would cause or contribute to such a release.
FIRE/FLASH FIRES/EXPLOSIONS/RUPTURE
 Ignition of a gas cloud (flashfire).
 Explosion (detonation, deflagration).
 Fire, flash, or generation of flaming soot, sparks in an area classified as potentially explosive
(hazardous area).
 Fire on board a ship or barge carrying flammable or explosive products.
 Fire/flashfire at loading or unloading of a tanker vehicle.
 Rupture of a gas bottle during filling or handling.
 Rupture of a pressure vessel.
 Sustained fire whether or not quickly controlled.

Appendix 4: Non-Exhaustive List of High Potential (HIPO) Events
2.3 Transport Risks
LIGHT VEHICLES & HEAVY VEHICLES USED FOR PUBLIC TRANSPORT OF PERSONNEL
 Vehicle rollover.
 Vehicle unintentionally running-off the road in particularly risky conditions (speed, fall in a ravine, fall
in water).
 Collision:
- With truck or fixed obstacle (to be assessed according to report). Excludes minor collisions at
low speeds (manoeuvres, etc.) and collisions when the light vehicle is stationary or parked
without passengers on board;
- Between light vehicles with significant damage to the cabin structure;
- Between light vehicles with high speed (> 75 km/h of differential speed).
 Any other incident resulting in a significant deformation of the cabin structure, including any incident
requiring extrication.
ROAD TRANSPORT (HEAVY TRUCKS TRANSPORTING GOODS)
 Truck (or trailer) rollover – regardless of loss of product (risk for the driver).
 Vehicle unintentionally running-off the road in particular conditions (speed, fall in a ravine, fall in the
water).
 Collision:
- With another heavy vehicle;
- Against a fixed rigid obstacle, such as a wall, bridge pier, etc.
Excludes minor collisions occurring during low speed manoeuvres, collisions with minor damages
(mirrors, scrapes) and collisions when the truck is stationary or parked without a crew member in the
cabin.
 Any other transport incident resulting in a significant deformation of the cabin structure, especially any
incident requiring extrication.
 Axle/tire fire on LPG truck, and uncontrollable by extinguisher.
 Complete damage to a vehicle due to fire while transporting hazardous material.
 Loss of packed load with injury of a third party or loss of packed load with direct fall on a third-party
light vehicle (or a two-wheeled vehicle).
 Unmanageable loss of LPG containment during transport.
 Sudden and important loss of primary containment of the cargo (bulk hazardous material).
 Transport incident near a sensitive area (protected area, numerous dwellings, etc.) – to be assessed
according to the nature of the product transported and the sensitivity of the area.
 Vehicle moving without its driver.
MARITIME AND INLAND WATERWAYS TRANSPORT, OPERATIONS AT SEA
 Person falling overboard – man overboard during navigation or depending on aggravating
circumstances (fall from height, water temperature).
 Person stuck between a vessel and another structure.
 Breakdown of steering gear or engine (non-manoeuvring barge/ship) or drifting ship near coastline or
in a heavy traffic area.
 Grounding
 Collison and Allision [In a collision, one vessel strikes another ship, while an allision occurs when a
vessel hits a stationary object, such as a dock, bridge, oil platform, or drilling rig etc.]

Appendix 4: Non-Exhaustive List of High Potential (HIPO) Events
 Any collision between a vessel or aircraft / seaplane and an offshore installation which causes damage
to the installation, the vessel or the aircraft / seaplane.
 Fire aboard a ship or transport barge carrying flammable or explosive products (other than in living
quarters, and controlled and immediately extinguished).
 Multiple mooring lines rupture. Failure or accidental release of mooring lines causing breakaway from
the loading/discharging terminal irrespective whether the cargo operation is ongoing or not!
 Hydrocarbon spills reaching water (to be assessed according to the sensitivity of the area and the
quantity). (Refer Section 7.14.6 on Environmental Incident)
 Any incident which causes the loss of stability or buoyancy of a floating offshore installation or a vessel.
AIR TRANSPORT (AIRPLANE/HELICOPTER)
Incident or serious accident according to the International Civil Aviation Organisation (ICAO) or
UAE General Civil Aviation Authority (GCAA):
 Collision;
 Near-collision requiring an avoidance manoeuvre to avoid a boarding or a dangerous situation;
 Impact with the ground with no loss of control (Control Flight Into Terrain [CFIT]), only marginally
avoided;
 Aborted take-offs on a closed or engaged runway, on a taxiway (excluding authorized operations by
helicopters) or take-off from such a runway with very small margin in relation to obstacles;
 Landing or attempted landing on a closed or engaged runway;
 Incursion on the take-off/landing runway classified as severity A;
 Incidents during take-off or landing such as taking too short ground, overrunning or unintentional
lateral run-off of the runway;
 Strong deterioration compared to the expected performances during take-off or initial climbing;
 Fire or smoke in the passenger compartment, or cargo compartments, or any engine fire, even though
such fires were extinguished by the use of extinguishing agents;
 Event requiring the emergency use of oxygen reserves by the flight crew;
 Aircraft structural failure or engine disintegration;
 Multiple malfunctions of one or more aircraft systems seriously affecting the operation of the aircraft;
 Flight crew incapacitation in flight;
 Fuel situation that requires the pilot to declare an emergency;
 System failures, weather phenomena, operations outside the approved flight envelope or other
occurrences which caused or could have caused difficulties controlling the aircraft;
 Failure of more than one system in a redundancy system mandatory for flight guidance and navigation;
 The unintentional, or as an emergency measure, release of a slung load or any other load carried
external to the aircraft.
COLLECTIVE TRANSPORT OF PERSONS (MARITIME AND INLAND WATERWAYS)
 Breakage or sudden and unforeseeable failure of a vehicle’s element/part putting the safety of its
passengers at risk (e.g. brakes, steering).
PIPELINES
 Impact on a pipeline transporting hazardous material by a drilling, construction, anchoring machine,
etc., with or without consequences on the pipe’s resistance or the integrity of its coating.
 Excavation or potentially aggressive works (Refer to ADNOC Excavation Standard (HSE-OS-ST15):
- Undeclared, or unauthorized or non-compliant with the operator’s safety requirements; and
- Carried out by third parties, the operator itself or its contractors, with mechanical equipment;
and

- In the right of way or next to a pipeline which transports hazardous material with or without
impacting the pipe.
 Theft or attempted theft of product by hot-tapping or other method endangering the containment.
 Act of vandalism with actual or potential consequences > 3.
 Loss of primary containment – to be assessed according to API RP 754.
2.4 Other Risks
NATURAL DISASTER
 Natural phenomenon such as floods, erosions, landslides, earthquakes, lightning strikes, causing
minor damage to the structure, but which could have reasonably caused major damage or loss of
primary containment.
 Natural events (e.g. earthquake, tropical storm) leading to minor damage to the sites, but which can
be reasonably estimated that could have caused major damage.
 Flooding of the sites leading to the shutdown of facilities for safety or environmental reasons
(excluding service stations). The impossibility of access to a site is not in itself a high potential event.
EXTERNAL AGGRESSIONS
 Fire, explosion on a nearby site with identified potential escalation (domino) effect.
 Leak of a toxic product on a nearby site with identified potential consequences on the site.

Appendix 5: Explanation of Exceptions To Work-Relatedness
APPENDIX 5: EXPLANATION OF EXCEPTIONS TO WORK-RELATEDNESS
The following is an explanation of exceptions to work-related injuries and illnesses, as listed in Section 7.2.1.
1. At the time of the injury or illness, the employee was present in the work environment as a member
of the general public rather than as an employee.
An example of this exception would occur if an employee of a filling station visiting the convenience store
located in the filling station as a customer on a non-work day and was injured in a fall. This exception allows
the employer not to record cases that occur outside of the employment relationship when his or her
establishment is also a public place and a worker happens to be using the facility as a member of the
general public. In these situations, the injury or illness has nothing to do with the employee's work or the
employee's status as an employee, and it would therefore be not required for the recordkeeping system to
capture the case.
2. The injury or illness involves signs or symptoms that are observed at work but result solely from a
non-work-related event or exposure that occurs outside the work environment.
ADNOC incident recording and reporting is intended to capture cases that are caused by conditions or
exposures arising in the work environment. It is not required to capture cases that have no relationship with
the work environment. For this exception to apply, the work environment cannot have caused, contributed
to, or significantly aggravated the injury or illness. This exception is consistent with the interpretation that
any work-related contribution to the injury or illness makes the incident work-related, and its basis--that any
injury or illness to which work makes no actual contribution is not work-related.
An example of this type of injury would be a diabetic incident that occurs while an employee is working.
Because no event or exposure at work contributed in any way to the diabetic incident, the case is not
recordable. This exception allows the employer to exclude cases where an employee's non-work activities
are the sole cause of the injury or illness.
3. The injury or illness results solely from voluntary participation in a wellness, health, fitness program
or recreational activity such as blood donation, flu vaccination, exercise class, sports activity.
This exception allows the employer to exclude certain injury or illness cases that are related to personal
medical care, physical fitness activities and voluntary blood donations. The key words here are "solely" and
"voluntary." The work environment cannot have contributed to the injury or illness in any way for this
exception to apply, and participation in the wellness, fitness or recreational activities must be voluntary and
not a condition of employment.
This exception allows the employer to exclude cases that are related to personal matters of exercise,
recreation, medical examinations or participation in blood donation programs when they are voluntary and
are not being undertaken as a condition of work. For example, if a worker was injured while performing
aerobics in the company gymnasium during his or her lunch hour, the case would not be work-related.
On the other hand, if an employee who was assigned to manage the gymnasium was injured while teaching
an aerobics class, the injury would be work-related because the employee was working at the time of the
injury and the activity was not voluntary.
Similarly, if an employee suffered a severe reaction to a flu shot that was administered as part of a voluntary
inoculation program, the case would not be considered work-related; however, if an employee suffered a
reaction to medications administered to enable the employee to travel overseas on business, or the
employee had an illness reaction to a medication administered to treat a work-related injury, the case would
be considered work-related
4. The injury or illness is solely the result of an employee eating, drinking, or preparing food or drink
for personal consumption (whether bought on the employer's premises or brought in).
An example of the application of this exception would be a case where the employee injured himself or
herself by choking on a sandwich brought from home but eaten in the employer's establishment; such a
case would not be considered work-related under this exception.
In addition, a note to the exception makes clear that if an employee becomes ill as a result of ingesting
food contaminated by workplace contaminants such as lead, or contracts food poisoning from food items
provided by the employer, the case would be considered work-related (Refer to Section 7.8.4). As a result,
Standard No.: HSE-GA-ST04 Version No.: 1 Effective date: Dec., 2019 Page 119 of 184

HSE Management System
HSE Governance & Administration Standards
Incident Notification, Investigation & Reporting
Appendix 5: Explanation of Exceptions To Work-Relatedness
if an employee contracts food poisoning from a sandwich brought from home or purchased in the company
cafeteria and must take time off to recover, the case is not considered work related. On the other hand, if
an employee contracts food poisoning from a meal provided by the employer at a business meeting or
company function and takes time off to recover, the case would be considered work related.
Food provided or supplied by the employer does not include food purchased by the employee from the
company cafeteria, but does include food purchased by the employer from the company cafeteria for
business meetings or other company functions.
5. The injury or illness is solely the result of an employee doing personal tasks (unrelated to their
employment) at the establishment outside of the employee's assigned working hours.
This exception, allows employers to exclude cases related to personal injuries where the employee is using
the employer's establishment for purely personal reasons during his or her off-shift time.
For example, if an employee were using a meeting room at the employer's establishment outside of his or
her assigned working hours to hold a meeting for a civic group to which he or she belonged, and slipped
and fell in the hallway, the injury would not be considered work-related. On the other hand, if the employee
were at the employer's establishment outside his or her assigned working hours to attend a company
business meeting or a company training session, such a slip or fall would be work-related.
6. The injury or illness is solely the result of personal grooming, self-medication for a non-work-related
condition, or is intentionally self-inflicted.
This exception allows the employer to exclude cases related to personal hygiene, self-administered
medications and intentional self-inflicted injuries, such as attempted suicide.
For example, a burn injury from a hair dryer used at work to dry the employee's hair would not be work-
related. Similarly, a negative reaction to a medication brought from home to treat a non-work condition
would not be considered a work-related illness, even though it first manifested at work.
7. The injury or illness is caused by a motor vehicle crash and occurs on a company parking lot or
company access road while the employee is commuting to or from work.
This exception allows the employer to exclude cases where an employee is injured in a motor vehicle crash
while commuting from work to home or from home to work or while on a personal errand.
For example, if an employee was injured in a car accident while arriving at work or while leaving the
company's property at the end of the day, or while driving on his or her lunch hour to run an errand
(to take a short trip to do a specific thing e.g. pick up child from school), the case would not be considered
work-related. On the other hand, if an employee was injured in a car incident while leaving the property to
purchase supplies for the employer, the case would be work-related.
Some injuries and illnesses that occur in company parking lots are clearly caused by work conditions or
activities e.g., being struck by a car while painting parking space indicators on the pavement of the lot,
slipping on ice permitted to accumulate in the lot by the employer--and by their nature point to conditions
that could be corrected to improve workplace safety and health.
8. The illness is the common cold or flu (Note: contagious diseases such as tuberculosis, brucellosis,
hepatitis A, or plague are work-related if the employee is infected at work).
This exception allows the employer to exclude cases of common cold or flu, even if contracted while the
employee was at work. However, in the case of other infectious diseases such as tuberculosis, brucellosis,
and hepatitis C, employers must evaluate reports of such illnesses for work relationship, just as they would
any other type of injury or illness.
9. The illness is a mental illness. Mental illness is not work-related unless the employee voluntarily
provides the employer with an opinion from a physician or other licensed health care professional
with appropriate training and experience (psychiatrist, psychologist, psychiatric nurse practitioner,
etc.) stating that the employee has a work-related mental illness. (Refer to Section 7.2.3)
Recording work-related mental illnesses involves several unique issues, including the difficulty of detecting,
diagnosing and verifying mental illnesses; and the sensitivity and privacy concerns raised by mental
illnesses. Therefore, the rule requires employers to record only those mental illnesses verified by a health
Standard No.: HSE-GA-ST04 Version No.: 1 Effective date: Dec., 2019 Page 120 of 184

HSE Management System
HSE Governance & Administration Standards
Incident Notification, Investigation & Reporting
Appendix 5: Explanation of Exceptions To Work-Relatedness
care professional with appropriate training and experience in the treatment of mental illness, such as a
psychiatrist, psychologist, or psychiatric nurse practitioner.
The employer is under no obligation to seek out information on mental illnesses from its employees, and
employers are required to consider mental illness cases only when an employee voluntarily presents the
employer with an opinion from the health care professional that the employee has a mental illness and that
it is work related.
In the event that the employer does not believe the reported mental illness is work-related, the employer
may refer the case to a physician or other licensed health care professional for a second opinion. Work-
related mental illnesses, like other illnesses, must be recorded only when they meet the severity criteria. In
addition, for mental illnesses, the employee's identity must be protected by omitting the employee's name
and instead entering "privacy concern case."

Appendix 6: Examples of Establishing Work-Relatedness & Reportable Incidents
APPENDIX 6: EXAMPLES OF ESTABLISHING WORK-RELATEDNESS & REPORTABLE INCIDENTS
1. Work Relatedness for Injuries & Illness during Meal Period
An injury or illness to an Employee during a meal period (lunch break etc.) is work-related if it occurs
on Company's Premises (Section 7.3.1), unless one of the specific Exceptions to Work-relatedness
listed in Section 7.2.1 applies.
 An injury or illness to an Employee that occurs off the Company's Premises during a meal period
is work-related if the employee is engaged in a work-related activity, is at the location as a
condition of employment, or is on work-related travel status.
 An injury or illness that is solely the result of an employee eating, drinking or preparing food or
drink for personal consumption (regardless of whether bought on the Company premises or
brought in) is not considered work-related.
 However, if the employee is made ill by ingesting food contaminated by workplace contaminants
(such as lead), or gets food poisoning from food purchased by or paid for by the Company, the
case would be considered work-related.
Example 1: Employee cuts his/her hand peeling an apple while eating lunch in his/her office. This
would not be considered a work-related injury as it falls under the exclusion for preparing food for
personal consumption.
Example 2: Employee is injured from choking on a sandwich brought from home but eaten in a
Company establishment. Such a case would not be considered work-related under the same
exception.
Example 3: Same situation as above, except the employee purchased the sandwich in the Company
cafeteria. This situation would still not be work-related as the exception still applies.
Example 4: A supervisor decides to buy pizza to reward the performance of his group. Several of the
group members become sick from food poisoning. This would be considered work-related as it results
from consumption of food provided by the Company.
Example 5: An employee fell down and injured while present in the employer's lunchroom. This case
would be considered work-related if employee was present as a condition of employment and the
investigation establishes contribution of inadequate or missing employers provided controls (e.g.
slippery floor, uneven surface, obstruction in walkway etc.).
Example 6: Employee is injured at an off-site restaurant during a Company-paid and required business
luncheon. This would be a work-related injury.
Example 7: Employee is injured at an off-site restaurant during a Company-paid, but entirely voluntary,
luncheon to celebrate another employee's retirement. The employee is not engaged in a work-related
activity, is not present as a condition of employment, and is not on travel status. This would not be a
work-related injury.
Example 8: An employee had an allergic reaction (food allergy) at a Company provided lunch while
attending a meeting. The employee was taken by ambulance to a hospital where treatment was
received from a licensed health care professional. There was no evidence of food contamination and
no other employees became ill. This would not be work-related because the resulting illness was
from a food allergy and was not food poisoning or workplace contamination.
2. Pre Existing Condition
 Example 1: An employee is walking on an obstruction free floor to the lunch room during the
workday when his left knee buckles and he falls down, resulting in Medical Treatment and days
away from work. LHCP provides documentation that the employee has a personal pre-existing
condition that surfaced in the work environment that was solely the result of non-work related
event, exposure or condition.
This injury would not be work-related (even though there appeared to be an identifiable
workplace event – a fall ) because the injury was solely the result of a personal pre-existing
Standard No.: HSE-GA-ST04 Version No.: 1 Effective date: Dec., 2019 Page 122 of 184

HSE Management System
HSE Governance & Administration Standards
Incident Notification, Investigation & Reporting
Appendix 6: Examples of Establishing Work-Relatedness & Reportable Incidents
condition AND LHCP provided evidence to support that the injury did not result from an identifiable
event or exposure in the work environment, but only manifested itself during work.
 Example 2: An employee begins to climb a fixed straight ladder in a processing unit and
experiences shoulder pain in the shoulder that was injured last year in a personal auto incident.
She is evaluated by LHCP and requires medical treatment. She tells LHCP that the shoulder pain
has been intermittent and occurs on and off the job whenever she overexerts it. LHCP documents
that medical treatment was required for a strain consistent with climbing ladders and not a result
of intermittent pain due to the off job incident.
This injury would be work-related because the injury was not solely the result of a non-work
related event, exposure or condition or a significant aggravation of the personal pre-existing condition.
3. Work Environment – Present as Condition of Employment
Example: An employee visits the washroom to put on makeup. On the way out of the washroom,
employee was injured with a cut whilst opening the washroom door. Employee was injured after
personal grooming in company premises where employee was present as a condition of employment.
This would be a work-related incident if the investigation establishes contribution of inadequate or
missing employers provided controls (e.g. defective doors, sharp edges, slippery floor, uneven surface,
etc.).
Other examples (subject to work relatedness exception in Section 7.2.1) include when Workers are:
 Hosting a service anniversary luncheon or a retirement dinner off Company Premises
 Attending a Company-paid and required business luncheon
 Hosting business clients at a sporting event
 Attending work-related training provided by a third party
 Living as required in Company-controlled accommodations
4. Work Relatedness for Cases Involving Mental Illness
The following are offered only as examples of the logic employed in determining work-relatedness in
mental illness cases. In each case, an opinion from a qualified LHCP was provided voluntarily by the
Worker.
Example 1: A Worker is diagnosed with severe depression by a psychiatrist and is hospitalized for two
weeks as part of the treatment. The psychiatrist writes a letter to the supervisor stating that the
Worker's current work assignment is too stressful, that it is contributing to the condition, and that the
Worker needs to be reassigned.
On review of the case Investigation Team found:
1. The clinical information provided confirms that the reported illness (Depression) meets diagnostic
criteria
2. The Worker was diagnosed with clinical depression three years earlier and has a number of other
significant ongoing life stressors (marriage difficulties, financial problems and recent death of a
family member). During this same period, the Worker’s work performance and attendance
dramatically declined to the point that the Worker was counselled and placed on a performance
improvement plan.
CONCLUSION: Not Work-Related. OH Specialist determined that the evidence did not support the
assertion that “stress” at work caused the Worker’s mental illness. The underlying condition
(depression) was not caused by work.
Example 2: A Worker becomes agitated after a performance review and does not return to work for
two weeks. The Worker’s private psychiatrist states in writing that the Worker has a work-related mental
illness and should be excused from work for that duration.
Standard No.: HSE-GA-ST04 Version No.: 1 Effective date: Dec., 2019 Page 123 of 184
