HSE Governance & Administration Standards
Incident Notification, Investigation & Reporting
Appendix 6: Examples of Establishing Work-Relatedness & Reportable Incidents
The Investigation Team found:
1. The clinical information provided does not meet diagnostic criteria for a mental illness. No specific
clinical diagnosis was given.
2. The clinical records obtained from the psychiatrist indicated that the Worker reported frustration
with their current job assignment, dislike for their supervisor, and complaints about unrealistic job
demands. The doctor recommended counselling on conflict resolution and time away from work
to attend counselling and resolve feelings.
CONCLUSION: Not Work-Related. The person was not diagnosed with a “mental illness”
Example 3: A supervisor received a letter from a Worker’s psychiatrist stating that the Worker’s
ongoing absence from work is because of a work-related mental illness and recommended the Worker
be reassigned to work under a different supervisor. As part of the incident investigation, the supervisor
stated that the Worker often had to be reprimanded for failing to meet deadlines. The supervisor
thought the Worker was probably lying about being ill to get time off work, because the Worker’s
vacation entitlement had already been used.
The Investigation Team found:
1. The clinical information confirmed that the condition met applicable diagnostic criteria for a mental
illness (Major Depressive Disorder and Adjustment Disorder).
2. The Worker had no other significant medical, family or social history.
3. The Worker’s symptoms started four weeks after starting a new assignment with the Worker’s
current supervisor.
 Workplace factors the Worker associated with the onset of the illness were verbal abuse,
threatening and demeaning remarks, unfair criticism, and denial of resources needed to
complete assigned tasks. After seven weeks in the new assignment, going to work caused
such anxiety that the Worker started taking frequent vacation days to avoid the workplace.
After ten weeks, one morning the Worker became panic stricken, could not face entering the
building, and went home. The Worker had a first appointment with the psychiatrist two weeks
later.
4. The Worker was being treated with an anti-depressant, an anti-anxiety medication and was
attending regular counselling sessions.
CONCLUSION: Work-Related. The case met applicable diagnostic criteria and the onset of
symptoms were consistent with work-related experiences.
5. Distinguishing between Injuries and Illnesses
 The basic definition of an occupational injury includes those cases that result from a work accident
or from an exposure involving a single instantaneous incident (or a number of linked events close
together in time) in the work environment. Contact with a hot surface or a caustic chemical which
produces a burn in a single instantaneous moment of contact is an injury. Sunburn or welding flash
burns that result from prolonged or repeated exposure to sunrays or welding flashes are
considered illnesses. Similarly, a one-time blow that damages the tendons of the hand is
considered an injury; while repeated trauma or repetitious movement which produces tenosynovitis
is considered an illness. The basic determinant is the single-incident concept. If the case resulted
from something that happened in one instant (or a number of linked events close together in time),
it is classified as an injury. If the case resulted from something that was not instantaneous, such
as prolonged exposure to hazardous substances or other environmental factors, it is considered
an illness.
 Carpal tunnel syndrome is a condition involving compression of the median nerve in the wrist that
results in tingling, discomfort, and numbness in the thumb, index, and long fingers. Because work-
related carpal tunnel syndrome cases almost always result from repetitious movement, they should
be classified as occupational illnesses.
Standard No.: HSE-GA-ST04 Version No.: 1 Effective date: Dec., 2019 Page 124 of 184

HSE Management System
HSE Governance & Administration Standards
Incident Notification, Investigation & Reporting
Appendix 6: Examples of Establishing Work-Relatedness & Reportable Incidents
 Conditions resulting from one-time exposure to chemicals are injuries. If a worker is exposed to a
chemical inhalation for more than one breath, the exposure is non-instantaneous. A one-time
chemical exposure lasting 45 minutes is an illness, because it is non-instantaneous.
 Eye irritation apparently caused by non-instantaneous exposure to chemical vapours is an illness.
 Ear conditions associated with descent of an aircraft (e.g., barotitis media) is an illness because
the change in air pressure during the descent occurred over time.
 Ear conditions associated with flying in aircraft (e.g., barotitis media). It is an injury if caused by
instantaneous depressurization. It is an illness if developed over time from exposure to minor
changes in air pressure during descent or multiple descents.
 Eye conditions caused by exposure due to weld flash. It is an injury if it is the result of an
instantaneous exposure to a welding arc. It is an illness when developed over time (non-
instantaneous) as the result of exposure to welding arcs.
 Reaction to a hepatitis B vaccination is considered an injury because the original exposure (the
inoculation) was instantaneous.
 An infection resulting from a laceration should be classified as an injury because the classification
is based on the original event—the laceration—not on the subsequent developments.
 All blood borne pathogen cases are classified as injuries, whether or not they are triggered by an
instantaneous event.
 All conditions resulting from animal bites, such as insect or snake bites, are considered injuries.
These include contagious diseases related to body contact exposure (e.g., scabies or lice).
 A chemical burn resulting from a single drop of a corrosive which appears several hours after
exposure is considered an injury.
 If a sprain or strain was caused by a non-instantaneous event or exposure, it is an illness. This
holds true for all conditions (e.g., hearing loss, hernias, and burns), except for back cases. Where
a worker lifts trays 50 times per day and feels pain in the shoulder, the case is an injury if it was
caused by a one-time lifting of the trays, and an illness if it was caused by the cumulative effect of
lifting trays. The employer must evaluate the circumstances and make a determination, giving
strong weight to any medical opinion.
5.1 Examples for Reportable Occupational Illnesses
 Any diagnosed occupational illness reported to the employer is reported, whether or not medical
treatment is given or lost workdays are involved.
 Occupational illnesses do not have to be diagnosed by a LHCP to be reported. “Diagnosis” is
commonly defined as the act or process of detecting and deciding the nature of a diseased
condition by examination of the symptoms. Diagnosis may be by a LHCP or registered nurse.
 Hearing loss may be classified as either an injury or an illness, depending upon the type of event
or exposure that caused the loss. If the hearing loss resulted from or was aggravated by an
instantaneous exposure, it is considered an injury, and is reportable only if it involves medical
treatment, loss of consciousness, restriction of work or motion, or transfer to another job. If the
hearing loss resulted from or was aggravated by anything other than an instantaneous exposure it
should be classified as an occupational illness.
 Work-related heart attacks are classified as illnesses because they normally do not result from
work accidents or single instantaneous incidents in the work environment. Heart attacks must
satisfy the same requirements for work relationship as any other type of illness before they are
reported. Heart attacks are not necessarily reported if they occur in the work environment, but
rather that they must result from an exposure in the work environment.
 Variations in the characteristics of particular employees or their susceptibility to various illnesses
do not affect decisions of incident classification or reporting.
Standard No.: HSE-GA-ST04 Version No.: 1 Effective date: Dec., 2019 Page 125 of 184

HSE Management System
HSE Governance & Administration Standards
Incident Notification, Investigation & Reporting
Appendix 6: Examples of Establishing Work-Relatedness & Reportable Incidents
 Employee complaints of such common subjective symptoms as general malaise, headache, and/or
nausea, etc., are not reported as cases of illnesses if there are no indications that the symptoms
are work-related.
 Cases are reportable where medical treatment was clearly required, but for one reason or another,
was not actually provided.
 Injuries resulting in fractures are usually reportable because they are not minor in nature and
ordinarily require medical treatment or involve restriction of work or motion.
 Injuries that result in chipped or broken teeth are reportable because they ordinarily require medical
treatment.
 Work-related situations where an employee damages a prosthetic device, such as an artificial arm
or leg are reportable if they involve either some form of medical treatment or restriction of work or
motion.
 More than one follow-up visit to a doctor for minor cuts or burns is not reportable if the second visit
is simply for observation or to change an adhesive or small bandage. It would be reportable,
however, if any medical treatment were provided.
 If an employee is injured and loses work time in travelling to or from a doctor’s office for a medical
examination, this does not necessarily result in a reportable injury. Injuries should be evaluated on
the extent of medical treatment required, not on the amount of time spent seeking treatment. If the
examination revealed that no medical treatment was required, the case would not be reportable.
Restriction of work or motion concerns the employee’s ability to perform normal job duties; it does
not include loss of work time for travel to or from a doctor’s office.
 Tetanus shots provided for minor scratches should not be regarded as reportable unless other
treatment was provided. Rabies vaccinations constitute medical treatment since they are
considered absolutely necessary and involve a series of injections far more extensive than the
concept of first aid.
 Treatments given by a chiropractor for a work-related injury are considered to be reportable
medical treatment, as are any other required medical treatments by any medical professional.
 Use of prescription medications to treat an occupational injury normally constitutes medical
treatment. However, it is considered first aid when a single dose of a prescription medication is
administered on the first visit for minor injury or discomfort. A “single dose” is the measured quantity
of a therapeutic agent to be taken at one time. The best available treatment should not be reduced
or eliminated in order to avoid reporting.
 If prescription drugs are provided to employees solely for psychological care in connection with a
job-related injury should be considered medical treatment.
 If an employee suffers an adverse reaction in response to a nonprescription medication provided
as treatment for a minor injury, the case is deemed to be a reportable injury. This case should be
considered an injury since the case determination must relate back to the original event. The
affected employee would not have suffered the adverse reaction to the medication but for the
occupational injury. Initially, the case was not reportable because the provision of a nonprescription
medication does not constitute medical treatment. THE CASE MAY NOW BE REPORTABLE. To
be reportable, the adverse reaction must have been serious enough to require additional medical
treatment or involve loss of consciousness, restriction of work or motion, or transfer to another job.
Standard No.: HSE-GA-ST04 Version No.: 1 Effective date: Dec., 2019 Page 126 of 184

HSE Governance & Administration Standards
Incident Notification, Investigation & Reporting
Appendix 6: Examples of Establishing Work-Relatedness & Reportable Incidents
5.2 Burns
Burn cases as applicable are reportable to ADNOC Group HSE Function based on the treatment based
classification and/or as per the below mentioned categories. Burns may result from exposure to heat,
chemicals, electricity, intense light and radiation.
a) First Degree Burns:
Only the outer layer of skin is damaged, characterized by reddening of skin, pain and sometimes
swelling.
b) Second Degree Burns (Partial Thickness):
Injure the epidermis and damage the superficial underlying tissues. Characterized similar to first
degree burns but blistering of skin may also occur. Intense pain, reddening and a mottled skin
appearance are common.
When a blister (one inch or large in diameter) forms three days after the burn occurs, a first-degree
burn must be upgraded to a second-degree burn. All cases resuting in any blister(s) of one inch or
more in diameter as confirmed by LHCP are considered reportable irrespective of treatment provided.
c) Third Degree Burns (Full Thickness):
Most serious category of burns where the entire skin thickness is affected, with blackened or
blanched (grey-white) skin. Treatment includes debridement (removal of burned skin) and skin
grafts. All cases are reportable irrespective of treatment provided.
6. Examples of Restricted Work Cases (RWC)
Example 1:
A pipe-fitter twists his ankle while performing pipefitting work. He is taken to a hospital and x-rays
reveal that there are no broken bones. He does not require any medical treatment (therefore this is
not a medical treatment case) and the doctor allows him to return to work but restricts the worker from
doing pipefitting work. He returns to work the next day and is put to work reviewing drawings in the
construction office. This would be classified as a Restricted Work Case because the worker is unable
to perform a major part of his job function as a pipe-fitter.
Example 2:
A welder receives a muscle strain to his leg while welding on an above ground platform. He is checked
out by the appropriate medical personnel but does not require medical treatment (i.e. the incident is
not a medical treatment case). He returns to work but is unable to perform welds that require him to
weld above ground (Doctor has told him not to climb a ladder). He is put to work welding pipe spools
in the fabrication shop at site. This would be classified as a Restricted Work case because of the
Doctor's restrictions and therefore would be a recordable incident.
Example 3:
A welder receives a muscle strain to his leg while welding on an above ground platform. He is checked
out by the appropriate medical personnel but does not require medical treatment (i.e. the incident is
not a medical treatment case). He returns to work and is physically capable of climbing ladders to weld
above ground (Doctor has told him to take it easy on that leg, but hasn't indicated any restrictions). As
a precautionary measure in the interest of not unnecessarily aggravating the leg, he is put to work
welding pipe spools in the fabrication shop at site.
This would not be classified as a Restricted Work case and would not be a recordable incident.
In example 2 where a doctor has indicated a restriction, this should clearly be considered a Restricted
Work case, and the employer should get doctor's clearance that the restriction is removed before
having the worker do this type of work. In example 3 judgement concludes the worker is capable of
performing normal duties, & proactive management has been used to avoid any possible aggravation.
Example 4:
Employees are at a business dinner meeting, required as part of their normal job. One develops a
food related stomach problem, and arrives at work the next day in a condition in which he cannot
perform his job safely. His supervisor sends him home to recover. This would be a work-related lost
time illness. Had he been in a safety-critical position, and his supervisor temporarily assigned him to
less sensitive duties, it would be classed as a restricted work illness.
Standard No.: HSE-GA-ST04 Version No.: 1 Effective date: Dec., 2019 Page 127 of 184

HSE Governance & Administration Standards
Incident Notification, Investigation & Reporting
Appendix 6: Examples of Establishing Work-Relatedness & Reportable Incidents
7. Non-Operating Heavy Machinery / Vessels
Work relatedness for incidents involving heavy machinery (e.g. mobile crane, diggers, pile drivers,
winching equipment), marine vessels etc. while non-operating and either on standby / suspended or
finished work status within the work environment shall be carefully established as per requirements of
this standard and considering the contractual obligations.
8. Situations Where Establishing Work Relationship is Not Clear
As the specific circumstances of each case weighs in the outcome, the following are offered only as
examples of the logic employed in such cases.
Example 1: While working to meet a tight deadline, a Worker experiences pain in the wrist after multiple
hours of continuous mouse usage. The Worker visits a Licensed Health Care Professional (LHCP),
who prescribes prescription anti-inflammatory medication. During the incident investigation, the
Worker indicates that there are other possible causes of the wrist pain, including extensive typing at
home for non-work purposes.
CONCLUSION: Work-related. GC OH staff concludes that it is more likely than not that the intense
usage of the mouse at work contributed to the pain in the wrist.
Example 2: A Worker reports pain in the shoulder while using the mouse at work. The Worker visits
an LHCP, who prescribes Prescription Medication. During the incident investigation, the Worker
indicates that there are other possible causes of the shoulder pain, including extensive gardening at
home the evening before.
CONCLUSION: Not Work-related. Outside activity is likely the cause, and it is not likely that using
the mouse at work contributed to the pain in the shoulder.
Example 3: A Worker with no history of back problems was inspecting equipment. This necessitated
bending from the waist. While performing this action, the Worker felt a twinge in the back. The next
day the Worker visited an LHCP, who recommended days away from work.
CONCLUSION: Work-related. Since the Worker had no other contributing factors to the development
of back pain, the event at work was deemed more likely than not to be a discernible contributing factor.
Example 4: A Worker with a personal pre-existing back condition receives once-weekly medical
treatment. While at work, the Worker lifts a box which aggravates the back condition to the point where
medical treatment twice per week is now required.
CONCLUSION: Work-Related. A work event or exposure “Significantly Aggravated” the pre-existing
condition.
Example 5: A Worker exercises with free weights before going to work at a refinery. Later that day,
closing a valve caused intense and immediate wrist pain. An LHCP diagnoses a sprain and gives a
prescription for Prescription Medication. The Worker reported experiencing some mild discomfort in
the wrist after the morning exercise.
CONCLUSION: Work-related. Since closing of valve caused intense and immediate pain, the event
at work was deemed to be a discernible contributing factor.
Example 6: Worker contracts diarrhea while on a 5-day overseas trip to a Company facility, where
several personal meals were eaten at local restaurants.
CONCLUSION: Not Work-Related. There is no clear link to the workplace. Hence, it is not “more
likely than not” that an exposure in the workplace was a cause.
Example 7: Two Workers that work in different areas of a refinery contract Tuberculosis during the
same year. There is no clearly defined workplace exposure (e.g. close quarters, sharing unclean
respirators).
CONCLUSION: Not Work-Related. There is no clear source of infection in the workplace, and it is
not “more likely than not” that a work exposure was the cause.
Standard No.: HSE-GA-ST04 Version No.: 1 Effective date: Dec., 2019 Page 128 of 184

Appendix 6: Examples of Establishing Work-Relatedness & Reportable Incidents
9. EXAMPLES OF REPORTABLE INCIDENTS
9.1 Incidents Occurring at Company Premises
 Injuries or Illnesses occurring on Company Premises (including Company restrooms, hallways and
stairwells, parking areas and access roads, or non-public cafeterias) are presumed to be work-
related, unless a specific exception applies (Section 7.2.1).
- A worker who gets food poisoning from eating company’s cafeteria food at work is considered
to have a work-related condition.
- Food poisoning from food brought in from outside for a business meeting is work-related.
- Food poisoning from food brought in from the worker's home is not work-related.
 Injuries incurred while a worker is intoxicated at work are work-related despite the intoxication.
 Injuries from "Acts of Nature" such as being struck by lightning while at work are considered work-
related.
 Incidents involving workers at on-site physical fitness centers are not work-related, even though
the employer encourages participation in fitness programs and subsidizes the costs of participation,
and only workers may use the centers. If participation is mandatory, then any resulting incidents
are work-related.
 The following situations are considered Work-Related when they occur in the Work Environment:
- Workplace Violence: An injury inflicted by or arising from violence (regardless of the motivation
for the violence) Example: Worker injured in fistfight with other Worker or injured during
robbery by third party.
- Loss of Temper: An injury resulting from loss of temper (e.g., banging fist on table, punching a
cabinet).
- Altercation: If a disagreement strictly related to work leads to an act of aggression, the injury
of the person being hit is included in the statistics. However, any injury to the person who dealt
the blow is not taken into account. In the case of a fight unrelated to work (personal dispute),
none of the injuries are included in the statistical reporting. Altercation is a heated or angry
dispute; noisy argument or controversy.
 Marine Non-Product Vessels (MNPvs) that are:
- Connected (e.g., via loading hoses or mooring (anchorage) lines) to an offshore Company
facility
- Within an “Exclusion Zone”
- Contracted Marine Non-Product Vessels (MNPVs) moored or otherwise held stationary or
planned to be held stationary for more than five consecutive days (typical examples:
accommodation vessels, dedicated fuel barges, and drilling / workover / completion rigs)
Exception: Such vessels are not considered Company Premise (i.e., are not reportable) during
periods when conducting work ONLY on behalf of the vessel owner (e.g., performing vessel preventive
maintenance, general housekeeping, marine re-certifications, etc.). If ANY work is conducted on behalf
of the Company, the MNPV is considered Company Premise. Refer to 9.3 in this Appendix for more on
MNPVs
 Offshore Premise
- Incidents involving aircraft maintenance crew while on company’s premise for repair and
maintenance of a defective (grounded) aircraft are not work related. Refer to definition of “Third
Party”
Example: a helicopter operated by an Air Charter Service Provider grounded at an offshore
platform due to technical fault. The service provider brought the maintenance crew for repair.
Any injury to the maintenance crew while performing maintenance on the aircraft is not

Appendix 6: Examples of Establishing Work-Relatedness & Reportable Incidents
reportable as per the definition of Third Party (having no direct contract to provide services to
the client).
 An employee was inspecting the rotor head of an aircraft that had been involved in a heavy landing.
This necessitated him standing on a platform and bending from the waist around 40 degrees to
inspect the machine. There was no lifting, twisting, or other manual handling hazard involved. As
he performed this action, he felt a twinge in his back and went and sat down. During the day and
prior to the activity of inspection he had been performing normal office based duties. He reported
that he had never had back pain of this intensity in the past. The next day the IP visited his LHCP
who recommended days away from work.
Work related – yes. An event or exposure in the work environment either caused or contributed
to the resulting condition
9.2 Marine Product Transportation Vessels
These vessels are used to transport bulk fuel, crude, chemical and LPG / LNG product. These are
operated or managed by the relevant Group Companies.
Incident ownership (recording and reporting): For Marine Product Transportation Vessel
Contractors, the holder of the vessel’s International Safety Management Code Document of
Compliance (DOC) is required to steward vessel safety performance and incidents. Contractors
working on Marine Product Transportation vessels that are:
 Company owned and operated or bareboat (owned by third-parties but manned, fueled and
operated by ADNOC Group) are reportable
 Company owned and operated by third-parties are reportable
 Owned by a third-party (vessel operator or owner holds the DOC) are not reportable at any
time. These include:
- Time charters (vessel leased by an owner to the charterer for a given period, and
under the lease agreement, the charterer decides the voyages to be made and the
cargoes to be carried)
- Spot / voyage charters (vessel is employed for a single voyage).
9.3 Marine Non-Product Vessels (MNPVs)
These are defined as marine vessels that do not meet the definition of Marine Product Transportation
Vessels. These include personnel transportation vessels, supply vessels, work boats, anchor handling
vessels, tugs, work barges, pipe-laying vessels, installation vessels, hook-up vessels, seismic vessels,
security vessels, stand-by vessels, drilling rigs/ships, completion rigs, and their associated small craft
and lifeboats. Refer to No. 10.1 in this Appendix for more on MNPVs
Incident ownership (recording and reporting): While the Company provides due diligence when
selecting vessels via pre-mobilization and periodic inspections, the Company, often has little direct
influence on the MNPV’s activities when not on Company Premises, except in the case of personnel
transportation vessels which are carefully stewarded. As such:
- Incidents involving personnel transportation vessel crews – Crew working in
Company/Reportable Contractor-provided personnel transportation vessels (e.g., crew
boats/surfers) are recordable / reportable when travelling to or from an offshore Company
Premise and when they are on Company Premise (i.e., inside of a 500 Meter Exclusion Zone or
otherwise moored or connected to the offshore facility).
- Incidents to other MNPV crews – crews of MNPVs other than personnel transportation vessels
are only recordable / reportable when they are on Company Premise (i.e., inside of a 500 Meter
Exclusion Zone or otherwise moored or connected to the offshore facility).

Appendix 6: Examples of Establishing Work-Relatedness & Reportable Incidents
9.4 Land or Air Charter Contractors
“Full Time Dedicated Charter (Land or Air)” (also known in common use as “term charter”, “term
contractor”, or “dedicated charter/carrier”) is a land or air charter that is essentially dedicated for
Company use.
Incidents involving full time dedicated land or air charter drivers (and their crews) are recordable and
reportable (as per severity level) while en-route to a Company facility, at a Company facility, delivering
Company product/personnel, or returning to their home base.
“Spot / Voyage Charter (Land or Air)” (also known as “common carrier” or “time charter”) is a land
or air charter that is non-exclusive to Company use, and other third parties can and do regularly use
the same carrier and carrier's equipment.
Incidents involving Land and Air Spot Charter or Common Carrier drivers and crews are
recordable and reportable (as per severity level) only when on Company Premises including operation
sites, terminals, well-sites, etc. At other times, the Company does not control their work activity.
Note: Spot / Voyage charters for which the Company closely oversees the work as per a written
contract should be treated as a Full Time Dedicated Charter for recordkeeping purposes.
Example: a remote location helicopter transport service where the Company is not the only customer,
but per a written contract closely stewards flight schedules, working conditions and/or safe practices
9.5 Incidents Involving Suppliers
Incidents involving suppliers (i.e., providers of materials that are not specifically under contract to
provide services) are recordable and reportable (as per severity level) specifically when serving a
business need on Company Premises.
Examples of supplier activities for which supplier workers are considered work related for the entire
duration while on Company Premise include:
- Field erection of purchased equipment
- Delivery of materials that require connection to the facility’s (e.g.: process / operation / well
site) equipment / systems during the delivery
Note: If the delivery is of the loaded tank trailer (or other container containing such materials)
only and no connection to the facility systems is made during the delivery, this constitutes a
simple delivery and is not work related.
- Equipment (non-office) repair (Note: Per definition of excluded contractors, office equipment
service personnel are excluded)
- Sales personnel or other professionals providing services (e.g., testing, fitting, training) beyond
simple delivery. Examples include:
 Fitting, equipment training, and user orientation for safety eyeglasses, flame retardant
clothing uniforms, footwear
 Provision of training.

HSE Governance & Administration Standards
Incident Notification, Investigation & Reporting
Appendix 6: Examples of Establishing Work-Relatedness & Reportable Incidents
9.6 Examples of Process Safety Events
The following are examples of reportable Process Safety Events:
Example 1: A scaffold builder experiences a days away from work injury after falling from a scaffold ladder while
evacuating from a LOPC on nearby equipment. This is a Tier 1 PSE.
Example 2: An operator walks past a steam trap and the operator’s ankle is burned by the steam resulting in a
days away from work injury. This is a Tier 1 PSE because even though the LOPC was steam (vs hydrocarbon or
chemical), the physical state of the material was such that it caused a day away from work injury. Unplanned or
uncontrolled releases of non-toxic and non-flammable materials are within the scope of this recommended practice.
Example 3: A reactor vessel has been intentionally purged with nitrogen. A contractor bypasses safety controls,
enters the enclosure and dies. This is not a PSE because there was no unplanned or uncontrolled LOPC, but it
would be recorded on the Company’s injury and illness log.
Same as above, except that nitrogen inadvertently leaked into the enclosure. This would be a Tier 1 PSE because
there was a fatality associated with an unplanned or uncontrolled LOPC
Example 4: An internal deflagration in a vessel causes equipment damage $100,000, but there was no loss of
containment. Is this a PSE?
While this is a serious process event and should be investigated as such, it does not meet the definition of a Tier
PSE because there was no LOPC involved.
Example 5: Hydrocarbon fumes migrate into the QA/QC laboratory located within the facility and results in a fire
with $5000 damage. The source of the hydrocarbon fumes is the oily water sewer system. This incident is a Tier
2 PSE since the LOPC was from the process and resulted in a Tier 2 consequence (a fire that results in a direct
cost greater than $2500).
Example 6: A water surge drum is filled with no discharge pumps operating; the drum is over pressured and
large crack opens on the bottom of the drum releasing water. There are no injuries, but the damage to the drum
is $35,000. Does this over pressure meet the definition of an explosion meaning this would be a Tier 2 PSE?
This overpressure does not meet the definition of an explosion because there was no release of energy that
causes a pressure discontinuity or blast wave. A company may choose to record this event as a Tier 3 Other
LOPC
Example 7: Ten bbl of gasoline (1150 kg, 2530 lb) leak from piping onto concrete and the gasoline doesn't reach
soil or water. Facility personnel estimate that the leak occurred within one hour. This is a Tier 1 PSE because
there was a LOPC of 7 bbl (1000 kg, 2200 lb) or more of liquid with a flash point <23 °C (73 °F) in any one-hour
period. If the spill had been less than 7 bbl, but equal to or greater than 0.7 bbl (100 kg 220 lb), it would be a Tier
2 PSE.
Example 8: A maintenance contractor opens a process valve and gets sprayed with less than the Tier 1 or Tier
2 TQ of sulfuric acid resulting in a severe burn and days away from work injury. This is a Tier 1 PSE because it is
an unplanned or uncontrolled LOPC that resulted in a days away from work injury. If this incident had resulted in
a recordable injury, it would be a Tier 2 PSE.
Example 9: The regenerative thermal oxidizer (RTO) is typically fed materials with low concentrations of
flammable gas (LEL). For the incident in question, materials with higher than normal LEL were fed into the RTO.
The combustion of the higher LEL materials caused an overpressure of the outer structure of the RTO, resulting
in a rupture of the box. The direct cost of the incident exceeds $100,000. Is this a Tier 1 PSE even though the
explosion was not specifically caused by an LOPC?
The event did result an LOPC; therefore, the definition of a PSE is satisfied. The $100,000 direct cost damage
classifies the PSE as a Tier 1 event. The LOPC does not have to occur first.
Example 10: An operator purposely drains 20 bbl of material with a flash point > 60 °C (140 °F) at a
temperature below its flash point into an oily water collection system within one hour as part of a vessel
cleaning operation. Since the drainage is planned and controlled and the collection system is designed
for such service, this is not a reportable Tier 1 or 2 PSE.
If the material released had been unplanned or uncontrolled and flowed to an open drain, sewer, or
other collection system, it would be a reportable Tier 2 PSE based on the threshold quantity and
material below its flash point.
Standard No.: HSE-GA-ST04 Version No.: 1 Effective date: Dec., 2019 Page 132 of 184

Appendix 6: Examples of Establishing Work-Relatedness & Reportable Incidents
9.7 Third Parties Incidents
Group Companies shall maintain processes to ensure that incidents experienced by Third Parties are
investigated to an appropriate level to determine causation and to develop suitable actions to prevent
recurrence. Refer to Section 11 for more details.
The following are examples of third-party fatalities for illustrative purposes:
Example 1: Customers at Company-owned-Company-operated retail stores.
Example 2: Customers, dealers or dealers' employees at Company-owned-dealer-operated retail
stores.
Example 3: Customers, agents or agent's employees at Company-owned-agency-operated (COAG)
retail stores.
Example 4: Spot charter or common carrier drivers if killed in an incident while transporting Company
material while not on Company premises. (If the fatality occurs on Company premises, then the incident
is reportable as a Contractor fatality.)
Example 5: Third parties (e.g., pedestrians, cyclists, passengers, occupants of other vehicles, etc.)
killed as a result of an incident with:
- A Company owned and operated motor vehicle.
- A full-time or dedicated charter transport while the transport is chartered by the Company.
- A Company operated or bareboat chartered marine vessel.
- A common carrier or spot charter truck while transporting Company product, or while on
Company premises.
- A personal vehicle while in use on Company business.

Appendix 7: Examples of Incidents involving Multiple Group Companies
APPENDIX 7: EXAMPLES OF INCIDENTS INVOLVING MULTIPLE GROUP COMPANIES
The below examples explain the incident ownership and Recordability in HSE performance requirements for
incidents involving multiple Group Companies.
 Incident Ownership: The Group Company who is the site owner accountable for managing a specific
work location, equipment, activity / operation or person(s) involved in an incident. The site owner where
the incident occurred is required to conduct the incident management, notification, investigation &
reporting.
 HSE Performance Reporting: Incidents (during execution of services) shall be reported in HSE
performance of Group Companies receiving the services (Client GC) at their premises and NOT to GC
providing Services (Contractor GC) as it will be regarded as a Contractor under this arrangement and
hence, the Client GC will be maintaining the man-hours and recording the incident under its HSE
performance / KPIs. Once the work is suspended by the Client GC, the HSE Performance shall be
recorded with the Contractor GC
 Rationale: The reasoning/ justification to support the assessment of the incident.
Example 1
While inspecting drilling pipes in the storage area on ADNOC Offshore artificial island as part of ADNOC Drilling
preventive maintenance, a pipe inspector, who was working as an ADNOC Drilling subcontractor, slipped and
hit his head on the pipe, resulting in head laceration.
Ownership: ADNOC Offshore
HSE Performance: ADNOC Drilling
Rationale: Although the Injured Person (IP) was a Reportable Contractor working in ADNOC Offshore area,
the work he carried out was specifically for ADNOC Drilling and NOT directly in connection with ADNOC
Offshore well drilling operations program. Additionally the man hours for the drilling personnel are maintained
by ADNOC Drilling.
Example 2
Ruwais Site Integration (RSI) employee injured while providing Services to Group Company (other than
ADNOC Refining)
Ownership: Client GC
HSE Performance: Client GC
Rational: RSI will be regarded as a Contractor under this arrangement and hence the Man-hours of service
staff (execution phase) is credited towards the Client GCs. HSE Performance during execution of services for
Client GC will be allocated to Client GC receiving the service at their premises and NOT RSI.
Example 3
Ruwais Site Integration (RSI) employee injured while providing Services for specific ADNOC Refining Site
Ownership: Specific ADNOC Refining Site
HSE Performance: Specific ADNOC Refining Site
Rational: It has been agreed within ADNOC Refining & Downstream that RSI will be regarded as an
independent entity from ADNOC Refining, hence any services provided by RSI to specific ADNOC Refining
Site shall be considered as Contractor services. The specific ADNOC Refining Site shall be responsible for the
quality of service provided by RSI along with the HSE performance i.e. man-hours and incident recording.
Example 4
After completing drilling activities, an ADNOC Drilling Rig was standing-by for rig move, waiting-on-weather i.e.
work was suspended. While carrying out housekeeping activity, a floor man sustained a finger injury.
Ownership: ADNOC Drilling
HSE Performance: ADNOC Drilling
Rationale: The work-relationship with ADNOC Offshore had ended. The Injured Person (IP) was carrying out
an activity solely for ADNOC Drilling and not directly affecting ADNOC Offshore.
Standard No.: HSE-GA-ST04 Version No.: 1 Effective date: Dec., 2019 Page 134 of 184

Appendix 7: Examples of Incidents involving Multiple Group Companies
Example 5
A driller was carrying out major maintenance work on an ADNOC drilling rig while work was suspended by
ADNOC Offshore. His hand got caught in between the gears resulting in hand injury.
Ownership: ADNOC Drilling
HSE Performance: ADNOC Drilling
Rationale: The Injured Person (IP) was carrying out an activity solely for ADNOC Drilling and not directly
affecting ADNOC Offshore well operations
Example 6
While assisting in loading of equipment in ADNOC Offshore yard in Mussafah, an ADNOC Logistics & Services
contractor stepped on a protruding nail resulting in punctured wound.
Ownership: ADNOC Offshore
Recordability: ADNOC Offshore
Rationale: The Injured Person (IP) is a Reportable Contractor carrying out an activity for ADNOC Offshore in
an ADNOC Offshore controlled area.
Example 7
While an ADNOC Logistics and Services contractor supply vessel was steaming towards an ADNOC Offshore
site, a mechanic sustained head injury while servicing the boat’s engine. The boat was outside the 500 meters
exclusion zone at that time.
Ownership: ADNOC Logistics and Services
HSE Performance: ADNOC Logistics and Services
Rationale: The Injured Person (IP) is carrying out an activity for ADNOC Logistics and Services outside of
ADNOC Offshore controlled area.
Example 8
Similar to example 6, but the vessel was inside the 500 exclusion zone of an ADNOC Offshore pipeline.
Ownership: ADNOC Offshore
HSE Performance: ADNOC Offshore
Rationale: The Injured Person (IP) is a Recordable Contractor carrying out an activity for ADNOC Offshore
within ADNOC Offshore controlled area.
Example 9
During rig move, an ADNOC Offshore contractor rig mover slipped and fell from a ladder on board an ADNOC
Logistics and Services contractor tug boat located 300 meters within ADNOC Offshore area.
Ownership: ADNOC Offshore
HSE Performance: ADNOC Offshore
Rationale: The Injured Person (IP) is a Reportable Contractor carrying out an activity for ADNOC Offshore
within ADNOC Offshore controlled area.
Example 10
While uploading supplies from an ADNOC Logistics and Services contractor supply vessel towards an ADNOC
Offshore Super Complex using the complex’s pedestal crane, the crane wire snapped and injured an ADNOC
Logistics and Services contractor who was assisting the loading.
Ownership: ADNOC Offshore
HSE Performance: ADNOC Offshore
Rationale: The Injured Person (IP) is a Reportable Contractor carrying out an activity for ADNOC Offshore
within ADNOC Offshore controlled area.

Appendix 8: Managing the Conflicting Medical Recommendations
APPENDIX 8: CONFLICTING CONTEMPORANEOUS MEDICAL RECOMMENDATIONS (CCMR)
REVIEW PROCESS - EXAMPLES
While the specific aspects of each case differ and will influence the outcome, the following are offered as
examples of the logic followed in determinations of conflicting medical recommendations.
Example 1: On Thursday, a worker reports shoulder pain to the supervisor after performing a non-routine task.
The worker is examined by the physician (LHCP) at the site health clinic, who applies a cold pack and advises
use of non-prescription strength Ibuprofen if needed. The worker is released to return to work with a
recommendation not to perform the non-routine task for one week. The Worker leaves the site clinic and
telephones a personal doctor who prescribes a prescription pain reliever and recommends 3 days off work.
The worker informs the supervisor and goes home based on the recommendation of the personal doctor. The
worker returns to work the following Monday.
CCMR Reviewers’ Recommendation: First Aid. The conflicting treatment recommendations:
a) Were concurrent (i.e., at approximately the same time with no change in condition) and
b) Were given before the Worker received Lost Time or Restricted Work
c) But the Worker received Medical Treatment (issuance of prescription for prescription medicine)
As this case (a) only involves Medical Treatment as of the time the recommendations were given, the CMR
exception can be considered and, if “most authoritative” the on-site LHCP recommendation can be used for
recordkeeping. Given the direct physical examination involved, the site LHCP recommendation is considered
more authoritative.
The site LHCP’s “most authoritative” treatment recommendation would cause the case to be classified as
Restricted Work or First Aid (versus Lost Workdays). Therefore, the case shall be recorded as First Aid, since
the task being restricted was not a routine duty for the Worker, no Restricted Work occurred.
Example 2: Same as Example 1, but rather than returning to work on Monday, the Worker sees a personal
doctor who recommends an additional two months off work and refers the Worker to an orthopedic surgeon.
Subsequently, the surgeon diagnosed a torn rotator cuff and performed corrective surgery.
CCMR Reviewers’ Recommendation: Lost Time Incident. In this case there were three conflicting
concurrent treatment recommendations opinions, as the condition had not progressed over the weekend. The
orthopedic surgeon’s opinion was assessed to be the “most authoritative” based on a comparison of medical
training and expertise.
Example 3: A Worker sustains a work-related hand injury that was initially managed with First Aid. Months
later, because of persistent symptoms, the Worker sees a surgeon that performs an operation on a Friday and
recommends two weeks off work. On the following Tuesday, the Worker’s supervisor calls site LHCP, describes
the Worker’s job duties, provides a copy of an e-mail the Worker sent over the weekend as an evidence that
the Worker “worked from home” and asked if the doctor thought the recommendation for time off was actually
necessary. The LHCP responded that, based on the information provided, the person apparently could have
performed work during the time off. The OH Specialist completed a CMR report, selected the site LHCP’s
opinion as the most authoritative, and recommended the case be classified as “Medical Treatment”.
CCMR Reviewers’ Recommendation: Lost Time Incident
1. Lost Time had already occurred.
2. There was no valid conflicting contemporaneous treatment recommendation. The site LHCP’s opinion:
 Was not “concurrent” (it occurred at a separate time),
 Was not “authoritative” (being based solely on second hand information) and
 Did not constitute a valid “treatment recommendation” for CCMR purposes (being an opinion about
another doctor’s recommendation).
Example 4: A Worker is involved in an incident while driving on Company business. The Worker is taken to
the local Emergency Room, examined, x-rayed, and released with advice to use cold packs and non-
prescription strength ibuprofen if needed for stiffness. The next morning, the Worker cannot get out of bed
Standard No.: HSE-GA-ST04 Version No.: 1 Effective date: Dec., 2019 Page 136 of 184

HSE Management System
HSE Governance & Administration Standards
Incident Notification, Investigation & Reporting
Appendix 8: Managing the Conflicting Medical Recommendations
because of excruciating back pain. The Worker’s spouse takes the Worker back to the Emergency Room
where a different doctor prescribes pain medication and muscle relaxants and recommends time off work and
physical therapy.
CCMR Reviewers’ Recommendation: Lost Time Incident.
The two treatment recommendations were conflicting, but were not concurrent. The Worker’s condition had
clearly changed between the initial assessment and the second. Since the recommendations were not
concurrent a judgment about which opinion is most authoritative becomes irrelevant.
Example 5: A Worker incurs a laceration that is initially self-treated with First Aid at the work site. That evening,
the laceration continues to bleed, so the Worker goes to a personal physician, who sutures the laceration. The
Worker is examined by the site LHCP the next morning and returned to work. Two days later, the site's Safety
Manager calls the site LHCP to ask if the sutures were necessary. The LHCP states that the sutures were not
necessary.
CCMR Reviewers’ Recommendation: Medical Treatment.
The Medical Treatment (sutures) was already rendered. The opinion of a physician after medical treatment is
rendered is not contemporaneous and no longer relevant for recordkeeping purposes, even if the treating
physician states that the medical treatment was "precautionary" or “unnecessary”

BRIEF DESCRIPTION:

INVESTIGATION TEAM
| Team         | NAME  |     | JOB TITLE  |     |     | COMPANY  | EMAIL CONTACT  |
| ------------ | ----- | --- | ---------- | --- | --- | -------- | -------------- |
| Team Leader  |       |     |            |     |     |          |                |
| Secretary    |       |     |            |     |     |          |                |
|              |       |     |            |     |     |          |                |
| Members      |       |     |            |     |     |          |                |
|              |       |     |            |     |     |          |                |

THE INVESTIGATION TEAM WILL INVESTIGATE THE INCIDENT WITH FOLLOWING OBJECTIVES:
1.  INVESTIGATE THE CIRCUMSTANCES LEADING UP TO THE INCIDENT AND ESTABLISH SEQUENCE OF EVENTS.
2.  INVESTIGATE THE RESPONSE TO THE INCIDENT, INCLUDING EMERGENCY ACTIONS TAKEN.
3.  ANALYZE THE INFORMATION GATHERED AND IDENTIFY THE IMMEDIATE/ROOT CAUSES INCLUDING HUMAN FACTORS, IOGP CASUAL
FACTORS & BARRIER FAILURES (AS APPLICABLE), VERIFY EFFECTIVENESS OF EXISTING CONTROLS AND IDENTIFY ANY MISSING
CONTROLS. FINAL INVESTIGATION REPORT FORM (FM-GAST04-07) FACILITATES IDENTIFYING IOGP REPORTING INFORMATION.
4.  MAKE RECOMMENDATIONS TO AVOID RECURRENCE OF SIMILAR INCIDENTS AND TO IMPROVE THE INCIDENT RESPONSE AS APPROPRIATE.
TIMELINE AND DELIVERABLES:
1.  A PRESENTATION TO BE DELIVERED BY THE GROUP COMPANY CEO / FUNCTION MANAGER OF RELEVANT DIRECTORATE TO ADNOC
ELT ON DD/MM/YYYY ALONG WITH THE PRELIMINARY INVESTIGATION REPORT.
2.  THE FINAL INCIDENT INVESTIGATION REPORT SHALL BE SUBMITTED TO INCIDENT OWNER AND COPY TO ADNOC GROUP HSE BY
DD/MM/YYYY

1.  TOR SHALL BE PREPARED BY INCIDENT OWNER WITHIN 48 HOURS FOR ALL REPORTABLE INCIDENTS (SEVERITY 3 OR ABOVE) AND HIPO (SEVERITY 4 OR ABOVE)
2.  COPY OF APPROVED TOR SHALL BE SENT TO ADNOC GROUP HSE: hsemanageroffice@adnoc.ae
3.  ALL THE GATHERED INFORMATION AND IDENTIFIED CAUSES SHALL BE KEPT CONFIDENTIAL.
4.  THE CONCERNED INCIDENT OWNER / GROUP COMPANY MUST ENSURE THAT THE INVESTIGATION TEAM INCLUDING TEAM LEADER, SECRETARY, MEMBERS ARE RELEASED
FROM THEIR NORMAL DUTY EXCLUSIVELY FOR INVESTIGATION TO ENSURE EFFECTIVENESS AND MEETING THE DEFINED TIMELINE.
5.  A MEMBER FROM ADNOC GROUP MEDICAL SERVICES DIVISION AND ADNOC GROUP COMPANY / AFFILIATE / FUNCTION HSE-OH SME SHALL BE INCLUDED IN THE INVESTIGATION
TEAM FOR NAD INVESTIGATION. DULY COMPLETED NAD INVESTIGATION CHECKLIST (FM-GAST04-14) SHALL BE ENCLOSED WITH FINAL INVESTIGATION REPORT.

ADNOC Incident Standard [HSE-GA-ST04] Page No. Ver. 1 Dec. 2019
FM-GAST04-12

1. Executive Summary
This section shall include:
 Brief description of the event
 Key Findings
 Key Causes and Contributors
 Key Recommendations
2. Introduction
A brief outline shall be provided about what happened, when and where the incident occurred, and who
initiated the investigation
2.1 Terms of Reference
The ‘Terms of Reference’ given to the investigation team should be stated here. This establishes the
parameters and indicates the scope of work of the investigation in question as issued to the investigation
team and for understanding by those subsequently reading the report.
2.2 Investigation Methodology
This section should describe the way in which the investigation was carried out. It will indicate the method
of investigation, time table involved, places or sites visited and the names of the personnel who assisted
with investigation by discussion, interview, etc.
 Identification of witnesses
 Documentation/ Procedures in operation at the time of incident (e.g. PTW/ certificates)
 Phase of operations, process condition, etc.
 Markings left by equipment involved
 Position of personnel and equipment
 Documentation of emergency response procedures immediately following the incident
3. Background
Various background information relevant to a proper understanding of the circumstances of the incident
should be stated in this section. This could include descriptions of equipment, organizations and management
systems (policies/ standards/ procedures).
4. Sequence of Events
4.1 Status of Operation at Time of Incident
A brief description of the operation as a whole illustrating the background of activity just before the incident.
If relevant, the weather conditions should be stated.
4.2 Sequence of Events Leading Up to and Following the Incident
This should include the names of those involved and the time of significant events, if the incident was
considered to be time critical.
Date Time Event
ADNOC Incident Standard [HSE-GA-ST04] Page No. Ver. 1 Dec. 2019
FM-GAST04-12

5. Description Emergency Response
Incident Response Summary (Include any gaps and deviations from established procedures/practices)
6. Findings & Causes
Investigation findings and associated causes (immediate and root) shall be reviewed and discussed at length
to their importance. As a rule, the main three to five findings should be presented here. ADNOC Life Safety
Rules violations (LSR) if applicable shall also be highlighted here. List in Table 1 below the investigation
findings along with description of associated immediate and root cause(s):
Table 1: Investigation Findings and Causes
No. Findings Associated Causes
1.
2.
3.
Table 2: ADNOC Life Safety Rules Violation
No. Life Safety Rule
1.
2.
3.
7. Recommendations
Recommendations to avoid incident recurrence shall be presented here. Recommendations shall be risk
classified in accordance with the ADNOC Risk Matrix and ADNOC HSE Audit & Assurance Standard (HSE-
GA-ST09). This section shall also identify responsible parties and target dates for completing actions on
findings and recommendations.
Table 2: Findings and Recommendations
Target
Priority / Responsible Party
No. Findings Recommendations Completion
Risk Rating to Implement
Date
1.
2.
3.
ADNOC Incident Standard [HSE-GA-ST04] Page No. Ver. 1 Dec. 2019
FM-GAST04-12

8. Root Cause Analyses
ADNOC Incident Standard [HSE-GA-ST04] Page No. Ver. 1 Dec. 2019
FM-GAST04-12

9. References
All documents that the investigation team has reviewed / used are attached as follows:
Annexure 1: Investigation Terms of Reference
Annexure 2: Incident Photos
Annexure 3: Root Cause Analysis
Annexure 4: Witness Statements
Annexure 5: Relevant documents such as Permit, Lab Analysis, P&IDs, etc.
Copies of Incident Notification & Reporting Forms
a. Incident Notification (FM-GAST04-01)
b. Preliminary Investigation Report (FM-GAST04-03)
c. Occupational Injury & Illness Classification Report (for Occup. Injury/ Illnesses) (FM-GAST04-06)
d. Final Investigation Report (FM-GAST04-07)
e. Occupational Illness Investigation Report (for Occupational Illnesses) (FM-GAST04-08)
f. Non Accidental Death (NAD) Investigation Checklist (for NAD Occurrences) (FM-GAST04-14)
Note: Relevant documents should include procedures, copies of Work Permits or other documents of importance,
maintenance records, risk register, training records, emergency response, etc. If equipment was damaged, the
details may be attached in the relevant document section.

professional?
